The period between 1920 and 1988 was one of profound transformation for Tazewell County, as the region moved from an economy dominated by coal to one seeking diversification through light manufacturing, services, and agriculture. The decline of the coal industry was not a sudden event but a gradual process, unfolding over more than half a century as the region grappled with changing markets, technological transformation, and the exhaustion of its most accessible reserves. The story of this decline is also the story of resilience, as communities that had been built around a single industry struggled to adapt to a world that no longer needed their primary product in the same quantities.

The coal camp system that had defined life in Tazewell County for four decades began to fade in the 1920s, as the most accessible seams were exhausted and as the economic pressures of the Great Depression exposed the vulnerability of the region's economy. The New Deal legislation of the 1930s addressed some of the worst abuses of the company store system, but the company towns and stores did not end entirely until the second half of the twentieth century with the decline of coal and U.S. manufacturing. The mechanization of the mines in the post-war era reduced the need for labor, contributing to the out-migration that would depopulate the region for decades to come.

The creation of the Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority in 1988 marked a new beginning for the region, a recognition that the old economy could not be revived and that a new approach was necessary for survival. The Authority's mission to enhance and diversify the economy of the seven-county, one-city coalfield region has been realized in the decades since its creation, as the region has attracted new industries and created thousands of jobs. The process of diversification is ongoing, as the region continues to seek new economic opportunities while preserving the cultural heritage that makes it unique.

The legacy of the "Sixteen Tons" era is one of resilience and resistance, of a population that endured hardship and oppression with determination and courage. The union struggles of the early twentieth century, the mine wars that erupted from the tensions of the era, and the political mobilization that followed were expressions of a determination to achieve justice and dignity. The story of Tazewell County between 1920 and 1988 is a story of transformation, of a region that was remade by the forces of industrial capitalism and that continues to bear the scars of that transformation to the present day.

The decline of the coal industry and the search for diversification have shaped the identity of Tazewell County in profound ways. The skills, values, and relationships that miners developed during the coal era—the hard work, the community solidarity, the determination to overcome adversity—have become crucial resources for navigating the challenges of economic transformation. The cultural heritage of the region, preserved in the oral histories, the music, and the traditions of the community, provides a foundation for building a new economic future. The creation of VCEDA was a recognition that the region could not rely on the past to secure its future, and that a new approach was necessary for the region's economic survival.

The future of Tazewell County lies in its ability to build on its agricultural heritage, its manufacturing capacity, and the resilience of its people. The PSH project on East River Mountain, the sustainable timber plantations on reclaimed mine lands, and the renewable energy generation facilities that have been proposed or developed represent a new direction for the region, one that honors its history while embracing the opportunities of the future. The story of Tazewell County between 1920 and 1988 is a story of loss and adaptation, of communities that refused to disappear even as the economic foundations that had created them eroded away. It is a story that illuminates the challenges facing any region dependent on a single extractive industry and the difficult process of building a new economic identity.

The people of Tazewell County, like the miners who extracted the coal that powered American industry, have demonstrated an extraordinary capacity for resilience and adaptation. The creation of VCEDA was a testament to the determination of the region's leaders to build a new economic future for their communities. The Authority's work continues today, supporting economic diversification and creating opportunities for the region's residents. The story of Tazewell County is not just a story of decline, but a story of renewal, of a region that has faced extraordinary challenges and has responded with creativity, determination, and hope.

---

### Part II: The Modern Economic Landscape (1988-Present)

# Chapter Four: The Transportation Backbone: US 19 and I-81

## Introduction: The Arteries of Commerce

The story of Tazewell County's economic development is inextricably linked to the story of its transportation infrastructure. For generations, the region's prosperity has depended on its ability to move goods, people, and resources efficiently through the mountain passes and along the river valleys that define its geography. Before the railroad, the rough roads and trails that connected the county to the wider world limited economic opportunity to what could be carried by horse-drawn wagon or driven on the hoof. The arrival of the Norfolk & Western Railway in the 1880s transformed the region, opening the coalfields to industrial development and creating the boom towns that defined the county for generations.

Today, the transportation landscape of Tazewell County and the broader US 19 corridor reflects a remarkable continuity with the past, even as it embraces new technologies and new economic opportunities. The same mountain passes that challenged the first settlers still shape the route of modern highways, and the same rail lines that carried the first coal shipments continue to serve as vital arteries of commerce. But the transportation system of the region is also evolving, adapting to the changing needs of an economy that has shifted from coal extraction to manufacturing, agriculture, renewable energy, and logistics. The roads and rails that once served the coal industry are now being repurposed to support a more diversified economic future.

The story of transportation in the US 19 corridor is a story of investment and innovation, of communities that have recognized the critical importance of infrastructure to their economic well-being. From the designation of US 19 as a Corridor of Statewide Significance to the groundbreaking of major projects like the Beckley Z-Way, the region has demonstrated a commitment to maintaining and improving its transportation network. This chapter explores the current state of that network, the challenges it faces, and the opportunities it presents for the economic revitalization of the region.

## US 19 as a Corridor of Statewide Significance

U.S. Route 19 holds a position of remarkable importance within Virginia's transportation network. The highway is listed as a Corridor of Statewide Significance (CoSS), a designation that reflects its critical role in maintaining the economic vitality of southwestern Virginia. The CoSS designation is not merely ceremonial; it represents a formal recognition that US 19 serves as a vital link in the state's multi-modal transportation system, connecting rural communities to urban centers and facilitating the movement of goods essential to the regional economy.

The purpose of the Corridor of Statewide Significance designation is to provide a multi-modal vision for the corridors that helps guide localities in their land use and transportation planning decisions. This designation ensures that US 19 receives the attention and investment necessary to maintain its functionality as a major transportation artery. The corridor is expected to continue to result in the safe and efficient movement of people and goods, a goal that requires ongoing investment in infrastructure, safety improvements, and capacity enhancements.

The CoSS designation also reflects the broader economic significance of US 19 as a major commercial corridor that supports multiple counties and provides essential connectivity to the Interstate 81 system. The highway serves as the primary means of maintaining freight mobility in and out of western Virginia, a function that has become increasingly important as the region seeks to diversify its economy beyond coal extraction. Without the connectivity provided by US 19, the economic development efforts that have defined the region's recent history would be impossible.

The US 19 corridor extends far beyond the boundaries of Tazewell County, connecting communities across multiple states. The highway runs from Memphis, Florida, to Erie, Pennsylvania, traversing the Appalachian Mountains and linking the Southeastern United States to the industrial centers of the Northeast. In Virginia, the highway runs for 88.89 miles, connecting the twin cities of Bristol on the Tennessee border to the twin cities of Bluefield on the West Virginia border. Along the way, it passes through the counties of Washington, Russell, and Tazewell, serving as a vital economic lifeline for communities that might otherwise be isolated by the mountain geography.

The history of US 19 in Virginia reflects a century of investment and improvement. Initially, the route followed existing paths, including U.S. Route 11 (the Lee Highway) from Bristol northward to Abingdon, then Virginia State Route 106 to Hansonville and other local roads toward the West Virginia line. By 1929, the entire Virginia portion was paved except for the segment from Hansonville to Lebanon, marking early federal involvement that built on the road's prior status as Virginia's first recipient of federal aid under the 1916 Federal Aid Road Act. The 1930s brought significant realignments to improve directness and safety, including the rerouting of the highway in Bristol and the construction of new alignments north of Abingdon.

The mid-twentieth century saw further improvements, including bypass constructions to address congestion and obsolete alignments. The Tazewell County bypass opened in May 1970, with bridges completed by 1974, leaving behind a business route and converting sections to freeway standards. Major improvements in the latter half of the century focused on widening and freeway conversions to accommodate growing traffic from the Appalachian coal industry. Four-laning occurred in Tazewell County during the 1980s and 1990s, including the 1985 Lebanon Bypass, a 1995-1996 multilaning from the Russell-Tazewell line to Claypool Hill, and a 1997-1998 widening of the Lebanon Bypass.

Today, the highway continues to be a focus of investment and improvement, with VDOT studies addressing safety and congestion concerns at critical intersections. The US 19 / ALT US 58 intersection in Russell County has been identified as a location with a history of crashes, with 66 crashes occurring over nine years, 24 resulting in injuries. A significant portion of these crashes occurred at night, raising concerns about visibility that the VDOT study is designed to address. The study, part of the state's Project Pipeline Program, is developing performance-based solutions that can be effectively implemented and compete for various funding sources.

## The Southside Corridor: Connecting to I-81

The designation of US 19 as a Corridor of Statewide Significance is closely related to its role as part of Virginia's "Southside Corridor," a transportation network that provides access to Interstate 81 for Russell, Wise, Dickenson, and Buchanan Counties. This function is essential to the economic vitality of southwestern Virginia, as I-81 serves as the region's primary connection to the broader national transportation system. Without the connectivity provided by the Southside Corridor, the economic development of these counties would be severely constrained.

The Southside Corridor designation reflects the recognition that US 19 serves as more than a local road; it is a vital link in the state's transportation network, connecting rural communities to the interstate system and facilitating the movement of goods essential to the regional economy. The corridor is heavily traveled, with approximately 17,000 vehicles per day using the highway, and approximately 5% of this traffic being trucks. This truck traffic is essential to the region's economy, as it enables the movement of manufactured goods, agricultural products, and other commodities to markets throughout the state and beyond.

The importance of the Southside Corridor has only increased in recent decades, as the region has diversified its economy beyond coal extraction. The manufacturing facilities that have located in the region, including Lawrence Brothers in Bluefield, Simmons Equipment in Tazewell and Russell Counties, and the food processing operations that are being developed, depend on reliable transportation connections to move their products to market. The agricultural sector of the region, including cattle, dairy, and timber production, similarly depends on the truck traffic that moves along US 19.

The Southside Corridor designation also has implications for transportation planning and investment. The Virginia Department of Transportation (VDOT) conducts corridor studies to address safety and congestion concerns, ensuring that the highway continues to serve its vital function. The studies evaluate the portion of U.S. Route 19 between State Route 633 (Black Hollow Road) and the western Town limits of Abingdon, VA, a distance of approximately 2.5 miles. The subject segment has not been designated a limited access facility, nor is it expected to be based on anticipated development patterns and expected local growth. This recognition of the corridor's character and function is essential for effective transportation planning.

The US 19 / ALT US 58 intersection study in Russell County is a prime example of the kind of planning that the Southside Corridor designation facilitates. The study includes current traffic data collection, including intersection turning counts and hourly volume data, to better understand traffic flow. The study also addresses safety issues, including the history of crashes at the intersection, and considers the impact of recent developments, such as the casino, on traffic volumes. This detailed analysis is essential for developing effective solutions to the corridor's challenges.

## Traffic Volumes and Safety Concerns

The approximately 17,000 vehicles per day that use US 19, with 5% being trucks, create both economic opportunity and transportation challenges. The traffic volume is a testament to the highway's importance to the regional economy, but it also creates safety and congestion concerns that must be addressed through ongoing investment and planning. The presence of heavy truck traffic, in particular, requires careful attention to road design, maintenance, and safety measures.

The crash data for the US 19 / ALT US 58 intersection in Russell County illustrates the safety challenges that the corridor faces. Over a nine-year period, 66 crashes occurred at the intersection, with 24 resulting in injuries. The PSI metric, a tool used to rank locations for safety improvements, has been applied to the intersection to guide investment decisions. The crash analysis is an essential component of the VDOT study, enabling the development of targeted solutions that address the specific causes of accidents at the location.

The reasons for the crash history at the intersection are multifaceted. A significant portion of the crashes occurred at night, raising concerns about visibility. The intersection also presents challenges for left turns, which have been identified as a particular safety concern. The impact of recent developments, such as the casino, on traffic volumes adds additional complexity to the safety analysis, as increased traffic volume can lead to increased crash risk. These factors must all be considered in the development of effective safety solutions.

The VDOT study process is designed to address these safety concerns through a thorough analysis of the intersection and its traffic patterns. The Project Pipeline process includes data collection and problem identification, detailed analysis with public feedback, and refinement of solutions for funding applications. The goal is to develop performance-based solutions that can be effectively implemented and that will compete for funding in the state's SMART SCALE process. This systematic approach ensures that investments are targeted where they will have the greatest impact on safety.

The safety concerns at the US 19 / ALT US 58 intersection are not unique within the corridor; they reflect broader challenges that the highway faces. The US 19 corridor includes numerous intersections that have experienced significant crash histories, as noted in the VDOT studies. Addressing these safety concerns is essential for the corridor to fulfill its function as a safe and efficient transportation route for both local traffic and through freight.

## The Beckley Z-Way: A Transformative Infrastructure Project

One of the most significant transportation infrastructure projects in the US 19 corridor is the Beckley Z-Way, a $110 million infrastructure investment designed to alleviate congestion in and around Beckley, West Virginia. The project, which has been in planning for over two decades, represents a transformative investment in the region's transportation network. The groundbreaking ceremony in September 2024 marked the beginning of construction, with officials from across the state celebrating the long-awaited project.

The Beckley Z-Way is a 5.5-mile stretch of new highway that will run from Shady Spring through Beaver and into Beckley. The project is being completed in two sections, with Mountaineer Contractors awarded the Shady Spring to Beaver section and Kanawha Stone Company awarded the Beaver to South Eisenhower section. The project is funded through the governor's Roads to Prosperity program, money for which was approved by voters in a 2017 referendum that passed by approximately 73 percent. The project is expected to be completed by the spring of 2028.

The need for the Beckley Z-Way reflects the growth and development of the Beckley area. With more than 17,000 residents, Beckley is the ninth most densely populated city in West Virginia, and more than 16,000 traveling vehicles pass through the area daily. The city's attractions, including shopping, locally made arts and crafts, miles of hiking, and underground mining tours, draw visitors from across the region. The New River Gorge National Park and Preserve, located nearby, adds to the tourism traffic that contributes to congestion on the highway.

The Z-Way project is designed to address this congestion through a comprehensive set of improvements. Section one involves widening a three-lane roadway and converting a two-lane road into a three-lane road by adding a center turn lane within US-19 and WV-3, stretching 3.6 miles. Section two consists of a total of 1.93 miles, involving relocating and adding a roadway as well as realigning a stream. The complexity of the work, particularly in section two, requires experienced contractors like Kanawha Stone Company, which has been blasting sections and relocating utilities since late 2024.

The economic impact of the Beckley Z-Way is expected to be significant. Raleigh County Commission President Greg Duckworth called the project "transformative for our community and beyond," stating that it will mean a "brighter, more prosperous future for all of us". The project will improve access to businesses, reduce travel times, and enhance the quality of life for residents of the region. The improved transportation infrastructure will also make the area more attractive to businesses considering location or expansion in the region.

The Z-Way project reflects a broader trend of infrastructure investment in the US 19 corridor. The project follows other major improvements, including the Coalfields Expressway, which has opened additional 2.74 miles of highway in the region, and the US 460 Corridor improvements that have enhanced connectivity to Tazewell County. These investments demonstrate the commitment of state and local officials to maintaining and improving the transportation infrastructure essential to the region's economic development.

## VDOT Corridor Studies: Addressing Safety and Congestion

The Virginia Department of Transportation (VDOT) conducts corridor studies along US 19 to address safety and congestion concerns, ensuring that the highway continues to serve its vital function as a Corridor of Statewide Significance. These studies are part of the state's broader transportation planning process, which includes the identification of critical transportation needs, the development of performance-based solutions, and the application for funding through programs like SMART SCALE.

The VDOT corridor studies are designed to be comprehensive, covering both safety and congestion issues. The US 19 / ALT US 58 intersection study in Russell County, for example, includes an overview of current traffic conditions, an analysis of crash data, and consideration of environmental considerations and the need for careful planning to accommodate future growth. The study also addresses traffic access points and the implications of proposed developments on intersection management. This comprehensive approach ensures that the solutions developed are effective and sustainable.

The study process includes public engagement, an essential component of effective transportation planning. The kickoff meeting for the Russell County study included participants from VDOT and the Planning District Commission, and future public engagement opportunities are planned. The release of a framework document will provide additional opportunities for public input, ensuring that the concerns of the community are addressed in the study's recommendations. The project aims for significant progress by March 2024, aligning with SMART SCALE application deadlines.

The VDOT studies address a range of safety and congestion concerns. The crash data analysis at the US 19 / ALT US 58 intersection, for example, identified 66 crashes over nine years, with 24 resulting in injuries. The study also noted a significant portion of crashes occurred at night, raising concerns about visibility. The PSI metric, a tool to rank locations for safety improvements, is used to guide investment decisions. The safety analysis informs the development of solutions designed to address the specific causes of crashes at the intersection.

The VDOT studies also consider the impact of recent developments on traffic volumes and congestion. The US 19 / ALT US 58 intersection study, for example, noted the impact of the casino on traffic volumes and considered the implications for intersection management. The study also addressed traffic access points and the implications of proposed developments on intersection management, ensuring that the solutions developed will be effective in accommodating future growth. This forward-looking approach is essential for ensuring that the corridor can continue to serve its vital transportation function.

## The Rail Legacy: CSX and Norfolk Southern Networks

The railroad networks that transformed Tazewell County in the 1880s continue to serve as vital assets for the region's transportation and logistics infrastructure. The Norfolk Southern Railway and CSX Transportation, the two major Class 1 railroads operating in the region, maintain extensive networks that connect the US 19 corridor to national and international markets. These rail assets, originally built to support the coal industry, are now being repurposed to support a more diversified economy.

The Norfolk Southern Railway has a particularly deep history in the region, having built the Clinch Valley Division that connected Tazewell County to the broader railroad network. The company's commitment to Virginia's rail infrastructure continues today, with Norfolk Southern investing in the network that supports businesses and strengthens the Commonwealth's economy. The company's rail lines connect the region to the Port of Virginia, a critical gateway for international trade that enables the export of manufactured goods and the import of raw materials.

The rail networks in the US 19 corridor are characterized by their connectivity and their capacity. The CSX Transportation network includes the Raleigh Southwestern and Winding Gulf Subdivision, a short railroad line that extends approximately 6 miles from a junction with the CSX Piney Creek Subdivision at Raleigh to Pemberton. The line, completed in 1912 and connected to the Virginian Railway's Winding Gulf Branch in 1917, was originally built to support coal mining in the Winding Gulf Coalfield. Today, it facilitates freight interchange with the adjacent Norfolk Southern Winding Gulf Branch at Pemberton, enabling the transport of coal and other commodities from Raleigh County mines to broader rail networks.

The capacity of the regional rail network is significant, though it is primarily focused on freight rather than high-volume intermodal traffic. The Raleigh Southwestern and Winding Gulf Subdivision, for example, consists of a single Class 1 track with a maximum freight speed of 10 mph (as of 2020) and is operated under Direct Traffic Control rules. The line's infrastructure limits include a maximum gross weight of 286,000 pounds per car and no double-stack clearances, reflecting its focus on regional freight rather than high-volume intermodal traffic. These limitations reflect the rail network's original purpose of serving the coal industry, and they highlight the potential for investment and upgrading to support a more diversified freight portfolio.

The rail legacy of the region is not merely historical; it has practical implications for the economic development of the US 19 corridor. The existing rail lines provide a ready-made logistics network that can support new industries, including the renewable energy sector, manufacturing, and agriculture. The rail lines can transport heavy equipment, construction materials, and commodities more efficiently than trucks, reducing transportation costs and environmental impacts. The rail infrastructure also provides access to national and international markets, enabling the region to compete in the global economy.

The rail networks also face challenges and controversies that affect their operation and potential for expansion. The Norfolk Southern Railway, for example, has been involved in a dispute with CSX Transportation over access to the Port of Virginia, with Norfolk Southern charging that CSX is using a taxpayer-funded subsidy to make trucking artificially cheap at the expense of local communities and the broader rail network. This dispute reflects the broader tensions between rail and truck transportation in the region, and it has implications for the future of rail freight in Virginia.

## The Future of Transportation in the US 19 Corridor

The transportation backbone of the US 19 corridor is evolving to meet the changing needs of the region's economy. The corridor studies being conducted by VDOT, the major infrastructure projects like the Beckley Z-Way, and the ongoing investment in the rail network all point to a future in which transportation will continue to play a critical role in the region's economic vitality. The challenge for the region is to ensure that its transportation infrastructure is adequate to support the diversified economy that is emerging.

The growth of the renewable energy sector, including the Pumped Storage Hydropower projects proposed for the region, will place new demands on the transportation network. The construction of these facilities will require the movement of heavy equipment, construction materials, and workers, all of which will need to be transported efficiently and safely. The existing US 19 corridor and the rail network provide the basic infrastructure for these movements, but investments in road and rail capacity may be necessary to ensure that the projects can be completed on schedule and within budget.

The expansion of manufacturing in the region will also place demands on the transportation network. The manufacturing facilities that have been developed, including Lawrence Brothers in Bluefield, Simmons Equipment in Tazewell and Russell Counties, and the food processing facilities being developed, depend on reliable transportation connections to move their products to market. The US 19 corridor and the rail network provide the connectivity that these industries require, and investments in these networks will support their continued growth.

The agricultural sector of the region, including cattle, dairy, timber, and the emerging food processing industry, also depends on the transportation network. The movement of livestock, dairy products, timber, and processed foods requires reliable road and rail connections, and the transportation network must be adequate to support this traffic. The US 19 corridor, with its high truck traffic volume, is essential to the agricultural sector, and investments in safety and capacity will benefit the sector.

The tourism sector of the region, which has grown significantly with the attractions of the "Back of the Dragon" and the New River Gorge National Park, also depends on the transportation network. The US 19 corridor provides access to these attractions, and the safety and congestion improvements being made through projects like the Beckley Z-Way will enhance the visitor experience and support the continued growth of tourism. The natural beauty of the region is a major asset for tourism, and the transportation infrastructure that enables visitors to access it is essential to the sector's success.

## Conclusion: The Foundation for Economic Revitalization

The transportation backbone of the US 19 corridor is the foundation for the region's economic revitalization. The US 19 highway, designated as a Corridor of Statewide Significance, provides essential connectivity to Interstate 81 and supports the movement of goods, people, and resources essential to the regional economy. The highway serves as the primary means of maintaining freight mobility in and out of western Virginia, a function that has become increasingly important as the region seeks to diversify its economy beyond coal extraction.

The traffic that moves along US 19, approximately 17,000 vehicles per day with 5% being trucks, is a testament to the highway's importance to the regional economy. The safety and congestion challenges that the highway faces are being addressed through VDOT corridor studies and major infrastructure projects like the Beckley Z-Way, a $110 million investment designed to alleviate congestion and improve traffic flow. These investments reflect a commitment to maintaining and improving the transportation infrastructure essential to the region's economic development.

The rail legacy of the region, embodied by the CSX and Norfolk Southern networks, provides a ready-made logistics infrastructure that can support new industries. The rail lines, originally built to serve the coal industry, can be repurposed to support manufacturing, renewable energy, agriculture, and other sectors of the emerging economy. The connectivity that the rail network provides to national and international markets is a major asset for the region.

The future of the US 19 corridor depends on continued investment in transportation infrastructure. The region's economic development efforts, including the growth of manufacturing, agriculture, renewable energy, and tourism, all depend on reliable transportation connections. The US 19 corridor and the rail network provide the basic infrastructure for these connections, but investments in capacity, safety, and sustainability will be necessary to support the region's continued growth.

The transportation backbone of the US 19 corridor is more than a network of roads and rails; it is a symbol of the region's resilience and determination. The communities of the region have demonstrated a commitment to investing in their transportation infrastructure, recognizing that it is essential to their economic well-being. The story of transportation in the US 19 corridor is a story of progress, of a region that has faced the challenges of economic transition and is building a new future for itself. The transportation backbone will continue to support that future, carrying the goods, people, and resources that the region needs to thrive.

**Chapter 6: Major Cities Along the Corridor and Their Economies**

# Chapter Five: The Cities of the Corridor

## Introduction: The Urban Fabric of the Region

The transportation corridors that bind Tazewell County to the broader Appalachian region are punctuated by a series of cities and towns, each with its own distinctive character, economic identity, and historical trajectory. These urban centers—ranging from the county seat of Tazewell to the bustling commercial hub of Beckley—represent the human dimension of the region's economic story. They are places where the abstract forces of industrial transformation, economic diversification, and community resilience take concrete form in the lives of residents, the activities of businesses, and the rhythms of daily life.

Understanding the cities of the US 19 corridor is essential for comprehending the region's economic geography. Each town occupies a specific niche in the broader economic system, shaped by its location, its historical legacy, and the strategic choices made by its leaders and residents. Some have successfully navigated the transition from coal dependency to economic diversification, while others continue to struggle with the legacy of industrial decline. Together, they form a complex urban system that reflects both the challenges and opportunities facing the Appalachian region in the twenty-first century.

This chapter examines the six major urban centers along the US 19 corridor: Tazewell, Bluefield, Richlands, Lebanon, Abingdon, and Beckley. For each city, we explore its historical roots, its current economic base, the challenges it faces, and the strategic initiatives underway to build a more prosperous future. The cities are presented in geographical order from southwest to northeast, tracing the route that has shaped the region's development for more than a century.

---

## Tazewell Town: The County Seat Reinvents Itself

**Historical Roots: From Jeffersonville to the County Seat**

The town of Tazewell, the county seat of Tazewell County, traces its origins to the earliest period of European settlement in the region. The town was originally called Jeffersonville, a name that reflected the political sentiments of its founders during the early years of the American republic. The name was changed to Tazewell in honor of Henry Tazewell, a United States Senator from Virginia who served from 1794 until his death in 1799, and whose family name would come to define the county and its principal town.

The town's location in the Clinch River valley, surrounded by the mountains that define the county's geography, made it a natural center for the agricultural economy that characterized the region before the arrival of the railroad. The town served as a market for the farmers of the surrounding valleys, a center for the legal and governmental services essential to the county's administration, and a gathering place for the community. The courthouse, built in the town center, became the symbolic heart of the county, representing the institutions of law and governance that bound the community together.

The arrival of the Norfolk & Western Railway in the 1880s transformed Tazewell from a quiet agricultural center into a bustling transportation hub. The Clinch Valley Division of the railroad connected the town to the broader rail network, and the Tazewell Depot, built in 1888 of frame construction, became one of the most important stations on the line. The depot, replaced in 1928 by a brick structure that remains standing today, symbolized the town's integration into the industrial economy that was reshaping the region.

**Modern Economy: Retail, Services, and Tourism**

Today, the economy of Tazewell is characterized by a mix of retail, services, and tourism, with the town serving as a commercial and administrative center for the county. The town's population was 4,419 in the 2023 estimates, with a median age of 43.8 years, significantly older than the state median of 38.8 years . This demographic profile reflects the broader aging of the region's population, a trend that has implications for the town's economic future.

The largest employment sectors in the town reflect its role as a service center for the surrounding county. Healthcare and social assistance, retail trade, and accommodation and food services are among the leading industries, providing employment for residents and serving the needs of the broader community. The town's retail establishments, located along the US 19 corridor, serve as a shopping destination for residents of the surrounding rural areas, while the service sector provides the professional and personal services essential to daily life.

The town's economic base is being strengthened by a growing tourism sector, driven largely by the "Back of the Dragon," a 32-mile stretch of winding road with 438 curves that has become a major attraction for motorcyclists and classic car enthusiasts . The Back of the Dragon draws nearly 400,000 riders annually, stimulating the local hospitality and retail sectors. The town has recognized the potential of this attraction and is investing in infrastructure to support the tourism industry, including the development of a permanent farmers market.

**The Back of the Dragon: A Motorcycle Mecca**

The Back of the Dragon is one of the most significant economic development initiatives in the Tazewell area, transforming a scenic mountain road into a tourism destination that draws visitors from across the country. The route, which follows US 16 from Tazewell to Marion, Virginia, features 438 curves over 32 miles of mountain terrain, offering motorcyclists the kind of challenging driving experience that enthusiasts seek. The road has been compared to the famous Tail of the Dragon in North Carolina, and it has become a key attraction for the region.

The economic impact of the Back of the Dragon extends well beyond the direct spending of tourists. The visitors who come to ride the route stay in local hotels, eat in local restaurants, and shop in local stores, providing a significant boost to the region's hospitality sector. The attraction has also stimulated investment in tourism-related businesses, including motorcycle rental operations, tour companies, and specialty retailers. The town of Tazewell has recognized the potential of this industry and has made efforts to capitalize on the traffic that the attraction generates.

The Back of the Dragon is also a symbol of the region's ability to reinvent itself, transforming a legacy of coal mining into a new economic opportunity based on the natural beauty of the landscape. The road that once carried coal trucks now carries tourists seeking the thrill of the curves and the beauty of the mountains. This transformation reflects the broader transition that the region is undergoing, as it moves from extraction to experience, from mining to tourism, from a past defined by industry to a future defined by recreation and hospitality.

**Proximity to Proposed PSH Projects**

One of the most significant factors shaping Tazewell's economic future is its proximity to the proposed Pumped Storage Hydropower (PSH) projects on East River Mountain. The proposed site, located just outside the town, would take advantage of the mountain's elevation—over a 1,000-foot drop—to create a large-scale energy storage facility that could provide grid-scale electricity storage for the region. The project, estimated to cost approximately $2 billion, would create thousands of jobs during construction and dozens of permanent positions during operation.

The proposed PSH project represents a new direction for Tazewell's economy, one that would leverage the region's topography and existing infrastructure to create a renewable energy asset of national significance. The project would be a flagship investment in the region's transition from coal dependency to energy diversification, providing a long-term economic engine and a new tax base for the county. The project's developers have already filed applications with the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC), and the project is supported by state legislation that recognizes pumped storage in the coalfield region as "in the public interest."

The proximity of the PSH project to Tazewell town would have significant economic benefits for the community. The construction phase would create demand for housing, retail, and services, while the operational phase would provide permanent, high-quality jobs for the region. The project would also enhance the town's identity as a center for energy innovation, building on its historical role as a gateway to the coalfields. The synergy between the Back of the Dragon's tourism appeal and the PSH project's economic development potential positions Tazewell as a key player in the region's future.

---

## Bluefield: The Twin Cities as Regional Hub

**Historical Roots: The Gateway to the Coalfields**

Bluefield, located on the Virginia-West Virginia state line, emerged as one of the most important cities in the Appalachian coalfields during the industrial boom of the late nineteenth century. The city's growth was driven by the Norfolk & Western Railway, which established Bluefield as its western terminal and a major division point for the railroad. The city quickly became a transportation hub, with rail lines extending into the coalfields of West Virginia and westward toward the Ohio River.

The city's location on the state line gave it a unique character, with Bluefield, West Virginia, and Bluefield, Virginia, growing together into a single metropolitan area divided by the boundary between the two states. The twin cities shared a common economic base in the coal industry, and their fortunes were closely tied to the boom-and-bust cycles of coal extraction. The city was known as the "Gateway to the Pocahontas Coalfields," a designation that reflected its strategic importance to the region's coal economy.

The Norfolk Southern rail yard in Bluefield remains a significant transportation asset, serving as a center for rail operations and logistics. The rail yard, which was originally constructed by the Norfolk & Western, continues to support the movement of freight through the region, including coal, manufactured goods, and agricultural products. The yard is a reminder of the city's historical role in the coal industry, and it remains a vital component of the region's transportation infrastructure.

**Modern Economy: Commerce, Healthcare, and Education**

Today, Bluefield's economy has diversified significantly from its historical dependence on coal. The city serves as a regional hub for commerce, healthcare, and education, providing services and employment for a broader region that extends well beyond its municipal boundaries. The unemployment rate for the Bluefield metropolitan area was 4.7% in December 2025, with a labor force of 37,915 and employment of 36,132 . This rate, while higher than the national average, reflects the region's gradual economic recovery.

The healthcare sector is a significant employer in Bluefield, with hospitals and medical facilities providing both direct employment and indirect economic activity. The city's healthcare institutions serve a broad region, providing services that would otherwise be unavailable to residents of the surrounding rural areas. The education sector, including public schools and higher education institutions, provides employment for hundreds of residents and contributes to the city's role as a regional center.

The city's retail and commercial sector serves as a shopping destination for residents of the surrounding region, with downtown Bluefield experiencing a revitalization in recent years . The Bluefield Economic Development Authority has worked to attract new businesses to the downtown area, including a coffee shop that is set to open in 2026 . These investments reflect a broader effort to make the city more attractive to residents and visitors, and to create a vibrant urban center that can compete with larger cities in the region.

**Lawrence Brothers, Inc.: A Manufacturing Anchor**

Lawrence Brothers, Inc. (LBI), based in Bluefield, Virginia, is one of the region's most significant manufacturing employers, specializing in heavy metal fabrication and custom metal applications . The company serves diverse industries including underground mining, energy, electrical, automotive, and material handling, and holds a 45% market share of mine battery trays and a 50% market share of material handling battery boxes. LBI has recently expanded with a new fiber optic laser, increasing its capacity by 75%.

The success of Lawrence Brothers reflects the region's ability to transition from coal extraction to advanced manufacturing, leveraging the skills and infrastructure developed during the industrial era to create new economic opportunities. The company's expansion is a testament to the workforce development efforts that have been a priority for the region's economic development organizations, and it demonstrates the potential for manufacturing to serve as a pillar of the region's diversified economy.

The company's continued investment in the region is supported by the Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority (VCEDA), which provides loans and grants to businesses seeking to expand or relocate to the region . VCEDA's support for LBI and other manufacturers reflects a strategic approach to economic development, one that recognizes the importance of building on existing industrial capabilities while diversifying into new sectors.

**Revitalization and Future Prospects**

Bluefield is currently in the midst of an "exciting revitalization period," according to U.S. Senator Shelley Moore Capito, who toured the city in February 2026 to view ongoing redevelopment efforts . The city's revitalization efforts, supported by Congressionally Directed Spending investments secured by Senator Capito, focus on key sites including the Mercer County Airport, The Emergency Services Campus, and Hotel Thelma . These investments reflect a bipartisan commitment to the city's economic future and recognition of its strategic importance to the region.

The revitalization of downtown Bluefield is a key priority for local leaders, with the Bluefield Economic Development Authority working to attract new businesses and create a vibrant urban center . The recent announcement of three new businesses opening in 2026, including a coffee shop, is a sign of the progress being made . The city's leadership has focused on "bringing more people downtown" as a primary goal, recognizing that a vibrant urban core is essential to attracting residents and businesses .

The future of Bluefield will depend on continued investment in its economic diversification, building on its strengths in manufacturing, healthcare, education, and commerce. The city's historical role as a gateway to the coalfields remains a part of its identity, but the modern Bluefield is increasingly defined by its ability to adapt to changing economic circumstances. The investments in downtown revitalization, manufacturing expansion, and workforce development are all part of a broader effort to create a sustainable economic future for the city and the surrounding region.

---

## Richlands: Healthcare as the Dominant Sector

**Historical Roots: A Coal Boom Town**

Richlands, located in the Clinch River valley of Tazewell County, emerged as a boom town during the coal era of the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries. The town's growth was driven by the development of coal mines in the surrounding area, and its economy was closely tied to the extraction of coal from the rich seams that lay beneath the mountains. The town's location on the Clinch Valley Division of the Norfolk & Western Railway provided a connection to the broader rail network, enabling the shipment of coal to markets throughout the East Coast.

The town's history reflects the broader story of the region, with the boom years of the early twentieth century followed by decline as the coal industry contracted. The town's population and economic base were closely tied to the fortunes of the coal companies that operated in the area, and the decline of coal employment in the post-war decades had a significant impact on the community. The town's search for economic diversification has been a central theme of its recent history, as it has sought to build a new economic identity on a foundation different from coal.

**Modern Economy: Healthcare as the Largest Employer**

The economy of Richlands today is dominated by the healthcare sector, with Clinch Valley Health serving as the largest employer in the town . The hospital, which is part of a broader healthcare system, provides employment for hundreds of residents and serves as a vital medical center for the region. The healthcare sector is not only the largest employer but also one of the highest-paying industries in the town, providing good jobs for skilled workers.

The healthcare sector's dominance is reflected in the town's employment statistics. In 2024, the largest industries in Richlands were Health Care & Social Assistance (272 people), Retail Trade (244 people), and Accommodation & Food Services (131 people) . The healthcare sector's employment is significantly larger than any other industry, reflecting the town's role as a regional medical center. The highest paying industries are Mining, Quarrying, & Oil & Gas Extraction ($120,357) and Agriculture, Forestry, Fishing & Hunting, & Mining ($120,357), reflecting the continuing presence of mining in the region's economy .

The town's median household income was $37,154 in 2024, with a median property value of $111,000 . The homeownership rate is 63.8%, slightly lower than the national average, and the average commute time is 27.9 minutes . These statistics reflect a community that is working-class and relatively modest, with many residents commuting to jobs outside the town.

**Recent Investments and Infrastructure Improvements**

Richlands has been the focus of significant investment in recent years, driven by the town's strategic location along the US 19 corridor and the support of state and federal programs. The town has installed a new Level 2 dual-port EV charging station in its downtown district at Greenway Park on Front Street, funded through the Mid-Atlantic Electrification Partnership (MAEP), a federal grant program supported by the U.S. Department of Energy . The station is the first of its kind in the region, and it is expected to attract visitors and support the town's economic development efforts .

The Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority (VCEDA) has also invested in Richlands, approving $1 million in loans and grants for natural gas utility infrastructure and improvements in the Richlands area of Tazewell County . The investment, which includes $500,000 in loan funds and $500,000 in grant funds, will be used to finance the purchase and installation of natural gas decompression equipment and infrastructure. The infrastructure improvements are designed to support economic development in the area, attracting businesses that require natural gas for their operations.

The town is also addressing housing challenges, with over 110 abandoned mobile homes in Richlands, most from the 1960s and 1970s and containing asbestos and other hazardous materials . The town and county have proposed a $2-3 million investment to address the housing situation, with the infrastructure already in place for housing redevelopment . The initiative, supported by state and local leaders, would replace abandoned mobile homes with modern housing that would increase property tax revenue and build generational wealth for families .

**Future Prospects**

The future of Richlands will depend on continued investment in its healthcare sector, its infrastructure, and its housing stock. The town's role as a regional medical center provides a stable economic base, while the investments in infrastructure and housing are designed to attract new businesses and residents. The town's location on the US 19 corridor provides access to the broader region, and the improvements being made to the corridor will enhance Richlands's connectivity to markets in Virginia and beyond.

The town's leadership has recognized the importance of diversifying the economy beyond healthcare and retail, with discussions about developing a technology zone and a tourism zone that would provide certain incentives for businesses . These initiatives reflect a broader strategy of economic development that builds on the town's strengths while seeking new opportunities. The town's commitment to electric vehicle infrastructure, natural gas improvements, and housing redevelopment are all part of a comprehensive effort to create a sustainable economic future.

---

## Lebanon: Government Services and Regional Retail

**Historical Roots: The Russell County Seat**

Lebanon, the county seat of Russell County, has served as an administrative and commercial center for the region since its founding. The town's location at the intersection of US 19 and US 460 has made it a natural hub for the surrounding area, providing access to the broader region. The town's history is closely tied to the agricultural economy of Russell County, with the town serving as a market for the farmers of the surrounding valleys and a center for the legal and governmental services essential to county administration.

The town's role as the county seat has provided a stable economic base, with government services providing employment and economic activity that is less subject to the boom-and-bust cycles that have characterized the coal industry. The courthouse, located in the town center, remains the symbolic heart of the county, representing the institutions of law and governance that bind the community together. The town's retail sector serves the county's residents, providing goods and services that would otherwise require travel to larger cities.

**Modern Economy: Government, Retail, and Manufacturing**

The economy of Lebanon today is characterized by a mix of government services, retail trade, and manufacturing. In 2024, the most common employment sectors were Health Care & Social Assistance (235 people), Retail Trade (137 people), and Accommodation & Food Services (123 people) . The town's median household income was $53,050 in 2024, with a median property value of $162,400 . These statistics reflect a community that is relatively prosperous by regional standards, with the stability of government employment providing a solid economic foundation.

The town's manufacturing sector is supported by the presence of the former Alcoa building, which has been repurposed for manufacturing operations . The building, located in Lebanon, has been the site of expansion by Tate, a manufacturer of data center components. The expansion, supported by a $4 million VCEDA loan, is expected to create at least 35 new jobs in the town . The investment reflects the broader strategy of repurposing existing industrial facilities for new uses, building on the region's manufacturing heritage.

Lebanon serves as a regional retail center for Russell County and surrounding areas, with the town's retail establishments providing goods and services for a population that extends well beyond the town's boundaries. The town's location at the intersection of US 19 and US 460 provides access to the broader region, making it a convenient shopping destination for residents of the surrounding rural areas.

**VCEDA Support and Economic Development**

Lebanon is the headquarters of the Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority (VCEDA), the regional economic development agency that serves the seven-county, one-city coalfield region of Southwest Virginia. VCEDA has been instrumental in supporting Lebanon's economic development efforts, providing loans and grants to businesses and infrastructure projects in the town. In September 2025, VCEDA approved nine loans and grants totaling $7.83 million, including significant funding for projects in the Lebanon area .

VCEDA's support for Lebanon reflects the town's role as an administrative center for the region and its potential for continued economic growth. The Authority's loans and grants have supported a range of projects, including workforce development, site development, and business expansion. The town's proximity to the US 19 corridor and its location at the intersection with US 460 make it an attractive location for businesses seeking access to the broader region.

The future of Lebanon will depend on continued investment in its infrastructure, its manufacturing sector, and its downtown commercial district. The town's role as the county seat provides a stable economic base, while the investments in manufacturing and retail development are designed to create new opportunities. The town's location at the intersection of two major transportation corridors provides access to the broader region, a strategic advantage that the town's leaders are working to capitalize on.

---

## Abingdon: A Model of Cultural and Economic Revitalization

**Historical Roots: A Vital Location on the Great Valley Road**

Abingdon, the county seat of Washington County, has a history that predates the formation of Tazewell County by several decades. The town was established on the Great Valley Road, an important transportation route that connected the Shenandoah Valley to the broader Appalachian region. The town's location on this route made it a natural center for commerce and administration, and its role as a gateway to the Southwest Virginia region ensured its importance throughout the nineteenth century.

The town's historical significance has been preserved in its architecture and cultural institutions, providing a foundation for the cultural tourism that has become an important part of its modern economy. Abingdon is known for its handsome federal buildings and period homes, which have been maintained and restored over the years. The town's cultural heritage is a source of pride for its residents and a draw for visitors seeking to experience the history and character of the Appalachian region.

The Barter Theatre, founded during the Great Depression in 1933, is one of Abingdon's most significant cultural institutions . The theatre, which vies for the position of oldest operating theatre in the United States, was established during the Depression with admission set at 35 cents, or the equivalent in vegetables or dairy products . The theatre's name reflects the spirit of resourcefulness that characterized the Depression era, and its continuing operation is a testament to the town's cultural vitality.

**Modern Economy: Tourism, Healthcare, and Professional Services**

Abingdon has managed to diversify its economy successfully, becoming a regional center for healthcare, tourism, and professional services. The town's total retail sales were $380,575,000 in 2022, with retail sales per capita of $45,731, significantly higher than the state average . The healthcare sector is a major employer, with total health care and social assistance receipts/revenue of $342,242,000 in 2022 . The town's median household income was $54,544 in 2024, with a per capita income of $48,002 .

The town's tourism sector is built on its cultural heritage and natural beauty, with attractions including the Barter Theatre, the Virginia Highlands Festival, and the nearby Appalachian Trail. The town's historic downtown has been preserved and revitalized, with shops, restaurants, and galleries that attract visitors from across the region. The town's farmers market, held weekly during the summer, features locally produced syrups, fabrics, and meat products, supporting the local agricultural economy .

Abingdon's economy has been strengthened by its role as a regional center for professional services, including legal, financial, and consulting services. The town's location on the US 19 corridor provides access to the broader region, making it a convenient location for professionals serving clients throughout Southwest Virginia. The town's quality of life and cultural amenities have made it an attractive location for professionals and retirees, contributing to its economic stability.

**Agricultural Support Services and Local Food Production**

Abingdon serves as an important center for agricultural support services, providing the services that farmers in the surrounding rural areas require. The town's location on the US 19 corridor provides access to the broader region, and its agricultural infrastructure supports the cattle, dairy, and timber industries that are important to the regional economy. The town's farmers market, which operates weekly during the summer, provides a direct connection between farmers and consumers .

The local food movement has taken hold in Abingdon, with a growing appreciation for the benefits of local produce . As one local producer noted, "People are willing to pay more" for locally produced food, but there is a balance to be struck between fair pricing and accessibility . The COVID-19 pandemic revealed the fragility of the supply chains that dominate the American food system, and there has been a renewed interest in local production and processing as a result .

The town's support for agriculture extends beyond the farmers market to include adult education programs that help farmers improve their operations . As a former mayor noted, "It just amazes me the talent we have here" . The town's commitment to agricultural education and support reflects a broader recognition that agriculture remains an important part of the region's economy and that supporting local farmers is essential to the region's economic future.

**Culture as an Economic Driver**

Abingdon has demonstrated that culture can serve as a powerful driver of economic development, transforming a town that could have been devastated by industrial decline into a vibrant center for arts and culture. The Barter Theatre, which was established during the Great Depression, continues to attract visitors and support the local economy . The town's cultural amenities have made it an attractive location for residents and businesses, and the tourism that they generate provides a significant boost to the local economy.

The town's economic success is reflected in the range of cultural offerings available, from the Barter Theatre to the Virginia Highlands Festival and the weekly farmers market . These cultural offerings are not merely entertainment; they are economic drivers that bring visitors and residents into the town, supporting the local retail and hospitality sectors. The town's investment in culture has paid dividends, creating a virtuous cycle of economic growth and cultural vitality.

The future of Abingdon will depend on continued investment in its cultural assets, its downtown infrastructure, and its quality of life. The town's success provides a model for other communities in the region seeking to diversify their economies and build a prosperous future. As one local leader noted, "Times have changed... the coal is gone" , and the communities that have recognized this reality and adapted to it are the ones that are succeeding.

---

## Beckley: The Commercial and Retail Center of Southern West Virginia

**Historical Roots: A Growing City on the US 19 Corridor**

Beckley, West Virginia, is the largest city in southern West Virginia on the US 19 corridor, serving as a commercial and retail center for the broader region. The city's growth was driven by the development of the coal industry in the surrounding area, and its economy was closely tied to the extraction of coal from the rich seams of southern West Virginia. The city's location on the US 19 corridor provided access to the broader region, and its role as a transportation hub ensured its importance throughout the industrial era.

The city's history reflects the broader story of the Appalachian coalfields, with the boom years of the early twentieth century followed by decline as the coal industry contracted. The city's population has stabilized in recent decades, and its economy has diversified beyond coal to include healthcare, retail, education, and services. The city's role as the commercial and retail center of southern West Virginia has been strengthened by investments in infrastructure, including the Beckley Z-Way project.

**Modern Economy: Commercial Hub, Retail, and Education**

Beckley's economy today is characterized by its role as a commercial and retail center for southern West Virginia, with the city's retail establishments serving a population that extends well beyond the city's boundaries. The city's healthcare sector is a significant employer, with hospitals and medical facilities providing employment for hundreds of residents. The education sector, including the WVU Beckley campus, is a growing part of the economy, providing employment and preparing the workforce of the future.

West Virginia University's Beckley campus, home to the WVU Institute of Technology (WVU Tech), generated a total annual economic impact of $94.4 million in FY24, serving as a key anchor for workforce development and community growth in the southern part of the state . Of this total, $16.6 million came from direct institutional spending on operations, payroll, and capital investments, while an additional $77.8 million was produced through indirect and induced impacts as university-related spending circulated through local businesses, suppliers, and households . The campus supports 849 jobs statewide, including 307 on-campus positions and 542 additional jobs in surrounding industries .

The Beckley area's job market has seen fluctuations in recent years, with the area losing 200 jobs in April 2026 after gaining 300 in March 2026 . Over the latest five-year period (May 2021 to April 2026), the Beckley area gained jobs in 28 out of 60 months, with the highest gains occurring in July 2021 (+900) and the weakest in January 2025 (-600) . These fluctuations reflect the ongoing economic transition that the area is undergoing.

**The Beckley Z-Way: Reducing Congestion and Improving Access**

The Beckley Z-Way is one of the most significant infrastructure projects in the region, designed to reduce congestion and improve access to the city. The project, a 5.5-mile stretch of new highway running from Shady Spring through Beaver and into Beckley, has been in planning for over two decades. The groundbreaking ceremony in September 2024 marked the beginning of construction, with the project expected to be completed by the spring of 2028.

The need for the Z-Way reflects the growth and development of the Beckley area, with more than 17,000 residents and more than 16,000 traveling vehicles passing through the area daily. The city's attractions, including shopping, locally made arts and crafts, miles of hiking, and underground mining tours, draw visitors from across the region. The New River Gorge National Park and Preserve, located nearby, adds to the tourism traffic that contributes to congestion on the highway.

The Z-Way project is expected to have a significant economic impact, improving access to businesses, reducing travel times, and enhancing the quality of life for residents of the region. The project is funded through the governor's Roads to Prosperity program, money for which was approved by voters in a 2017 referendum that passed by approximately 73 percent. The project reflects a bipartisan commitment to infrastructure investment in the region.

**Future Prospects**

The future of Beckley will depend on continued investment in its infrastructure, its downtown, and its economic diversification. The Z-Way project, once completed, will enhance the city's connectivity and make it more attractive to businesses and visitors. The WVU Beckley campus, with its focus on workforce development and economic vitality, will continue to play a crucial role in the city's economic future. The city's status as the commercial and retail center of southern West Virginia will be strengthened by continued investment in its downtown and retail infrastructure.

The city's tourism sector has significant potential for growth, with the New River Gorge National Park and Preserve drawing visitors from across the country. The city's role as a gateway to the park can be strengthened through continued investment in tourism infrastructure, including hotels, restaurants, and visitor services. The city's cultural amenities, including museums, galleries, and performing arts venues, can also contribute to a vibrant tourism sector.

The economic diversification of Beckley will require continued efforts in workforce development, business attraction, and infrastructure investment. The city's leadership has demonstrated a commitment to these efforts, with investments in the Z-Way project, the WVU campus, and downtown revitalization. The city's future will be built on a foundation of education, healthcare, retail, and tourism, with coal continuing to recede as an economic force.

---

## Conclusion: The Urban System of the US 19 Corridor

The cities of the US 19 corridor represent a diverse urban system, each with its own distinctive character and economic identity. Tazewell, the county seat, is reinventing itself through tourism and proximity to renewable energy projects. Bluefield, the historic gateway to the coalfields, is revitalizing its downtown and leveraging its manufacturing and transportation assets. Richlands, once a coal boom town, is building a future on healthcare and new infrastructure. Lebanon, the Russell County seat, is expanding its manufacturing base and serving as a regional retail center. Abingdon, a model of cultural and economic revitalization, is building on its heritage to attract visitors and businesses. Beckley, the largest city in southern West Virginia, is investing in infrastructure and education to secure its economic future.

Together, these cities form a network of economic activity that supports the broader region, providing employment, services, and quality of life for hundreds of thousands of residents. The success of the US 19 corridor's urban system is not guaranteed; it depends on continued investment in infrastructure, workforce development, and economic diversification. But the evidence from across the corridor is encouraging: the cities are adapting to changing circumstances, building on their strengths, and creating new opportunities for their residents.

The story of the US 19 corridor's cities is ultimately a story of resilience, of communities that have faced significant challenges and have responded with creativity, determination, and hope. The coal industry may be declining, but the region's cities are finding new ways to thrive. Their success is a testament to the human capacity for adaptation and renewal, and a guide for other communities facing similar challenges.

### Part III: Strategic Growth Pillars

# Chapter Seven: Reinventing Energy Production

## Section 7.1: Pumped Storage Hydropower (PSH) Overview

### The Fundamentals of Pumped Storage Technology

Pumped Storage Hydropower represents one of the most mature and reliable forms of grid-scale energy storage available to modern electrical systems. The fundamental principle underlying PSH technology is elegantly simple yet operationally sophisticated: water is pumped from a lower reservoir to an upper reservoir during periods of low electricity demand and low energy prices, then released back through turbines to generate electricity during periods of high demand and high prices . This cycle of pumping and generating allows PSH facilities to function as enormous batteries, storing energy in the form of gravitational potential energy and converting it back to electrical energy with remarkable efficiency and speed.

The basic infrastructure of a PSH facility consists of two reservoirs at different elevations, connected by tunnels or penstocks that channel water between them. The upper reservoir serves as the primary energy storage medium, with the volume of water it contains and the elevation difference between the two reservoirs determining the total energy storage capacity of the facility . When the facility operates in generation mode, water flows from the upper reservoir through the penstocks to the lower reservoir, passing through turbine-generators that convert the kinetic energy of falling water into electrical energy. When operating in pumping mode, the turbines reverse function to become pumps, using electricity from the grid to move water from the lower to the upper reservoir, where it is stored until needed .

The efficiency of PSH facilities is remarkably high, with modern installations achieving round-trip efficiencies of 70 to 80 percent. This means that for every unit of electricity used to pump water to the upper reservoir, approximately 0.7 to 0.8 units can be recovered during generation. While this efficiency loss is significant, it is comparable to or better than many alternative storage technologies, and the long operational life of PSH facilities—often 50 years or more—makes them economically competitive over their full lifecycle. The daily operational cycle of a typical PSH facility involves pumping during nighttime hours when electricity demand and prices are low, and generating during daytime and evening peak hours when demand and prices are high .

The turbines used in PSH facilities are sophisticated pieces of engineering, designed to operate efficiently in both pumping and generating modes. Most modern PSH facilities use reversible pump-turbine units that can rotate in either direction, serving as pumps when spinning one way and as turbines when spinning the other. These units are typically vertical-axis Francis turbines, a type of reaction turbine well-suited to the medium-head applications common in PSH facilities. The transition between pumping and generating modes can be accomplished in a matter of minutes, allowing PSH facilities to respond quickly to changes in grid conditions .

The geological and hydrological requirements for PSH facilities are significant. Suitable sites require a substantial elevation difference between the upper and lower reservoirs, typically at least 300 to 500 meters, with the Tazewell County site offering a 1,000-foot (approximately 305-meter) drop that is well within the optimal range . The reservoirs themselves require sufficient water storage capacity to support daily cycling, with the upper reservoir typically holding enough water to generate at full capacity for 6 to 12 hours. The geology of the site must be suitable for dam construction, with stable bedrock that can support the weight of the reservoirs and the hydraulic pressures they contain .

The environmental impact of PSH facilities is generally moderate compared to other forms of energy infrastructure. The reservoirs occupy land that would otherwise be used for other purposes, but the total land area is relatively small for the amount of energy storage provided. The Bath County facility, for example, occupies only about 5,000 acres while providing 3,003 megawatts of generating capacity . The facilities can also provide environmental benefits, such as improved stream flows during droughts from regulated releases from the reservoirs. The Bath County facility supplements flows to Back Creek and Little Back Creek during dry periods, enhancing the environment for fish and other aquatic life .

### The Bath County Pumped Storage Station: A North American Benchmark

The Bath County Pumped Storage Station, located in the rugged Allegheny Mountains of western Virginia, serves as the definitive North American benchmark for PSH technology. With a net generating capacity of 3,003 megawatts, it is the largest pumped storage facility in North America and one of the largest in the world . The facility is capable of powering approximately 750,000 homes, providing essential grid reliability services to a six-state region . The sheer scale of the Bath County facility is difficult to comprehend: the lower reservoir dam is 135 feet high and 2,400 feet long, containing 4 million cubic yards of earth and rock fill, while the upper reservoir dam is 460 feet high and 2,200 feet long, containing 18 million cubic yards of fill .

The scale of construction required for the Bath County facility was monumental. The earth and rock fill moved to construct the dams and other project facilities, if piled up, would create a mountain 1,000 feet high . Enough concrete was poured to build 200 miles of interstate highway. The upper reservoir covers 265 surface acres, and its water level fluctuates 106 feet during normal operation . The lower reservoir covers 555 surface acres, with a water level fluctuation of 60 feet during operation . When operating at full capacity, water flows through the tunnels to the lower reservoir at a rate of 13.5 million gallons per minute, turning six turbine generators .

The Bath County facility came online in 1985, receiving its operating license from the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission in January 1977 and commencing commercial operation in December 1985 . The facility was cited as one of the nation's most outstanding engineering achievements of 1985, a recognition of the technical complexity and scale of the project. The facility is owned jointly by Dominion Energy (60%), Bath County Energy, LLC (approximately 24%), and Alleghany Power System (approximately 16%) . The ownership structure reflects the collaborative nature of large-scale energy infrastructure projects, with multiple utilities sharing the costs and benefits of the facility.

The operational characteristics of the Bath County facility provide valuable insights for the proposed projects in the Tazewell County region. The facility operates on a daily cycle, pumping water from the lower to the upper reservoir during nighttime hours when electricity demand is low, and generating power during daytime and early evening hours when demand peaks. The facility can respond quickly to changes in grid conditions, reaching full generating capacity within minutes of being called upon. This rapid response capability makes PSH facilities particularly valuable for grid stability, as they can compensate for sudden losses of generation from other sources .

The Bath County facility also demonstrates the long-term economic viability of PSH technology. The facility has been in continuous operation for nearly 40 years, with its original equipment still functioning effectively.
