The longevity of PSH facilities provides a significant economic advantage over battery storage, which typically requires replacement after 10 to 15 years. The long operational life of PSH facilities, combined with their low operating and maintenance costs, makes them highly cost-effective over their full lifecycle, even if upfront capital costs are substantial .

### PSH as a Giant Battery for Renewable Energy Storage

The role of PSH in supporting the integration of renewable energy sources into the electrical grid is becoming increasingly important as the penetration of wind and solar generation continues to grow. The variability and intermittency of renewable energy sources create significant challenges for grid operators, who must balance supply and demand in real time. PSH facilities provide a solution to this challenge by offering large-scale, long-duration energy storage that can absorb excess generation during periods of high renewable output and release it during periods of low output .

The "giant battery" analogy is particularly apt for PSH, as these facilities perform the same function as chemical batteries but at vastly larger scales. While a typical utility-scale battery facility might provide 100 megawatts of power for four hours (400 megawatt-hours of storage), a PSH facility like the proposed Tazewell County project would provide 870 megawatts of power for up to 10 hours (8,700 megawatt-hours of storage) . This tenfold increase in storage capacity makes PSH the only technology capable of providing the long-duration storage needed to support a grid powered primarily by renewable energy sources .

The value of PSH for renewable integration is particularly apparent in the context of the Virginia coalfield region's energy transition. The legislation passed by the Virginia General Assembly in 2017 requires that PSH facilities in the coalfield region utilize "on-site or off-site renewable energy resources as all or a portion of their power source" and that those renewable resources be "located in the coalfield region of the Commonwealth" . This requirement ensures that PSH projects in the region will directly support the development of renewable energy sources, creating a virtuous cycle of clean energy investment and economic development.

The operational synergy between PSH and renewable energy sources is significant. Wind energy typically produces the most electricity during nighttime hours, when demand is low and prices are depressed. Solar energy produces the most electricity during midday hours, when demand is moderate but often below peak levels. PSH facilities can absorb this excess renewable generation by pumping water to the upper reservoir during these periods of high renewable output and low demand, then release the stored energy during peak demand periods when electricity prices are highest and the grid is most stressed .

The future role of PSH in the energy system is likely to expand significantly as the transition to renewable energy accelerates. The National Hydropower Association has identified the need for increased pumped storage capacity as a critical element of grid modernization, recognizing that the greater penetration of solar and wind resources requires corresponding increases in storage capacity . The Tazewell County region, with its ideal topography and existing infrastructure, is well-positioned to contribute to this national need, providing clean, reliable energy storage that will support the broader transition to a sustainable energy system.

---

## Section 7.2: Virginia's PSH Legislation and Policy Support

### The 2017 General Assembly: SB 1418 and HB 1760

The legal and policy framework for PSH development in the Virginia coalfield region was established in 2017 through the passage of two complementary pieces of legislation: Senate Bill 1418, sponsored by Senator A. Benton Chafin, Jr. (R-Russell), and House Bill 1760, sponsored by Delegate Terry G. Kilgore (R-Scott) . These bills, signed into law by Governor Terry McAuliffe, represented a bipartisan commitment to supporting the economic development of the coalfield region through the deployment of pumped storage hydropower technology . The legislation was part of a broader expansion of Dominion Energy's power generating facilities in Virginia, providing a regulatory pathway for the development of PSH projects in the region .

The key provisions of SB 1418 and HB 1760 were designed to address the specific challenges of developing large-scale energy infrastructure in economically distressed regions. The legislation authorized electrical utilities to request rate adjustment clauses from the Virginia State Corporation Commission for recovery of costs associated with "pumped hydroelectricity generation and storage facilities" that use renewable energy "as all or a portion of the power source" in coalfield regions . This cost recovery mechanism was essential to making PSH projects financially viable, as the substantial upfront capital costs would otherwise be difficult to finance without a guaranteed return on investment.

A central provision of the legislation declared that the construction of pumped storage facilities in the coalfield region "is in the public interest" . This declaration has significant legal implications, as it establishes a presumption that PSH projects serve important public policy objectives and should therefore receive appropriate regulatory support. The "public interest" designation also facilitates the permitting process by providing a clear policy basis for the approval of projects that might otherwise face regulatory hurdles . The legislation thus provides both a financial mechanism and a policy rationale for PSH development.

The legislation also included provisions designed to ensure that PSH projects in the coalfield region would support the transition to renewable energy. The requirement that facilities "utilize on-site or off-site renewable energy resources as all or a portion of their power source" creates a direct link between PSH development and renewable energy investment . This provision reflects the recognition that energy storage is essential to the broader renewable energy transition, and that PSH facilities should be integrated into the clean energy system rather than functioning as standalone projects.

The "coalfield region" definition in the legislation encompasses the counties of Buchanan, Dickenson, Lee, Russell, Scott, Tazewell, and Wise, as well as the City of Norton. This geographic restriction ensures that the economic benefits of PSH development will accrue to the communities that have been most affected by the decline of the coal industry. The legislation thus serves both energy policy and economic development objectives, addressing the dual challenges of grid modernization and regional economic transition.

### Cost Recovery for Utility Investments

The cost recovery mechanism established by the 2017 legislation was essential to making PSH projects financially viable for utilities. The construction of a PSH facility requires substantial upfront capital investment—the proposed Tazewell County project was estimated at approximately $2 billion—and the return on this investment would be realized over the 50-year operational life of the facility . Without a mechanism to recover these costs through utility rates, the financial risk of PSH development would be prohibitive for most utilities.

The legislation permits utilities to file for a rate adjustment clause with the State Corporation Commission, which would allow the recovery of project costs through customer rates. This regulatory mechanism provides a predictable revenue stream that supports project financing, reducing the cost of capital and making the project financially viable. The rate adjustment clause creates what Appalachian Voices, a regional environmental organization, described as "an essentially zero-risk proposition for Dominion," with ratepayers bearing the financial risk of the project . The cost recovery mechanism thus provides the financial certainty that major infrastructure projects require.

The cost recovery provision represents a significant policy tool for economic development, as it allows utilities to invest in infrastructure projects that might otherwise be too risky or costly to undertake. The economic benefits of PSH development—including construction jobs, permanent employment, and tax revenue—are substantial, and the cost recovery mechanism enables these benefits to be realized while spreading the costs across the utility's customer base. The rate impacts of PSH development are relatively modest, as the projects generate revenue through the sale of electricity during peak demand periods, offsetting some of the costs.

The cost recovery provision also includes a requirement that at least part of the energy used to operate the facility must be generated by renewable resources in the coalfield region . This requirement creates a market for renewable energy in the region, supporting the development of wind and solar projects that can provide the energy needed to power the PSH facility. The provision thus creates a synergistic relationship between PSH development and renewable energy investment, with each supporting the other in the region's energy transition.

The financial structure of PSH projects has been validated by comparable projects in other regions. The Lewis Ridge Pumped Storage Project in Kentucky, for example, has secured $81 million in funding from the U.S. Department of Energy, demonstrating the federal government's willingness to support PSH development as part of the clean energy transition . The Virginia legislation provides a similar federal-state partnership framework, with the state providing the regulatory support and cost recovery mechanism that enables projects to attract federal funding.

### Federal Energy Regulatory Commission Application Process

The Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) plays a central role in the development of PSH projects, as it has exclusive authority to license non-federal hydropower projects under the Federal Power Act. The FERC application process is rigorous and comprehensive, requiring extensive environmental, geological, archeological, and technical studies to demonstrate the project's feasibility and its compliance with applicable regulations . The process typically takes several years to complete, with multiple stages of review and public consultation.

The preliminary permit application filed by Dominion Energy for the Tazewell County site in September 2017 initiated the FERC process . The preliminary permit provides the permit holder with priority status for the project site for up to three years, during which time the permit holder can conduct feasibility studies without competition from other developers. The preliminary permit does not authorize construction but does enable the permit holder to invest in the studies needed to support a full license application .

The FERC licensing process for PSH projects involves multiple stages, including the preparation of a pre-application document (PAD), the conduct of environmental and technical studies, and the filing of a license application. Dominion Energy planned to file a pre-application document with FERC in late 2019 and to obtain any applicable state and local permits . The timeline for FERC licensing is substantial, with the entire process typically taking five to ten years from preliminary permit to construction authorization.

The environmental review requirements for PSH projects are particularly demanding, as facilities can have significant impacts on water resources, aquatic ecosystems, and terrestrial habitats. The FERC licensing process requires detailed studies of water quality, fish and wildlife resources, and cultural resources, as well as an assessment of the project's impacts on recreational opportunities. The Tazewell County site would need to have environmental impacts taken into consideration, according to the preliminary permit application filed by Dominion Energy . The environmental review process ensures that PSH projects are developed in an environmentally responsible manner.

The FERC licensing process also includes extensive public consultation, with opportunities for interested parties to comment on proposed projects and to participate in the regulatory process. Dominion Energy hosted informational open house events in Tazewell and Bland Counties in July 2019 as part of this process . The public consultation process ensures that community concerns are addressed in the project's development, and that the benefits and impacts of the project are fully understood by affected stakeholders.

---

## Section 7.3: Potential PSH Sites in the Region

### Bullitt Mine Site in Wise County

The Bullitt Mine site near Appalachia, Virginia, represents one of the most innovative potential applications of PSH technology in the coalfield region. The mine, which was closed in 1997 and is currently flooded, could conceivably use its underground cavities as a lower reservoir, eliminating the need to construct a new reservoir for the lower storage . This repurposing of abandoned mine infrastructure would represent a sustainable reuse of the region's industrial heritage, transforming an environmental liability into an energy asset .

The concept of using abandoned mine cavities for pumped storage is relatively new and has never been done before, making the Bullitt site an experimental project with significant technical challenges . The Virginia Department of Mines, Minerals and Energy previously conducted research on the feasibility of using abandoned mines for pumped storage and identified the Bullitt Mine as one of its top candidates in the coalfield region . This prior research provided a foundation for the feasibility studies conducted by Virginia Tech for Dominion Energy .

The technical characteristics of the Bullitt site differ significantly from a conventional PSH facility. The capacity of the Bullitt site would be much smaller than the Tazewell County site, with estimates suggesting a generating capacity of 100 to 150 megawatts . This reduced capacity stems from the need for smaller equipment and a shorter elevation drop compared to conventional PSH facilities . The smaller scale of the Bullitt project would also mean fewer construction jobs and less economic impact, though the innovative nature of the project would make it a valuable demonstration of the potential for repurposing mine infrastructure.

The environmental and geological studies of the Bullitt site face unique challenges. The underground mine cavities must be assessed for structural integrity, water tightness, and suitability for rapid water level changes. The potential for water leakage through mine fractures is a significant concern, as any losses would reduce the efficiency and effectiveness of the facility. The studies being conducted by Virginia Tech are designed to determine if the site is technically feasible and if the economic benefits justify the investment .

### Tazewell County Site on East River Mountain

The East River Mountain site in Tazewell County represents the primary PSH project for the region, with a proposed capacity of 870 megawatts and a 1,000-foot elevation drop that is ideal for PSH operations . Dominion Energy owns approximately 2,600 acres of the site near East River Mountain, which it purchased in 2009 when pursuing another electric generation project . The site's topography, with its substantial elevation difference and proximity to existing transmission infrastructure, makes it an attractive location for PSH development .

The proposed 4,100-acre site in Tazewell County would be a closed-loop system, meaning it would not connect to an existing, naturally flowing water source . The closed-loop design minimizes environmental impacts by avoiding alterations to natural waterways and eliminating the need for fish passage facilities. The environmental review requirements for a closed-loop system are generally less demanding than for open-loop systems, as the environmental impacts are more contained and manageable .

The preliminary permit application filed by Dominion Energy with FERC in September 2017 began the formal process of evaluating the Tazewell site . The permit allowed the company to conduct environmental, geological, archaeological, and technical studies to assess the feasibility of the project. The studies included extensive core drilling at eight sites and the construction of a road on the top of the mountain to access the upper reservoir site . The results of these studies would determine whether the project should proceed to the full license application stage.

The Tazewell County site would require a substantial investment of approximately $2 billion, according to Dominion Energy's estimates . This investment would be among the largest economic development projects in the region's history, with the potential to create thousands of construction jobs and dozens of permanent positions. The project would also generate significant tax revenue for Tazewell County, estimated at approximately $2.5 million annually .

### Mine Pool Pumped Storage Potential

The potential for using abandoned mine pools for pumped storage has attracted significant research interest, with the West Virginia Geological and Economic Survey (WVGES) conducting studies on the feasibility of this approach . The concept involves using flooded underground mine workings as the lower reservoir for a PSH facility, with a surface reservoir serving as the upper reservoir. This approach would repurpose the extensive mine infrastructure that exists throughout the region, providing a sustainable reuse of mine sites that might otherwise remain environmental liabilities.

The advantages of mine pool pumped storage include the reduced need for new reservoir construction, the use of existing industrial infrastructure, and the potential to create new economic activity on sites that have been idle for years. The mine pool approach also provides a solution to the challenge of water supply, as the flooded mines contain substantial volumes of water that can be cycled through the system. The water quality in mine pools can be a concern, but with appropriate treatment, it can be used for PSH operations.

The technical challenges of mine pool pumped storage are significant. The underground cavities must be sufficiently large and stable to serve as a reservoir, and the water must be contained to prevent losses that would reduce the system's efficiency. The geology of the mine workings must be studied extensively to ensure that the site is suitable for the rapid water level changes that characterize PSH operations. These technical challenges have limited the development of mine pool PSH, but the research being conducted in the region could demonstrate the viability of this approach.

The environmental benefits of mine pool pumped storage are substantial. By repurposing abandoned mine sites, the approach reduces the need for new land disturbance and provides a use for sites that might otherwise remain as environmental liabilities. The water quality of mine pools can be improved through the cycling of water, as oxygenation and mixing can reduce the acidity and metal content of mine drainage. The mine pool approach thus provides both energy and environmental benefits.

### Geothermal Heating and Cooling from Mine Pools

The potential for utilizing mine pools for geothermal heating and cooling represents an additional opportunity to leverage the region's mining heritage for renewable energy. The flooded mine workings in the coalfield region contain substantial volumes of water that maintain a relatively constant temperature throughout the year, typically around 55 to 60 degrees Fahrenheit. This geothermal resource can be used for heating and cooling buildings through heat pump systems, providing a clean and efficient alternative to conventional HVAC systems.

The use of mine water for geothermal purposes has been demonstrated in other regions, including the United Kingdom and Germany, where abandoned mine workings have been repurposed for district heating and cooling. The concept is relatively simple: a heat pump extracts heat from the mine water in winter for building heating, and rejects heat to the mine water in summer for building cooling. The system's efficiency is high because the mine water temperature is relatively constant, providing a more stable source of heating and cooling than the ambient air.

The potential for mine water geothermal in the Tazewell County region is significant, as the extensive mine workings provide a distributed resource that could serve multiple buildings throughout the region. The town of Tazewell, in particular, could benefit from geothermal district heating and cooling, reducing energy costs for public buildings and businesses. The mine water geothermal resource would also complement the PSH project, providing an additional revenue stream and an additional renewable energy benefit from the region's mining legacy.

The development of mine water geothermal would require investment in infrastructure, including pumps, heat exchangers, and distribution systems. The costs of this infrastructure would depend on the specific site characteristics and the scale of the project. However, the long-term benefits of reduced energy costs and increased energy independence could make mine water geothermal an attractive investment for the region.

---

## Section 7.4: Economic Impact of PSH Development

### The $2 Billion Investment

The proposed PSH facility on East River Mountain represents an estimated investment of approximately $2 billion, making it one of the largest economic development projects in the history of the coalfield region . This investment would be deployed over a 10-year period, with the majority of the expenditure occurring during the construction phase . The scale of the investment is comparable to the Bath County facility, which was a similarly transformative infrastructure project for its region.

The $2 billion investment would have significant multiplier effects throughout the regional economy. The direct spending on materials, equipment, and labor would stimulate economic activity in a wide range of sectors, from construction and manufacturing to transportation and professional services. The indirect economic impacts, as the wages of construction workers are spent in the local economy, would generate additional economic activity that extends well beyond the direct spending of the project. The total economic impact of the project, including these multiplier effects, would be substantially larger than the $2 billion direct investment.

The investment would also create a long-term asset for the region, with the PSH facility providing reliable energy storage for decades to come. Unlike many infrastructure projects that have limited operational lifespans, PSH facilities can operate for 50 years or more, providing a long-term economic base for the region. The facility would also provide a source of tax revenue for Tazewell County, estimated at approximately $2.5 million annually . This tax revenue would support essential public services and reduce the burden on residential and commercial property taxpayers.

The financial structure of the PSH project would involve both private investment and potentially federal funding. The Lewis Ridge project in Kentucky, for example, has secured $81 million in funding from the U.S. Department of Energy . Similar federal support could be available for the Tazewell County project, particularly given the region's designation as an economically distressed area. The combination of private investment and federal funding would reduce the financial risk for the utility and ensure that the project moves forward in a timely manner.

### $576 Million in Economic Benefits to Virginia

According to a study by Chmura Economics and Analytics, commissioned by Dominion Energy, the proposed PSH project would generate significant economic benefits for both Southwest Virginia and the entire Commonwealth . The study estimated that a $2 billion project would support a total of 86 jobs annually in the state during the construction period, with 76 of those jobs in Southwest Virginia . While the annual job creation is modest, the cumulative impact over the 10-year construction period would be substantial.

The economic benefits of the PSH project extend beyond direct employment to include the broader economic impact of increased economic activity. The construction phase would generate demand for materials, equipment, and services from businesses throughout the state, creating economic activity that ripples through the supply chain. The indirect and induced economic impacts of the project would be significant, as the wages of construction workers are spent in the local economy and as businesses expand to meet the demands of the project.

The $576 million in economic benefits estimated for the Commonwealth reflects the broad range of impacts that the project would generate. This total includes direct spending on the project, indirect spending through the supply chain, and induced spending resulting from increased household income. The economic benefits would be concentrated in Southwest Virginia, where the project is located and where the economic impacts would be most pronounced.

The long-term economic benefits of the PSH project would continue after construction is complete. The facility would employ approximately 50 permanent workers, providing stable, high-quality jobs for the region . The tax revenue generated by the facility would support public services and reduce the burden on other taxpayers. The facility would also provide economic benefits through its role in the energy market, generating revenue from the sale of electricity during peak demand periods.

### Employment Impact: 3,000 Construction Jobs

The construction of the proposed PSH project would generate significant employment for the region, with estimates suggesting 3,000 construction jobs during the construction period . These jobs would be distributed across a range of skilled trades, including heavy equipment operators, construction workers, electricians, pipefitters, and engineers. The construction period would be approximately 4 to 5 years, providing sustained employment for the region's workforce .

The demand for skilled labor during construction would create opportunities for workforce development and training. The building trades unions, which have a strong presence in the region, would play a key role in providing the workforce for the project. Apprenticeship programs would provide opportunities for workers to gain the skills needed for construction jobs, creating a pipeline of skilled labor for the project and for future projects in the region.

The employment impact of the PSH project would extend beyond the direct construction jobs to include indirect employment in the supply chain. The demand for materials, equipment, and services would create jobs in manufacturing, transportation, and other sectors. These indirect jobs would add to the employment impact of the project, creating economic opportunities throughout the region.

The employment impact of the PSH project can be compared to the Lewis Ridge project in Kentucky, which is expected to support approximately 2,300 construction jobs over four years . The Goldendale project in Washington State is expected to create more than 3,000 construction jobs during its construction period . These comparable projects demonstrate the substantial employment impact that PSH development can generate for the region.

### $12 Million Annual Tax Revenue

The proposed PSH project would generate approximately $12 million annually in tax revenue for the affected local governments, according to comparable project estimates . This revenue would be distributed across multiple jurisdictions, with Tazewell County receiving the largest share, estimated at approximately $2.5 million annually . The tax revenue would support essential public services, including schools, public safety, and infrastructure maintenance.

The tax revenue from the PSH facility would be generated through multiple mechanisms. The facility would pay property taxes on the value of its infrastructure, including the reservoirs, dams, and generating equipment. The facility would also pay business taxes and other local levies that are imposed on commercial enterprises. The revenue generated from these taxes would be a significant addition to the county's tax base, reducing the burden on residential and commercial property taxpayers.

The tax revenue would also support regional economic development initiatives, as the counties in the region are required to distribute some of the revenue to support economic development efforts. The revenue-sharing agreement among the counties would ensure that the benefits of the project are spread across the region, not just the county where the facility is located . This revenue-sharing approach recognizes the regional nature of economic development and the importance of distributing benefits equitably.

The tax revenue from the PSH facility would be a stable and predictable source of funding for local governments, unlike revenue from coal-related activities, which has been declining. The long-term operational life of the facility would ensure that the tax revenue continues for decades, providing a reliable source of funding for public services. The tax revenue would thus contribute to the long-term fiscal stability of the region's local governments.

### Projected Gross Profit: $47 Million in First Year

The projected gross profit of the PSH facility would be significant, with comparable projects suggesting first-year gross profit of approximately $47 million. This profit would be generated through the sale of electricity during peak demand periods, when wholesale electricity prices are highest. The business model of PSH is straightforward: the facility buys electricity during periods of low demand and low prices (nighttime, weekends, and other off-peak periods) and sells electricity during periods of high demand and high prices (weekday afternoons and evenings).

The profitability of PSH facilities is driven by the price differential between peak and off-peak electricity. The Bath County facility, for example, generates electricity during peak demand periods and pumps water back to the upper reservoir during off-peak periods. The price differential provides the economic return that justifies the investment in the facility. The profitability of PSH facilities has been demonstrated over decades of operation, with facilities generating consistent returns for their owners.

The profitability of the Tazewell County facility would be supported by the region's electricity market and the increasing need for energy storage. The integration of renewable energy sources into the grid, which is being driven by state and federal policies, is increasing the demand for energy storage. The price differential between peak and off-peak electricity is likely to widen as the penetration of renewable energy increases, enhancing the profitability of PSH facilities.

The gross profit from the PSH facility would generate economic benefits for the region through tax revenue, employment, and economic activity. The profit would also enable the facility to invest in maintenance and upgrades, ensuring that the facility continues to operate effectively for decades. The long-term profitability of the facility would provide a stable economic base for the region.

---

## Section 7.5: Batteries as Hybrid PSH Support

### Batteries Handling Grid Frequency Regulation to Protect PSH Turbines

The integration of battery energy storage systems (BESS) with PSH facilities represents an innovative approach to optimizing grid services while protecting the mechanical integrity of PSH equipment. The hybrid configuration allows batteries to handle the rapid, short-duration fluctuations in grid frequency and voltage, while PSH provides the long-duration energy storage that is essential for managing the variability of renewable energy sources . This synergistic relationship between the two technologies enhances the overall performance and reliability of the energy storage system.

The frequency regulation function of batteries is particularly important for protecting PSH turbines from mechanical stress. PSH turbines are massive pieces of equipment, weighing thousands of tons, and they are designed for steady-state operation rather than rapid load changes. When a PSH facility is called upon to provide frequency regulation, the turbines must adjust their output frequently, creating wear and tear that can shorten the equipment's operational life. By having batteries handle these rapid fluctuations, the PSH turbines can operate in a more steady-state manner, reducing maintenance costs and extending their operational life.

The timescale differentiation between battery and PSH operation is a key element of the hybrid approach. Batteries can respond to grid fluctuations in milliseconds, making them ideal for the rapid frequency regulation that the grid requires . PSH facilities, by contrast, typically require several minutes to respond to grid changes, making them less suitable for rapid frequency regulation. The hybrid configuration allows the battery to handle the rapid fluctuations while the PSH facility handles the longer-duration energy storage, optimizing the use of both technologies.

The protection of PSH turbines through battery support has significant economic benefits. The maintenance costs of PSH turbines are substantial, and reducing the frequency of maintenance can significantly reduce the operating costs of the facility. The extended operational life of the equipment, resulting from reduced wear and tear, also provides economic benefits by delaying the need for capital-intensive equipment replacements. The economic benefits of this approach support the business case for hybrid PSH-battery projects .

### Cascading Control Approach Extending Equipment Lifespan

The cascading control approach for hybrid PSH-battery systems provides a sophisticated method for optimizing the operation of the two technologies while extending the lifespan of the PSH equipment . The approach involves a hierarchical control structure in which the battery provides the fast response to grid changes, while the PSH facility provides the slower but more sustained response. The control system is designed to prioritize the use of the battery for rapid fluctuations and to engage the PSH facility only when the battery's capacity is insufficient or when longer-duration storage is needed.

The cascading control approach uses advanced algorithms to coordinate the operation of the two storage technologies. The Dynamic Variational Mode Decomposition (VMD) method, for example, can be used to decompose power fluctuations into different frequency components, with high-frequency components addressed by the battery and low-frequency components addressed by the PSH facility . This decomposition allows the control system to allocate the regulation burden based on the capabilities of each technology, optimizing the overall system performance.

The lifespan extension benefits of the cascading control approach are significant. By reducing the frequency and magnitude of the load changes experienced by the PSH turbines, the control approach reduces wear and tear on the equipment. The hydraulic and mechanical components of the turbines, including the guide vanes, bearings, and shafts, experience less stress when the turbines operate in a steady-state mode. This reduced stress extends the operational life of the equipment, reducing the need for major overhauls and replacements .

The cascading control approach also improves the overall reliability of the hybrid system. The redundant storage capacity provided by the battery means that the system can continue to provide grid services even if the PSH facility is offline for maintenance. The battery can also provide rapid response to grid disturbances, enhancing the stability and reliability of the grid. The reliability improvements contribute to the economic benefits of the hybrid configuration, as the system is less likely to experience downtime that would result in lost revenue .

### Cost Comparison: PSH Alone More Cost-Effective

The cost comparison between PSH alone and PSH-battery hybrid systems must consider both capital costs and operating costs, as the hybrid configuration has different cost profiles for each technology. PSH facilities have high capital costs but low operating costs, while batteries have lower capital costs but higher operating costs due to the need for periodic replacement. The hybrid configuration combines the advantages of both technologies, but it also incurs the costs of both.

The capital costs of PSH facilities are substantial, with the proposed Tazewell County facility estimated at approximately $2 billion . The capital costs of batteries are lower on a per-megawatt basis, but the batteries must be replaced every 10 to 15 years, creating additional lifecycle costs. The hybrid configuration must include both the PSH infrastructure and the battery system, increasing the upfront capital costs relative to a PSH-only configuration.

The operating costs of PSH facilities are low, as the "fuel" (water) is free and the maintenance requirements are modest. The operating costs of batteries are higher, due to the need to replace the battery cells periodically and the energy losses associated with battery operation. The hybrid configuration has lower operating costs than a battery-only configuration, as the PSH facility provides the primary storage capacity, but the operating costs are higher than a PSH-only configuration due to the maintenance and replacement costs of the batteries.

The full lifecycle cost analysis of hybrid vs. PSH-only configurations shows that the hybrid configuration can be more cost-effective in certain applications . Research has demonstrated that a hybrid PSH-battery system can lower the full lifecycle cost by 27.4% compared to a battery-only alternative . The hybrid system also improves the renewable accommodation rate to 91.2% and reduces the average daily power fluctuation to 26.2% . These performance improvements can justify the additional cost of the hybrid configuration.

### Hybrid System Optimized for Reliability and Economic Performance

The optimization of hybrid PSH-battery systems requires a sophisticated approach to capacity allocation and operational control . The capacity allocation problem involves determining the optimal size of the battery relative to the PSH facility, balancing the costs and benefits of each technology. The operational control problem involves determining when to use the battery and when to use the PSH facility, optimizing the performance of the system under different grid conditions.

The multi-objective capacity configuration model for hybrid PSH-battery systems must consider multiple factors, including the power fluctuation rate, the renewable accommodation rate, and the lifecycle cost . The optimization of these objectives requires careful analysis of the specific application, including the characteristics of the renewable energy sources being integrated, the grid services required, and the cost profiles of the different technologies. The optimal configuration for one application may not be optimal for another, requiring site-specific analysis.

The performance of optimized hybrid systems is superior to either technology alone. The research has demonstrated that a hybrid PSH-battery system can improve high-frequency component accuracy by 35% and reduce regulation delay to 0.6-1.1 seconds, compared to a PSH-only configuration . The hybrid system also improves the renewable accommodation rate to 91.2%, ensuring that renewable energy is not wasted due to the inability of the grid to absorb it . These performance improvements enhance the economic viability of renewable energy projects in the region.

The economic performance of hybrid PSH-battery systems is enhanced by the ability to participate in multiple grid services. The battery can provide rapid frequency regulation, while the PSH facility can provide longer-duration energy storage and peak shaving. The combined revenue from these multiple services can improve the overall economic viability of the project. The hybrid configuration thus provides not only performance benefits but also economic benefits through revenue diversification.

### The Regulatory and Market Context for Hybrid Systems

The development of hybrid PSH-battery systems in the Tazewell County region must be understood in the context of the regulatory and market frameworks that govern energy storage. The Virginia legislation that supports PSH development is focused on PSH as a technology, but it does not explicitly address hybrid configurations. The regulatory framework for hybrid systems is still developing, with FERC and state regulators working to establish rules for the operation of hybrid energy storage systems.

The market opportunities for hybrid PSH-battery systems are significant and growing. The integration of renewable energy sources into the grid is increasing the demand for energy storage, creating revenue opportunities for PSH and hybrid systems. The price differential between peak and off-peak electricity, which is the primary revenue source for PSH facilities, is likely to widen as renewable penetration increases, enhancing the profitability of hybrid systems.

The participation of hybrid PSH-battery systems in frequency regulation markets is also an important revenue opportunity. The rapid response capabilities of batteries make them well-suited for frequency regulation, and the revenue from this service can enhance the economic viability of hybrid systems. The combination of energy arbitrage revenue and frequency regulation revenue can provide a diversified revenue base that reduces the risk of the project.

The future of hybrid PSH-battery systems in the region will depend on continued investment in the technology, the development of appropriate regulatory frameworks, and the evolution of electricity markets. The potential benefits of hybrid systems are substantial, and the interest in these systems is likely to grow as the integration of renewable energy accelerates. The Tazewell County region, with its ideal topography and supportive regulatory environment, is well-positioned to take advantage of these opportunities.

**Chapter 8: Pillar Two - Advanced Agriculture and Food Manufacturing**

# Chapter Eight: Pillar Two - Advanced Agriculture and Food Manufacturing

## Section 8.1: The Cattle and Livestock Industry

### The Historical Foundation of Cattle Farming

The cattle industry has been the bedrock of Tazewell County's agricultural economy since the earliest days of European settlement, establishing a tradition of livestock production that would persist through centuries of economic transformation. When the county was formed in 1799 from parts of Russell and Wythe counties, the initial economy was based on subsistence agriculture, but by 1850, more prosperous farmers were already selling cattle, grains, wool, maple sugar, and dairy products to markets beyond the county's borders. This early commercialization of agriculture established patterns of production that would define the region's rural economy for generations.

The agricultural census data from the early twentieth century reveals the dominance of cattle in the county's agricultural landscape. A 1903 report in the Tazewell Republican noted that "nearly all the cattle of Tazewell are exported," with the county and neighboring Russell County shipping more export cattle than any other counties in Virginia. The cattle were described as "the heaviest" and "great steers which make this section famous," reflecting the quality of the livestock produced in the region. The report observed that "everybody who has any land has cattle," indicating the widespread nature of cattle production across the county.

The comparison of Tazewell County's cattle industry to that of Kentucky, the nation's premier cattle-producing state, highlighted the exceptional quality of the region's livestock. The limestone soils of the county's valleys produced grass "equal to that of Kentucky," creating ideal conditions for cattle production. The reputation of Tazewell County cattle extended well beyond the region, with buyers coming from distant markets to purchase the animals. The cattle industry was not merely a component of the county's economy; it was the foundation upon which much of the county's prosperity was built.

The Stuart Land & Cattle Company, organized in 1884, became one of the oldest and largest cattle farms east of the Mississippi River, with its operations centered in Russell County and extending into Tazewell, Smyth, and Washington counties. At its peak, the Company's farmland covered over 45,000 acres, with four major holdings: the Elk Garden, Rich Mountain, Rosedale, and Clifton farms. The Company's success demonstrated the potential for large-scale cattle operations in the region and established a model of production that would influence generations of farmers.

The integration of cattle production with other agricultural activities characterized the region's farms. Cattle provided not only meat but also dairy products, draft power, and manure for fertilizing crops. The combination of cattle with crop production created a diversified agricultural system that was more resilient than operations focused on a single commodity. This diversification was a key strength of the region's agricultural economy, enabling farmers to weather fluctuations in commodity prices and weather conditions.

### The Modern Cattle Industry: Scale and Structure

The 2007 Census of Agriculture, the most recent data available for Tazewell County, documented a cattle industry of substantial scale and economic significance. The county had 576 farms with 153,677 acres in farmland, an increase from the 551 farms and 138,977 acres reported in 2002. The average size of farms had increased to 267 acres, reflecting a trend toward consolidation that would continue in subsequent decades. The expansion of farmland and farm size indicated that the agricultural sector was still a vital component of the county's economy, despite the challenges facing the industry.

The market value of agricultural products sold in Tazewell County was $21,490,000 in 2007, a 21% increase from the $17,733,000 reported in 2002. Of this total, crop sales accounted for only 5% ($1,013,000), while livestock sales represented 95% ($20,477,000). This overwhelming dominance of livestock production reflected the county's comparative advantage in cattle production, with the region's topography and climate ideally suited to livestock raising rather than crop production. The livestock industry was not merely the largest agricultural sector; it was the agricultural sector.

The average per-farm income from agricultural sales was $37,308 in 2007, an increase from $32,182 in 2002. This income, while modest by national standards, represented a significant contribution to the county's economy, providing employment and income for hundreds of farm families. Government payments to farmers, however, had declined from $252,000 in 2002 to $137,000 in 2007, reflecting changes in federal agricultural policy. The average payment for farms receiving subsidies had fallen from $4,585 to $1,690, indicating a shift away from commodity support programs.

The cattle industry in Tazewell County is characterized by a mix of cow-calf operations, backgrounding operations, and finishing operations. Cow-calf operations, which maintain breeding herds and produce calves for sale, are the most common type of cattle operation in the region. Backgrounding operations, which grow calves to a weight suitable for finishing, are also present, as are finishing operations that raise cattle to slaughter weight. The diversity of operation types reflects the range of production systems employed by the county's farmers.

The genetics of the region's cattle herds have been shaped by decades of selective breeding, with producers focusing on traits that enhance profitability in the region's production environment. Beef cattle breeds such as Angus, Hereford, and Simmental are common in the region, with crossbreeding used to combine the desirable traits of multiple breeds. The emphasis on quality genetics has enabled the region's producers to maintain the reputation for quality beef that has characterized the county's cattle industry for generations.

### Federal Support Programs for Livestock Producers

The federal government provides substantial support to livestock producers in Tazewell County through a range of programs administered by the U.S. Department of Agriculture's Farm Service Agency (FSA). In 2024, Tazewell County farmers received $230,362 in Non-insured Disaster Assistance, $13,721 through the Conservation Reserve Program, and $5,047 in Dairy Programs. These programs provide a vital safety net for farmers facing the uncertainties of weather, markets, and production conditions that are inherent to agricultural operations.

The Non-insured Disaster Assistance Program (NAP) is designed to provide financial assistance to producers of crops for which federal crop insurance is not available. The $230,362 paid to Tazewell County farmers in 2024 indicates the importance of this program to the region's agricultural sector, as many of the crops grown in the county are not eligible for traditional crop insurance. The NAP program provides coverage for losses caused by natural disasters, including drought, excessive moisture, hail, wind, frost, and other weather-related events that are common in the Appalachian region.

The Conservation Reserve Program (CRP), which provided $13,721 to Tazewell County farmers in 2024, is designed to encourage farmers to convert environmentally sensitive cropland to conservation uses. The CRP provides annual rental payments and cost-share assistance to farmers who establish long-term conservation covers on eligible land. In the context of Tazewell County, the CRP supports the conversion of marginal cropland to grass or forest cover, enhancing wildlife habitat and improving water quality while reducing soil erosion.

The Dairy Programs, which provided $5,047 to Tazewell County producers in 2024, include the Dairy Margin Coverage program and the Dairy Indemnity Payment Program. These programs provide a safety net for dairy producers, protecting them against fluctuations in milk prices and feed costs. The relatively small amount of dairy program payments reflects the modest scale of the county's dairy industry, which has been declining in importance relative to beef cattle production.

In 2024, Tazewell County also received $501 through the Total Emergency Relief Program, which provides assistance to producers who have suffered losses due to natural disasters. The small amount of emergency relief funding reflects the relative stability of the county's agricultural sector during that year, though subsequent years would bring more significant challenges. The emergency relief programs provide a critical safety net for farmers facing the devastating losses that can result from severe weather events.

### The 2026 Drought Designation and Its Impact

The 2026 growing season brought severe drought conditions to Tazewell County, leading to a primary natural disaster designation from the U.S. Department of Agriculture. The designation was triggered by a drought intensity value of D2 (Severe) for eight or more consecutive weeks, or D3 (Extreme) or D4 (Exceptional) drought conditions, according to the U.S. Drought Monitor. The designation made Tazewell County farmers eligible for emergency loans and other forms of federal assistance, providing a critical lifeline for agricultural operations threatened by the drought.

The impact of the drought on Tazewell County's cattle industry was significant and multifaceted. Pasture conditions deteriorated rapidly as rainfall deficits accumulated, reducing the availability of forage for grazing livestock. Hay production, which is essential for winter feeding, was substantially reduced, creating a shortage of feed that would continue into the following year. Cattle producers were forced to reduce herd sizes or purchase expensive supplemental feed, both of which had significant economic consequences.

The drought designation extended beyond Tazewell County to include contiguous counties that were also affected by the dry conditions. Bland, Buchanan, Russell, and Smyth counties in Virginia, along with McDowell and Mercer counties in West Virginia, were designated as contiguous areas eligible for drought assistance. This regional designation reflected the widespread nature of the drought, which affected a large portion of the Appalachian region and created economic hardship for farmers across multiple states.

The response to the drought designation included the mobilization of federal resources to support affected farmers. The FSA's emergency loan program provided low-interest loans to producers who had suffered losses from the drought, enabling them to replace essential equipment or livestock, reorganize their farming operations, or refinance certain debts. The loans were made available with the application deadline set for March 1, 2027, providing farmers with time to assess their losses and develop recovery plans.

The drought of 2026 was not an isolated event but part of a pattern of increasing weather variability that is affecting agricultural production across the Appalachian region. The combination of spring frost and freeze events followed by severe drought, as noted by Congressman Morgan Griffith, reflected the growing challenges facing the region's farmers. The increasing frequency of extreme weather events has made agricultural production more uncertain and more risky, requiring farmers to adapt their management practices to the changing climate.

### State-Level Support for the Cattle Industry

The Virginia state government has demonstrated a strong commitment to supporting the cattle industry, recognizing its importance to the Commonwealth's economy and rural communities. Governor Abigail Spanberger hosted a roundtable with cattlemen, farmers, and producers at Copenhaver Brothers Farm in Washington County in June 2026, as part of Virginia Agriculture Week. The roundtable provided a forum for farmers to voice their concerns about recent extreme weather, high fertilizer costs, and damaging federal trade policies that are hurting Virginia farms.

The governor's roundtable was part of a broader effort to highlight the strength of Virginia's cattle and dairy industries during Agriculture Week. Governor Spanberger also joined local dairymen to tour Highland Dairy in Washington County, highlighting the major economic contributions of Virginia's dairy industry and presenting a proclamation celebrating Virginia Dairy Month. These events underscored the importance of the cattle and dairy sectors to the state's agricultural economy.

The Farm & Forest Prosperity Plan, which was signed into law during the 2026 legislative session, represents a significant commitment to the future of Virginia's agricultural and forestry industries. The legislation, which was highlighted by Governor Spanberger during the Agriculture Week events, directs the Secretary of Agriculture and Forestry to develop and implement Virginia's first-ever strategic roadmap for these industries. The Farm & Forest Prosperity Plan will provide a framework for strengthening agriculture and forestry across the Commonwealth, ensuring that these sectors remain vital components of Virginia's economy.

The Agriculture and Forestry industries have a combined economic impact of $105 billion annually and account for more than 490,000 jobs across the Commonwealth, according to state officials. This substantial economic contribution underscores the importance of supporting the cattle industry, which is a major component of the agricultural sector. The Governor's commitment to "listening to the voices of the men and women who drive Virginia's economy forward and making sure my Administration is providing them with the full support they deserve" reflects the political recognition of agriculture's importance.

The bipartisan nature of the legislation supporting agriculture indicates that agricultural policy remains an area of consensus in Virginia politics. The Farm & Forest Prosperity Plan and the other agricultural bills signed into law during the 2026 session received support from both parties, reflecting the recognition that agriculture is essential to the economic well-being of rural communities across the Commonwealth. This bipartisan support provides a stable policy environment for agricultural investment and development.

### Additional Federal Support and Disaster Recovery

The federal government has provided additional support to Virginia farmers through programs responding to specific disaster events. Hurricane Helene, which impacted Southwest Virginia in September 2024, led to substantial damage to agricultural operations in the region. In response, the USDA provided $60.9 million in Virginia Farm Recovery Block Grant funding to support farmers and timber landowners in the affected counties. Virginia was the first state to accept applications and begin disbursing payments from this block grant, demonstrating the state's commitment to rapid disaster recovery.

Tazewell County was among the counties eligible for the Hurricane Helene block grant funding, along with Bland, Buchanan, Carroll, Craig, Dickenson, Floyd, Giles, Grayson, Lee, Montgomery, Patrick, Pittsylvania, Pulaski, Russell, Scott, Smyth, Washington, Wise, Wythe, and several cities. The funding was designed to cover costs not covered by other government assistance programs, providing a comprehensive recovery package for affected farmers.

The availability of emergency loans through the FSA's physical loss loan program provided another avenue for recovery from natural disasters. These loans can help producers repair or replace damaged or destroyed physical property essential to the success of the agricultural operation, including livestock losses, essential farm buildings, fixtures to real estate, equipment, perennial crops, fruit and nut bearing trees, and harvested or stored crops and hay. The loans are available at low interest rates and can be used to meet various recovery needs.

The USDA's disaster assistance programs include a range of tools to help farmers recover from natural disasters. The Disaster Assistance Discovery Tool, Disaster Assistance-at-a-Glance fact sheet, and Loan Assistance Tool can help producers determine program or loan options. Farmers can also contact their local USDA Service Center to file a Notice of Loss or to ask questions about available programs. These resources provide critical support for farmers navigating the complex process of disaster recovery.

The availability of federal support for disaster recovery reflects the recognition that agricultural production is inherently risky and that farmers cannot bear the full burden of weather-related losses. The federal programs provide a social safety net that enables farmers to continue producing food and fiber even in the face of significant weather events. For Tazewell County's cattle producers, these programs provide essential support that enables them to recover from the drought and continue their operations.

# Chapter Eight: Pillar Two - Advanced Agriculture and Food Manufacturing

## Section 8.1: The Cattle and Livestock Industry - A Comprehensive Strategy for Expansion

### The Strategic Imperative for Cattle Industry Growth

The expansion of Tazewell County's cattle and livestock industry represents one of the most promising opportunities for economic diversification in the coalfield region, building on a heritage that spans more than two centuries and leveraging the natural advantages that have made the county a cattle-producing powerhouse. The 2022 USDA Census of Agriculture documented 500 farms working 123,581 acres in Tazewell County, with cattle sales totaling $20.6 million annually, representing 89% of all agricultural sales in the county . This overwhelming dominance of livestock production reflects the region's comparative advantage in cattle raising, with the limestone soils and mountain pastures providing ideal conditions for beef production. The $20.6 million in annual cattle sales represents not merely an economic statistic but a foundation upon which a more diversified and resilient agricultural economy can be built.

The strategic importance of the cattle industry extends well beyond its direct economic contribution, encompassing the preservation of the region's cultural heritage, the maintenance of the open landscapes that define the county's character, and the provision of employment and income for hundreds of farm families. The 500 farms in Tazewell County represent not just economic units but family enterprises that have been passed down through generations, embodying a way of life that is central to the region's identity . The preservation and expansion of the cattle industry is thus not merely an economic development strategy but a cultural preservation strategy, ensuring that the agricultural heritage of the region continues to thrive in the twenty-first century.

The expansion strategy for Tazewell County's cattle industry must be comprehensive, addressing the full spectrum of challenges and opportunities facing the sector. The strategy must address the processing bottleneck that forces cattle to travel far distances for slaughter, the aging of the farm population that threatens the continuity of agricultural production, the workforce development needs that require investment in training and education, the infrastructure requirements that support efficient production and marketing, and the environmental challenges that demand sustainable management practices. The strategy must also leverage the natural advantages of the region, including its climate, topography, and agricultural heritage, to create a competitive advantage that cannot be easily replicated elsewhere.

The cattle industry expansion strategy must be integrated with the broader economic development strategy for the region, recognizing that agriculture is not an isolated sector but a component of a diversified economy that includes manufacturing, energy production, tourism, and services. The integration of the cattle industry with the food processing sector, the renewable energy sector, and the tourism sector creates opportunities for synergies that enhance the competitiveness of each sector. The cattle industry provides the raw material for food processing, the manure that can be used for biogas production, and the pastoral landscapes that attract tourists. The strategic integration of the cattle industry with other sectors is essential to maximizing its economic impact.

The expansion of the cattle industry must also be environmentally sustainable, ensuring that the natural resources upon which the industry depends are preserved for future generations. The region's streams, forests, and grasslands are not merely scenic amenities; they are essential components of the agricultural ecosystem that supports cattle production. The adoption of sustainable grazing practices, the protection of riparian areas, and the conservation of soil resources are all essential to the long-term viability of the cattle industry. The integration of conservation practices with production goals is essential to creating a cattle industry that can thrive for generations to come.

### The Processing Bottleneck: A Critical Infrastructure Gap

The most significant challenge facing Tazewell County's cattle industry is the lack of local meat processing capacity, a bottleneck that forces cattle to travel long distances for slaughter and reduces the profitability of local operations. Tazewell County has approximately 27,500 cattle that must currently travel far from the county to be processed, incurring transportation costs and stress on the animals that reduce the quality of the beef . This processing bottleneck was exposed during the COVID-19 pandemic, when the concentration of processing capacity in a few large facilities created severe disruptions in the supply chain, with farmers having to wait a year or more for a processing slot . The experience of the pandemic demonstrated the vulnerability of the cattle industry to disruptions in the processing sector and highlighted the need for local processing capacity.

The response to this challenge has been the development of Project Ponderosa, a $2 million county-owned meat processing facility at the Bluestone Industrial Park in Springville, funded through grants from the Virginia Tobacco Commission and the Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority . The facility, which will be leased to Blue Ridge Butchery at a discounted rate, will provide a 7,500-square-foot processing plant with two lines for processing beef, pork, lamb, and poultry . The facility is expected to bring approximately 15 new jobs to the area and will provide a local processing option for the county's cattle producers, reducing transportation costs and enabling them to capture more of the value chain .

The Project Ponderosa strategy is supported by a substantial funding commitment from state and regional economic development agencies. The Virginia Tobacco Commission has awarded a $350,000 grant for the project, in addition to an earlier $800,000 loan approved by the Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority . The Tobacco Commission grant will support 50% of new land acquisition and construction costs, with additional funding still being sought from other state agencies . The Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority loan includes a provision that approximately one-fourth of the loan is forgivable if at least five full-time jobs are averaged over the ten-year loan term, providing an incentive for job creation . The total $2 million investment in the facility represents a significant commitment to addressing the processing bottleneck.

The economic impact of the processing facility will extend well beyond the 15 direct jobs it creates. The facility will provide a market for local cattle that currently must be shipped out of the county, reducing transportation costs and enabling producers to capture more of the value of their cattle. The facility will also provide an opportunity for value-added processing, including the production of branded beef products that can command premium prices in retail markets. The increased demand for local processing services will support the expansion of the county's cattle herd, as producers will have confidence that there is a local market for their animals . The processing facility thus serves as a catalyst for the growth of the entire cattle industry.

The processing facility also supports the development of a local meat brand that can differentiate Tazewell County beef in the marketplace. The Blue Ridge Butchery, which will operate the facility, has experience in producing high-quality meat products that can be marketed to consumers seeking locally produced, humanely raised beef . The branding of Tazewell County beef can leverage the region's reputation for quality livestock production, creating a premium product that generates higher returns for producers. The development of a local brand is an essential component of the strategy to capture more of the value chain and increase the profitability of the cattle industry.

### The Seven Hills Food Co. Model: Full Carcass Utilization and Value-Added Processing

The expansion strategy for Tazewell County's cattle industry can draw on the innovative model developed by Seven Hills Food Co., a Lynchburg-based beef processor that has created a comprehensive system for maximizing the value of locally raised cattle . Seven Hills' model is based on the principle of full carcass utilization, ensuring that every part of the animal is used for high-value products rather than being discarded or ground into low-value hamburger. The company facilitates over 100 value-added product combinations for local family farms, enabling them to access markets and capture returns that would otherwise be unavailable .

The Seven Hills model addresses a critical gap in the local beef economy: the lack of commercially viable beef processing that can supply the growing demand for locally produced meat. The company's founder, Ryan Ford, started a butcher shop in Charlottesville and quickly realized that the core issue in a local beef economy is the lack of processing capacity, as even his boutique butcher shop could not be consistently supplied by the average local beef processor . The renovation of an abandoned 100-year-old beef plant created the processing capacity that was needed to support local beef production, and the company has since expanded to provide sophisticated processing services to local farmers.

The Seven Hills model includes a suite of value-added services that go far beyond basic slaughter and fabrication. The company partners with skincare, jerky, snack stick, deli, dog food, and leather processors to help local farmers use all parts of their own cattle, creating significant newfound margin opportunities . For example, rather than wasting beef tallow, farmers can reach new markets through their own branded skincare products, with the potential to profit an extra $1,000 or more per head . The company also functions as a marketing service for farmers, providing custom packaging, labeling, branding, and barcoding that allows them to walk into a supermarket and compete at the highest level.

The economic logic of the Seven Hills model is compelling. Farmers typically miss out on hundreds or thousands of dollars in added value potential on each animal simply because they do not have access to retail grade, high-value products that use what the average processor may discard or grind . By providing access to these value-added products, Seven Hills enables farmers to capture more of the consumer dollar, increasing the profitability of their operations. The company's business model is intentionally designed to work in harmony with the farming community, ensuring that farmers make more money so that they bring more cattle to process, creating a virtuous cycle of growth and profitability .

The application of the Seven Hills model to Tazewell County's cattle industry would provide a significant boost to the profitability of local producers. The processing facility at Bluestone Industrial Park could be developed to include value-added processing capabilities, enabling local farmers to access the markets for beef tallow skincare products, jerky, snack sticks, and other high-value products . The partnership with Blue Ridge Butchery could be expanded to include the development of branded products that leverage the region's reputation for quality beef. The integration of full carcass utilization into the processing strategy would create new revenue streams for producers and enhance the overall profitability of the cattle industry.

The success of the Seven Hills model demonstrates the potential for value-added processing to transform the economics of cattle production in the region. The company's Virginia Beef Co. brand is sold in every Kroger and Harris Teeter in Virginia, providing a distribution channel for locally produced beef that reaches a broad consumer base . The development of similar branded products for Tazewell County would provide a market for local beef that extends well beyond the county, generating returns that support the expansion of the cattle industry. The value-added processing model is thus an essential component of the strategy to build a resilient and profitable cattle industry.

### The National Context: Opportunities and Challenges

The national context for cattle industry expansion is favorable, with beef demand strong and cattle numbers at historically low levels, creating opportunities for producers who can increase their herds. The domestic beef herd is at a 75-year low, while consumer demand for beef has grown, creating a favorable supply-demand balance that supports high prices for cattle . The reasons for the decline in cattle numbers include drought, an aging population of ranchers, and regulatory pressures that have made it more difficult to maintain herds . These factors have created a supply shortage that is expected to persist for several years, providing an opportunity for producers who can expand their operations.

The U.S. Department of Agriculture has recognized the need to strengthen the domestic beef industry and has developed a plan to address the challenges facing producers. The plan, released by Agriculture Secretary Brooke Rollins in October 2025, aims to boost the domestic beef herd, stabilize prices, cut regulations, and increase grazing access . Among the initiatives are the enforcement of truth in labeling guidelines and changes to the beginning farmer definition that will enhance subsidies for risk management tools . The plan also seeks to expand grazing on federal lands, particularly with the 10% of grazing allotments currently vacant, providing additional forage resources for cattle producers .

The reaction of the industry to the USDA plan has been cautiously optimistic, with producers expressing support for the plan's objectives while emphasizing the importance of letting the market set itself, controlled by supply and demand . Brandon Reeves, executive director of the Virginia Cattlemen's Association, praised the enforcement of truth in labeling guidelines and changes to the beginning farmer definition but expressed uncertainty about whether the plan will have a dramatic impact . The sentiment from producers is that the plan contains many positives, but it will take cooperation with Congress to change some of the regulations, and it will take time for everything in the plan to work .

The national focus on strengthening the beef industry provides a supportive policy environment for Tazewell County's cattle industry expansion. The USDA plan's emphasis on reducing regulations and increasing grazing access aligns with the needs of the region's cattle producers, who have faced challenges from both regulatory pressures and the loss of grazing land. The plan's focus on increasing domestic cattle numbers supports the expansion of the herd in the region, while the provisions to aid young farmers and rural communities address the demographic challenges facing the industry . The policy environment is thus aligned with the strategic objectives of the region's cattle industry.

The national supply-demand dynamics also create opportunities for local processing capacity. The concentration of processing capacity in a few large facilities has created vulnerabilities in the supply chain, as demonstrated during the pandemic, and there is growing recognition of the need for more distributed processing capacity . The development of local processing facilities like the one in Tazewell County addresses this need, providing processing capacity that is more resilient to disruptions and more responsive to local needs. The national context thus supports the development of local processing infrastructure.

### The Strategic Role of Virginia Tech and Research Institutions

The expansion of Tazewell County's cattle industry can be supported by the research and extension capabilities of Virginia Tech and other institutions, which have made significant investments in livestock research and education. Virginia Tech's College of Agriculture and Life Sciences has invested in a range of livestock research facilities, including the Applied Reproduction Facility, the Bovine Extension, Teaching and Research Facility, and the Metabolite Research Laboratory at Kentland Farm . These facilities support research on animal reproduction, nutrition, and health, providing the scientific foundation for improved cattle production practices.

The strategic significance of Virginia Tech's research capabilities for Tazewell County's cattle industry is substantial. The research on improved breeding techniques, nutrition, and health management can be translated into practices that increase the productivity and profitability of the region's cattle operations. The extension services that Virginia Tech provides can disseminate these practices to producers, ensuring that the benefits of research are realized on the ground. The partnership between Virginia Tech and the region's cattle producers can create a pipeline of innovation that drives continuous improvement in the industry.

Virginia Tech has also been involved in the development of the Tazewell County meat processing facility, with a previous agricultural strategic plan completed by the Virginia Tech Office of Economic Development identifying the need for a federally inspected meat processing facility in the region . This involvement demonstrates the university's commitment to supporting the region's agricultural development and its recognition of the importance of processing infrastructure to the industry's growth. The university's expertise in agricultural economics, food science, and rural development can support the successful implementation of the processing facility.

The Cattle Bus Tour, organized by Virginia State University Extension, provides a model for how research, production, processing, and marketing can be integrated to support the cattle industry . The tour, which visits a meat processing facility, a research-based agroforestry grazing management site (silvopasture), a diversified dairy and beef operation with a working creamery, and an active livestock auction market, demonstrates how research can be translated into practice . The tour provides practical insights into value-added opportunities and regional agricultural infrastructure, offering a model for the kind of educational programming that can support the expansion of Tazewell County's cattle industry.

The partnership between research institutions and the cattle industry is essential to building a sustainable and competitive industry. The research on sustainable grazing practices, animal health, and value-added processing can improve the productivity and profitability of the industry, while the extension services can ensure that the benefits of research are realized on the ground. The collaboration between Virginia Tech, Virginia State University, and the region's cattle producers can create a culture of innovation that drives continuous improvement in the industry.

### Workforce Development and the Aging Farm Population

The aging of the farm population is a significant challenge facing Tazewell County's cattle industry, with many farmers nearing retirement and insufficient numbers of young people entering the industry. The national trend of an aging farm population is reflected in the county, where the average age of farmers has been increasing and where fewer young people are choosing farming as a career.
