The non-local customers generated $2,143.65 in revenue at the Castlewood location, exceeding the $770.95 generated by local customers . This suggests that the canneries serve a broader region and are valued by customers from beyond the county.

The sustainability of the community canneries will require either increased revenue or subsidies to cover the operating losses. The county government, which operates the canneries, may need to consider the value of the services provided and the benefits to the community in deciding whether to continue operating the canneries. The canneries provide a valuable service to the agricultural community, enabling small-scale producers to preserve their products and access markets. The financial losses may be justified by the economic and social benefits that the canneries provide.

### Small Business Development in Food Manufacturing

The Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority provides support for small businesses in the food manufacturing sector through its Seed Capital Matching Grant program, which provides dollar-for-dollar matching grants up to $10,000 for start-ups and small businesses . The program is designed to encourage entrepreneurship and economic diversification in the region, providing the seed capital that new businesses need to get started. The program is open to for-profit businesses in qualifying categories with less than 10 full-time employees, and applicants must work with the Small Business Development Centers at Mountain Empire and Southwest Virginia community colleges to prepare their applications .

The success of the Seed Capital Matching Grant program is demonstrated by the recent grant awarded to PBall's Smoke & Grill, a mobile food truck operating in Russell County . The business, which received a $10,000 grant from VCEDA, will serve tourists and locals alike, providing food options in the community . The business projects two full-time employees and nine part-time employees within five years, demonstrating the job creation potential of small businesses in the food sector . The grant supported the purchase of the truck and equipment needed to start the business, enabling the entrepreneur to turn his vision into reality.

The Small Business Development Centers that support the program provide technical assistance to entrepreneurs, helping them to develop the business and financial plans needed to succeed. The partnership between VCEDA and the Small Business Development Centers ensures that entrepreneurs have access to the expertise and resources they need to start and grow their businesses. The centers provide training in business planning, financial management, and marketing, enabling entrepreneurs to build the skills needed to succeed.

The expansion of small-scale food manufacturing in the region can be supported by the VCEDA Seed Capital Matching Grant program, which provides the early-stage funding that is often difficult to secure. The program provides matching grants of up to $10,000, enabling entrepreneurs to leverage their own resources and reduce the financial risk of starting a business. The program is designed to support businesses in qualifying categories, including food processing, specialty food manufacturing, and other food-related businesses. The program is available to businesses in Buchanan, Dickenson, Lee, Russell, Scott, Tazewell, and Wise counties, and the City of Norton .

The development of a food manufacturing sector in Tazewell County will require a range of support services, including business planning, technical assistance, and access to capital. The VCEDA Seed Capital Matching Grant program provides the early-stage funding, while the Small Business Development Centers provide the technical assistance. The development of a food manufacturing incubator could provide shared facilities and services for food entrepreneurs, reducing the barriers to entry and enabling them to test and scale their businesses. The incubator could include shared commercial kitchens, processing equipment, and cold storage, providing the infrastructure that small businesses need to succeed.

### Incentives for Food Manufacturing Companies

The development of the food manufacturing sector in Tazewell County can be supported by a range of state and regional incentive programs, providing financial support for companies seeking to establish or expand operations in the region. The Virginia Tobacco Commission provides grants for economic development projects in the coalfield region, including food manufacturing projects, as demonstrated by the $350,000 grant for the Blue Ridge Butchery facility . The Commission funds projects that enhance economic growth across 40 localities in Southern and Southwest Virginia, including Tazewell County .

The Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority provides loans for economic development projects in the region, including the $800,000 loan for the Blue Ridge Butchery facility . VCEDA provides low-interest loans to for-profit basic employers and industrial development authorities, as well as seed capital matching grants for start-ups and small businesses, and loans and/or grants for renewable energy projects . The loan for the Blue Ridge Butchery facility includes a provision that approximately one-fourth of the loan is forgivable if at least five full-time jobs are averaged over the ten-year loan term, providing an incentive for job creation .

The Governor's Agriculture and Forestry Industries Development (AFID) Fund provides a discretionary, performance-based economic development incentive specifically for agriculture and forestry value-added or processing projects . The AFID program supports agribusinesses of all sizes, including dairy processors, meat and poultry processors, specialty food and beverage manufacturers, and greenhouse operations . Industries such as meat processing have been identified as target growth opportunities for support by the AFID program, giving these applications priority . The grant is awarded to a political subdivision for the benefit of the company and is critical to the success of the new or expanding facility .

The incentive programs available for food manufacturing companies provide a financial support package that can significantly reduce the cost of establishing or expanding operations in the region. The combination of Tobacco Commission grants, VCEDA loans, and AFID grants can provide substantial financial support for food manufacturing projects, reducing the risk and improving the return on investment. The availability of these programs should be actively marketed to food manufacturing companies considering expansion in the region, highlighting the financial support available.

The green food manufacturing concept, which emphasizes sustainability and environmental stewardship, is particularly well-suited to the region's agricultural economy. The production of value-added food products from locally sourced ingredients reduces transportation miles and supports the local agricultural economy. The use of renewable energy, energy-efficient technologies, and sustainable practices can further reduce the environmental impact of food manufacturing. The development of green food manufacturing can be supported by the incentive programs that emphasize sustainability, including the renewable energy programs offered by VCEDA .

### Green Food Manufacturing: Opportunities and Incentives

The concept of green food manufacturing offers significant opportunities for the Tazewell County region, combining the economic benefits of value-added food production with the environmental benefits of sustainable practices. The region's agricultural base provides the raw materials for green food manufacturing, including the dairy products, meat, and produce that can be processed into value-added products. The development of green food manufacturing facilities can create jobs, generate economic activity, and enhance the region's reputation as a leader in sustainable food production.

The integration of green food manufacturing with the region's dairy sector offers particularly promising opportunities. The goat milk produced by Bates Family Farm and other dairies in the region can be processed into a range of value-added products, including cheeses, yogurts, and other dairy products. The development of green food manufacturing facilities that process goat milk into value-added products would create jobs and generate economic activity, while the use of sustainable practices would reduce the environmental impact of the operations. The proximity of the dairy operations to the processing facilities would reduce transportation costs and support the local agricultural economy.

The USDA's Organic Dairy Marketing Assistance Program (ODMAP) provides financial assistance to certified organic dairy operations, helping them mitigate market volatility, higher input and transportation costs, and unstable feed supply and prices . The program provides a one-time cost-share payment based on marketing costs on pounds of organic milk marketed in the previous calendar year . In 2024, USDA provided more than $31 million in ODMAP payments, including an additional $8.7 million in a second round of payments . This program provides direct financial support to organic dairy producers, helping them maintain their operations and continue producing organic milk.

The Climate-Smart Commodities Market Program, administered by the Conservation Innovation Fund with a $25 million investment from USDA, helps small and underserved farms implement climate-smart practices that generate Scope 3 supply chain environmental outcomes for food and beverage companies . The program works with farms to facilitate the integration of climate-smart practices into beef, dairy, and grain supply chains, with a focus on reducing greenhouse gas emissions . The resulting GHG reductions meet stringent corporate sustainability reporting requirements, and the reductions are third-party verified and validated .

The Climate-Smart Commodities Market Program represents a significant opportunity for Tazewell County farms to generate additional revenue through the adoption of climate-smart practices. The program provides upfront financing for long-term contracts for climate assets, enabling farms to implement practices such as cover crops, no-till, rotational grazing, nutrient management, and other sustainable practices . The farms enrolled in the program's first round span across Virginia, Pennsylvania, Maryland, and New York, and include women- and veteran-owned farms, underserved producers, and small and family-owned farms . The program dramatically decreases risk for food companies seeking to reduce their Scope 3 emissions by providing upfront capital to farmers .

### The Conservation Innovation Fund Model

The Conservation Innovation Fund (CIF) provides a model for how green food manufacturing can be financed and developed, creating water, carbon, and biodiversity assets that align public and corporate sustainability agendas with regenerative agriculture producers and practices . The CIF creates a blended capital pool to expand market-based solutions to address pressing conservation challenges, combining public and philanthropic grant makers with mission-oriented investors . The CIF's Climate-Smart Commodities Market Program is a collaborative effort that prioritizes the implementation of new agricultural practices, providing upfront financing for long-term contracts for climate assets .

The CIF model has been demonstrated through its partnerships with farms in the region, including Zimmerman's HillTop Dairy in Danville, Pennsylvania . The dairy farm received resources and partnership that enabled the farm to improve soil health, demonstrating the benefits of the program for individual farms. The CIF works with farms enrolled in the program to ensure they are successful in meeting their sustainability goals, providing the support and resources needed to implement new practices . The program is open to eligible farms now, with the second round of enrollment currently underway .

The application of the CIF model to Tazewell County's agricultural sector could provide significant benefits for the region, enabling farms to implement climate-smart practices and generate additional revenue through carbon credits and other environmental assets. The program could support the adoption of rotational grazing practices on cattle farms, the implementation of cover crops and no-till on crop farms, and the adoption of nutrient management practices on dairy farms. The benefits of these practices would extend beyond the farms to include improved water quality, enhanced soil health, and reduced greenhouse gas emissions.

The partnership between CIF and food and beverage companies provides a market for the environmental assets generated by climate-smart agricultural practices. The companies are seeking to reduce their Scope 3 emissions, and the Climate-Smart Commodities Market Program provides the supply chain environmental outcomes they need . The companies are willing to pay for the emissions reductions, providing a revenue stream for the farms that implement the practices. The program thus creates a market for sustainability, aligning the economic interests of farms and food companies with the broader environmental goals of reducing greenhouse gas emissions.

### The Future of Food Manufacturing in Tazewell County

The future of food manufacturing in Tazewell County is bright, with the Blue Ridge Butchery facility providing the anchor for a growing food processing sector. The facility will provide local processing capacity for the region's livestock, reducing transportation costs and enabling producers to capture more of the value chain. The availability of local processing capacity will encourage the expansion of the livestock industry, as producers will have confidence that there is a local market for their animals. The facility will also attract other food processing businesses to the region, creating a cluster of food manufacturing that supports the agricultural economy.

The expansion of food manufacturing will require continued investment in the workforce, with training programs that prepare workers for jobs in the processing sector. The Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority's workforce training programs, which provide support for training workers in the region's industries, can be adapted to support the food manufacturing sector. The training programs should focus on the skills needed for meat processing, food safety, and quality control, preparing workers for the jobs that the processing facilities will create. The partnership between the community colleges, the Small Business Development Centers, and the food manufacturing companies will be essential to building a skilled workforce.

The development of a local meat brand will be an essential component of the food manufacturing strategy, enabling the region's producers to differentiate their products and command premium prices. The Blue Ridge Butchery facility provides the processing capacity for branded products, and the region's reputation for quality livestock production provides the foundation for a premium brand. The development of the brand will require investment in marketing and promotion, as well as the development of distribution channels that connect the region's products with consumers seeking locally produced meat.

The integration of green food manufacturing practices will further enhance the region's competitive position, appealing to consumers and companies seeking sustainable food products. The adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices, the use of renewable energy in processing facilities, and the implementation of sustainable waste management practices will all contribute to the green food manufacturing strategy. The Climate-Smart Commodities Market Program and other sustainability programs will provide the financial support for the adoption of these practices, ensuring that the economic and environmental benefits are realized.

The food manufacturing sector will be a pillar of Tazewell County's diversified economy, providing jobs, economic activity, and resilience for the agricultural sector. The Blue Ridge Butchery facility, the community canneries, and the small food businesses that are being developed represent a growing food manufacturing sector that supports the region's agricultural heritage while embracing innovation and sustainability. The future of food manufacturing in the region is bright, and its growth is central to the broader economic revitalization of the Tazewell County region.

# Chapter Eight: Pillar Two - Advanced Agriculture and Food Manufacturing

## Section 8.5: Agritourism and Diversification - A Comprehensive Strategy for Growth

### The Strategic Imperative for Agritourism

The diversification of Tazewell County's agricultural economy through agritourism represents one of the most promising opportunities for creating sustainable income streams that complement traditional farming operations. The concept of agritourism—inviting the public onto working farms for recreation, education, and direct sales of farm products—has grown significantly across the United States as consumers seek authentic experiences and a connection to the sources of their food. For farmers in the Tazewell County region, agritourism offers a means of generating income that is less dependent on commodity prices and weather conditions, providing a buffer against the inherent volatility of agricultural production.

The integration of agritourism with traditional farming creates a diversified business model that enhances the resilience of farm operations. As Sabrina Fleenor, co-owner of Fleenor Farms, noted, "There's no guarantee in anything agriculturally related, so that's something that we want to make sure that our farm has income coming in every single month" . This recognition of the need for diversified income streams reflects the reality of modern farming, where commodity prices, weather conditions, and market forces create uncertainty that threatens the viability of operations that rely on a single source of income.

The strategic value of agritourism extends beyond the direct income it generates, encompassing the marketing benefits of building a brand and developing relationships with consumers. Farms that engage in agritourism develop a loyal customer base that returns year after year, providing a stable market for farm products that extends well beyond the seasonal tourist season. The direct connection between farmers and consumers builds trust and understanding, creating a market for farm products that is less vulnerable to the price fluctuations that characterize commodity markets. Agritourism thus serves both an economic and a marketing function, strengthening the farm business through multiple channels.

The economic impact of agritourism extends beyond the individual farm to the broader community, as visitors to farms also patronize local restaurants, hotels, and shops. The Fleenor family's agritourism operation in Washington County draws visitors from both Southwest Virginia and Northeast Tennessee, creating economic activity that supports the broader hospitality sector . The integration of agritourism with regional tourism initiatives, such as the promotion of the Back of the Dragon motorcycle route, creates a network of attractions that collectively draw visitors to the region, enhancing the economic impact of each individual attraction.

### Fleenor Farms: A Model of Agritourism Success

Fleenor Farms in Washington County, Virginia, represents one of the most successful models of agritourism in the region, demonstrating how a family farm can transform its operations to include seasonal entertainment while maintaining its traditional agricultural production. The farm, owned and operated by Keith and Sabrina Fleenor along with their two children, is a generational farming operation that sells beef, pork, lamb, and goat at the Bristol State Street Farmer's Market, with a history of direct meat sales spanning over 23 years . This commitment to direct sales provided a foundation for the expansion into agritourism, building a customer base that was already familiar with the farm's products.

The Fleenor family's journey into agritourism began eight years ago when they decided to turn their farm into a fall experience, creating a seasonal attraction that draws visitors from across the region . The corn maze, which is the initial draw for many visitors, covers approximately five and a half acres and is designed to provide an engaging experience for families. The hayride around the farm takes visitors around the entirety of the corn maze, providing views of the mountains, other farms, and a glimpse into the maze itself . The combination of the corn maze, hayride, and other activities creates a comprehensive experience that occupies visitors for several hours, encouraging them to spend money on farm products and activities.

The pumpkin patch is another popular attraction at Fleenor Farms, with the family partnering with a neighboring farm on Rich Valley Road for pumpkin supply . The pumpkins are already cut and placed in the field, eliminating the need for visitors to worry about "getting little stickers in your fingers or tripping over pumpkin vines" . This attention to the visitor experience reflects the family's understanding that agritourism is a hospitality business, where the quality of the visitor experience determines whether guests will return in the future. The farm also offers activities such as corn shooting, a corn pit for children, and opportunities to feed farm animals, creating a range of activities that appeal to visitors of all ages .

The diversification of Fleenor Farms extends beyond seasonal entertainment to include a range of value-added meat products that distinguish the farm in the marketplace. The farm offers over 20 different flavors of bratwurst, which have become a niche product for the family . The bratwurst flavors are described as putting "a Southwest Virginia and Northeast Tennessee spin" on the traditional sausage, reflecting the farm's commitment to local flavors and ingredients . The variety of bratwurst flavors provides a product line that differentiates the farm from competitors, creating a unique selling proposition that generates customer loyalty and repeat business.

The success of Fleenor Farms is supported by the family's commitment to sustainable farming and community engagement, which is evident in the experiences they offer . The farm's iconic tobacco barn serves as a recognizable landmark, and the family's approachability and commitment to customer service create a welcoming atmosphere that encourages visitors to return. The farm's goal, as articulated by Sabrina Fleenor, is simple: "to create an affordable, fun experience for families and keep the love of farming alive" . This mission-driven approach to agritourism creates a connection with visitors that transcends the commercial transaction, building a community of supporters who are invested in the farm's success.

The Fleenor family's expansion into meat processing through the acquisition of Washington County Meat Packing and Livestock, LLC in 2020 represents a strategic integration of the value chain . This acquisition allows the family to control the processing of their meat products, ensuring quality and enabling the development of branded products like their signature bratwurst. The vertical integration of production and processing creates additional revenue streams and reduces the vulnerability of the business to disruptions in the processing sector. The acquisition also supports local agriculture by providing processing capacity for other farmers in the region, creating a hub of agricultural activity that benefits the broader community.

### The Back of the Dragon: Adventure Tourism as Economic Catalyst

The Back of the Dragon represents one of the most significant economic development initiatives in Tazewell County, transforming a scenic mountain road into a tourism destination that draws visitors from across the country. The route, which follows State Route 16 from Tazewell to Marion, Virginia, features 438 curves and 3,500 feet of elevation change over 32 miles of mountain terrain . The road's challenging driving conditions have attracted motorcyclists and sports car enthusiasts seeking the kind of thrilling experience that enthusiasts seek, transforming a rural highway into a globally recognized destination.

The economic impact of the Back of the Dragon extends well beyond the direct spending of tourists, encompassing the revitalization of downtown Tazewell and the development of businesses that cater to visitors. The Back of the Dragon Brewery, located on Main Street in Tazewell, serves as a gathering place for riders and a hub of activity during events like the Old Town Revival rally . The brewery offers a range of merchandise and memorabilia, providing visitors with souvenirs that promote the destination. The Bearded Dragon, a store and coffee shop adjacent to the brewery, offers apparel, souvenirs, and other merchandise that capitalizes on the Back of the Dragon brand .

The Old Town Revival, a weekend-long rally held annually in June, represents the culmination of the Back of the Dragon's economic impact. The event features cruise-ins, stunt shows, live music, derby races, Harley-Davidson giveaways, dyno testing, tattoo artists, and vendors, creating a festival atmosphere that draws visitors from across the region . The event's slogan, "Ride 'Em or Drive 'Em," reflects the inclusive nature of the gathering, welcoming all brands and types of vehicles . The economic impact of the event is substantial, with visitors filling hotels, restaurants, and shops throughout the weekend.

The integration of agriculture with the Back of the Dragon tourism creates opportunities for synergy between the two sectors. The farm-to-table restaurant at the Bearded Dragon, Dragon Fired Pizza Kitchen, provides a market for local agricultural products, supporting the region's farmers while providing visitors with an authentic taste of the region . The honey-infused specialties at Bee Noodle'd, a downtown restaurant, showcase local ingredients like honey from Thompson Valley Bee Farm, demonstrating the connection between tourism and agriculture . This integration creates a virtuous cycle, with tourism supporting agriculture and agriculture enhancing the tourism experience.

The Back of the Dragon also serves as a gateway to other recreational opportunities in the region, including the Spearhead Trails system, which offers off-road adventures for ATV and side-by-side enthusiasts . Tazewell County is home to two Spearhead Trails systems, the Original Pocahontas and the Jawbone Jeep Trail, offering trails ranging from family-friendly green routes to challenging black trails . The combination of on-road and off-road recreational opportunities creates a comprehensive adventure tourism destination that appeals to a wide range of visitors, extending the economic impact of the Back of the Dragon across a broader season.

### Burke's Garden: A Unique Agritourism Asset

Burke's Garden, located in Tazewell County, represents a unique agritourism asset that could be further developed to enhance the region's tourism offerings. The Garden lies in a massive bowl formed by a mountain collapsing in on itself over time, creating a depression surrounded by mountain ridges that has led to the Garden being known as "God's Thumbprint" . This unique geology, combined with the area's status as the highest valley in Virginia, creates a landscape of exceptional beauty that draws visitors seeking scenic views and outdoor recreation.

The agricultural heritage of Burke's Garden is preserved in its working farms and the country store that serves the community. The Burke's Garden General Store, located at 6988 Burkes Garden Rd, Tazewell, VA 24651, provides a link to the agricultural economy that has sustained the community for generations . The store serves as a gathering place for residents and visitors alike, offering local products and a connection to the community's agricultural heritage. The Burke's Garden Farms operation provides another resource for visitors, offering farm products and a glimpse into the region's agricultural production .

The recreational opportunities in Burke's Garden are substantial, with the area serving as a segment of the Appalachian Trail, where hikers enjoy the journey to the Chestnut Knob Shelter . The mix of woodland, grassland, and marsh provides habitat for a wide variety of birds, with 218 species reported to date . More elusive species such as bobolink and loggerhead shrike have been reported intermingled amongst more common eastern meadowlark, and grasshopper and song sparrows . The birding opportunities, combined with the scenic drives and hiking trails, create a comprehensive outdoor recreation experience that appeals to nature enthusiasts.

The development of Burke's Garden as a more comprehensive agritourism destination could build on the area's natural assets and agricultural heritage. The expansion of farm-to-table dining experiences, the development of farm stays and accommodations, and the creation of guided tours that showcase the area's unique geology and agricultural production could enhance the visitor experience and extend the economic impact of tourism in the region. The proximity of Burke's Garden to the Back of the Dragon route provides an opportunity for visitors to combine the two experiences, creating a comprehensive Tazewell County itinerary that appeals to a diverse range of interests.

### The Modern Local Store Network: A Successor to the Company Store

The development of local store networks in the Tazewell County region represents a modern successor to the Pocahontas Colliery Store model, transforming the historical legacy of the company store into a contemporary economic opportunity. The Pocahontas Colliery Store, established in about 1883, functioned as an instrument of economic control in the coal camps, with miners paid in scrip that could only be used at the company store . The passage of New Deal legislation curbed some of the worst abuses, but the company town and store system did not end entirely until the second half of the twentieth century with the decline of coal .

The modern local store network in the region serves a fundamentally different purpose, connecting farmers and artisans directly with consumers through retail outlets that showcase local products. The Back of the Dragon Adventure Center, with its 5,000 square feet of retail and lounge space, provides a venue for the sale of locally produced merchandise and agricultural products . The store offers apparel, souvenirs, and other merchandise that promotes the destination, creating a market for local products that might otherwise be unavailable to visitors. The merchandise at the Bearded Dragon includes a range of items that promote the Back of the Dragon brand, providing visitors with souvenirs that extend the economic impact of their visit .

The integration of local agricultural products into the retail network creates a market for farmers and artisans that builds on the region's agricultural heritage. The farm-to-table restaurants in downtown Tazewell source ingredients from local farms, creating a demand for local agricultural products that supports the region's farmers . The Bee Noodle'd restaurant, with its bee-themed decor and honey-infused specialties, sources honey from local apiaries, showcasing the region's agricultural production . The Thompson Valley Bee Farm in Tazewell County provides a source of locally produced honey that supports the restaurant's offerings .

The Abingdon farmers market provides another component of the local store network, connecting farmers directly with consumers and creating a market for locally produced food. The market, which operates weekly during the growing season, is a gathering place for the community and a resource for visitors seeking fresh, locally produced food. The market supports the region's farmers by providing a direct sales channel that eliminates the need for intermediaries, enabling farmers to capture more of the consumer dollar. The integration of the farmers market with the broader tourism sector creates a market for local food that extends beyond the local community to include visitors.

The Southwest Virginia Cultural Center & Marketplace in Abingdon provides a venue for the sale of authentic crafts, music, food, and wine, with products juried under strict standards . The center offers one-of-a-kind jewelry, pottery, quilts, and handcrafted musical instruments, providing a market for artisans from throughout the region. The Holston Mountain Artisans cooperative, one of the oldest craft cooperatives in the United States, offers a wide selection of finely-made gift items, decorations for the home, musical instruments, and local artwork . These retail venues create a network of outlets for local products that builds on the region's cultural heritage while providing economic opportunities for artisans and farmers.

### Farm-to-Table Dining: A Growing Movement

The farm-to-table dining movement in the Tazewell County region has grown significantly in recent years, with restaurants and chefs committed to sourcing ingredients from local farms and producers. Abingdon, located just to the southwest of Tazewell County, was recently named one of the Top Small Town Food Scenes in the country by USA Today Travel, with 34 independently owned restaurants in a town of just over 8,000 people . According to Eater.com, Abingdon has more restaurants per capita than San Francisco, New York, or New Orleans, a testament to the vibrancy of the town's culinary scene .

The "Rooted in Appalachia" logo designates restaurants that have committed to using local produce, purchased directly from the farmer . This certification program provides consumers with assurance that the food they are eating is locally sourced, supporting the region's agricultural economy. The Tavern, which serves fine dining inside Abingdon's oldest building, constructed in 1779, offers locally sourced cuisine in a historic setting . Sisters at The Martha offers a sophisticated menu and a cozy, pub-like atmosphere, with ingredients sourced from local farms .

The Harvest Table Restaurant in Meadowview, operated by renowned author Barbara Kingsolver and her husband Steven Hopp, represents the pinnacle of the farm-to-table movement in the region . Inspired by their best-selling memoir Animal, Vegetable, Miracle, the restaurant offers locally sourced cuisine and artisanal goods in their General Store. In addition to the produce grown on its own Harvest Table Farm, the restaurant offers products from over 50 regional farmers, gardeners, and ranchers . This commitment to local sourcing creates a market for agricultural products that extends well beyond the restaurant itself, supporting a network of producers throughout the region.

The farm-to-table dining experience in the region extends beyond restaurants to include special events that showcase local ingredients and producers. Kelly Ridge Farms in Meadowview hosts farm-to-table suppers prepared by Abingdon Chef Sarah Soto, featuring ingredients sourced from local and regional farmers and food producers . Each course is paired with locally sourced Tumbling Creek Ciders, and at least 70% of ingredients are sourced locally . These events provide a unique dining experience that connects consumers with the sources of their food, building appreciation for local agriculture and creating a market for local products.

The Nicewonder Farm & Vineyards in Bristol, Virginia, represents the integration of farm-to-table dining with agritourism, offering a comprehensive experience that includes dining, accommodations, and recreation. Hickory, the farm-to-table restaurant at Nicewonder, sources ingredients from the estate's farm and vineyard, offering a menu that celebrates Appalachian flavors and local hospitality . The restaurant's signature dishes include scratch-made biscuits, homemade pimento cheese, and seasonal chef-inspired twists on traditional Appalachian cuisine . The integration of dining with accommodations and recreation creates a comprehensive agritourism experience that appeals to visitors seeking a deeper connection with the region's agricultural heritage.

### Bee Keeping and Local Honey Production

The bee keeping industry in Tazewell County provides a valuable source of local honey and other bee products, supporting both agricultural production and the broader ecosystem. Thompson Valley Bee Farm, located at 571 Fernwood Dr, Tazewell, VA 24651, offers honey products and serves as a resource for local bee keepers . The farm is described by visitors as "all that is good in nature," reflecting the appreciation of the community for the services that bee keepers provide . The honey produced by the farm is used in local restaurants, including Bee Noodle'd in downtown Tazewell, creating a market for local honey that supports the apiary industry .

The bee keeping industry in the region plays an important role in supporting agricultural production, as bees are essential pollinators for many crops. The pollination services that bees provide enhance the productivity of farms, increasing yields and improving the quality of agricultural products. The presence of bee keepers in the region is thus essential to the viability of the agricultural sector, providing services that are often overlooked but are fundamental to agricultural production.

The integration of bee keeping with agritourism creates opportunities for educational programming and direct sales. Visitors to farms and apiaries can learn about the importance of bees to the ecosystem, the process of honey production, and the challenges facing bee populations. The direct sale of honey and other bee products to visitors provides a revenue stream for bee keepers, supporting the industry and encouraging the expansion of bee keeping in the region.

The honey produced in Tazewell County is valued for its quality and its connection to the region's agricultural landscape. The honey reflects the diversity of the region's flora, with the flavor and character of the honey varying with the seasons and the plants in bloom. The local honey is prized by consumers who appreciate the connection to the region's agricultural heritage and the environmental benefits of supporting local bee keepers.

### Challenges and Opportunities in Agritourism

The development of agritourism in Tazewell County faces challenges that must be addressed through strategic planning and investment. The weather vulnerability of outdoor attractions creates uncertainty for farmers who have invested in agritourism infrastructure, as demonstrated by the 2024 drought that affected Fleenor Farms. Sabrina Fleenor noted that "we were mainly worried about how it was going to affect our livestock production because of the grass, the forage" . The drought also threatened the farm's corn maze and sunflower fields, creating the risk that the fall attraction would be reduced or canceled . This weather vulnerability requires farmers to develop contingency plans and diversify their agritourism offerings to reduce the risk of weather-related disruptions.

The seasonality of agritourism presents another challenge, with many farms relying on the fall season for a significant portion of their agritourism revenue. Fleenor Farms, for example, relies heavily on the fall season to keep its business growing through festivities and local sales . The concentration of revenue in a single season creates financial risk, as a poor season or a weather-related disruption can significantly affect the farm's annual income. Farmers are developing strategies to extend their agritourism offerings across a longer season, including spring and summer events that appeal to visitors during the warmer months.

The workforce requirements of agritourism operations present another challenge, with farms needing to staff seasonal attractions while maintaining their agricultural production. The Fleenor family, for example, manages their farm operations while also running the agritourism activities, requiring careful time management and a flexible workforce. The development of a workforce that can support agritourism operations, including training in customer service and hospitality, is essential to the growth of the sector.

The marketing of agritourism opportunities presents both challenges and opportunities for the region. The Back of the Dragon brand has been successful in attracting visitors to the region, and the integration of agritourism with this brand creates opportunities for cross-promotion . The marketing of farm-to-table dining experiences, farm stays, and agritourism activities through regional tourism organizations can build awareness and attract visitors who might not otherwise visit the region. The use of digital marketing, including social media and online booking platforms, is essential to reaching potential visitors and building a customer base.

### The Future of Agritourism in Tazewell County

The future of agritourism in Tazewell County is bright, with the Back of the Dragon attraction providing a foundation for the development of a broader tourism sector that incorporates agriculture, recreation, and cultural experiences. The integration of the Back of the Dragon with agritourism, farm-to-table dining, and local craft markets creates a comprehensive visitor experience that appeals to a diverse range of travelers. The continued development of this integrated tourism sector will require investment in infrastructure, workforce development, and marketing.

The expansion of agritourism offerings in the region will require the development of additional farm-based attractions that appeal to visitors seeking authentic experiences. The development of farm stays and accommodations, the expansion of farm-to-table dining experiences, and the creation of educational programming that showcases the region's agricultural heritage are all opportunities for growth. The collaboration between farms, tourism organizations, and local government will be essential to the successful development of these offerings.

The integration of agritourism with renewable energy production and environmental stewardship creates opportunities for synergy between the economic and environmental objectives of the region. The use of renewable energy in agritourism operations, the implementation of sustainable agricultural practices, and the restoration of natural habitats all contribute to the region's environmental sustainability while enhancing the visitor experience. The marketing of the region as a destination for sustainable tourism can attract visitors who value environmental stewardship and authentic experiences.

The workforce development needs of the agritourism sector require attention to training and education, ensuring that workers have the skills needed to provide high-quality visitor experiences. The training programs should include customer service, hospitality, and food safety, as well as the agricultural skills needed to maintain the attractions. The partnership between community colleges, extension services, and agritourism operators can create a pipeline of trained workers who can enter the sector with the skills needed to succeed.

The agritourism sector will be a pillar of Tazewell County's diversified economy, providing income for farmers, creating jobs in the hospitality sector, and attracting visitors who support the broader community. The Back of the Dragon, Fleenor Farms, the farm-to-table restaurants, and the local craft markets represent a growing agritourism sector that builds on the region's agricultural heritage while embracing innovation and sustainability. The future of agritourism in the region is bright, and its growth is central to the broader economic revitalization of the Tazewell County region.

# Chapter Eight: Pillar Two - Advanced Agriculture and Food Manufacturing (Continued)

## Section 8.6: Expanding Food Production with Frozen Food Manufacturing and Packaging

### The Strategic Opportunity in Frozen Food Manufacturing

The expansion of Tazewell County's agricultural production into frozen food manufacturing and packaging represents one of the most promising opportunities for capturing value from the region's livestock and agricultural production while creating jobs and economic activity that extends well beyond the primary production sector. The frozen food market is substantial and growing, with dinners and entrées representing the largest segment of frozen food sales, exceeding $5.9 billion annually in supermarkets alone, with the average American consuming a frozen meal approximately six times per month . The Tazewell County region, with its growing meat processing capacity at the Blue Ridge Butchery facility, its agricultural production base, and its strategic location along the US 19 corridor, is well-positioned to capture a share of this market through the development of a frozen food manufacturing sector.

The strategic value of frozen food manufacturing lies in its ability to transform raw agricultural products into value-added goods that command premium prices and have extended shelf lives. A whole carcass that might sell for $1.50 per pound as a commodity can be transformed into portion-controlled frozen meals that sell for $4 to $6 per pound, capturing significantly more value for the region's producers . The frozen food manufacturing sector provides a market for the full range of cuts from each animal, including the lower-value cuts that are difficult to sell fresh but can be incorporated into value-added products. The sector thus supports the full utilization of each animal, maximizing the economic return from the region's livestock production.

The development of a frozen food manufacturing sector in Tazewell County builds on the investments already made in meat processing infrastructure, including the $2 million facility at the Bluestone Industrial Park that will provide two processing lines for beef, pork, lamb, and poultry . The 7,500-square-foot facility, which has received support from the Virginia Tobacco Commission, the Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority, and other funders, provides the foundation for a value-added processing industry that can transform the region's agricultural production into branded frozen food products . The facility's federal inspection status will allow products to be sold across state lines, providing access to national markets .

The economic impact of frozen food manufacturing extends beyond the direct jobs created, encompassing the broader economic benefits of food processing and distribution. The frozen food packaging market is substantial, with North America representing a significant share of the global market that is expected to grow through 2034 . The development of frozen food manufacturing in Tazewell County would create demand for packaging materials, logistics services, and distribution infrastructure, creating jobs and economic activity throughout the supply chain. The sector would also support the region's agricultural producers by providing a stable market for their products, reducing the vulnerability of the agricultural sector to commodity price fluctuations.

### The Hungry Man Concept: Portion Sizes for the Modern Consumer

The concept of a "Hungry Man" meal, popularized by the frozen food industry's iconic brand, represents a market opportunity that Tazewell County's food processing sector can tap into with locally sourced, premium quality ingredients. Swanson launched its Hungry-Man line in 1972, marketing it to customers seeking larger portions, and by 1987, Swanson was selling 150 million frozen dinners annually . The brand's marketing emphasized portion sizes, with products such as the Double Meat Bowls line marketed as "Hungry-Man XL" . The appeal of these larger portion meals reflects a consumer segment seeking substantial, satisfying meals rather than the smaller portions that often characterize diet-oriented frozen food products.

The Hungry Man concept's appeal extends beyond portion size to include the meal's ability to satisfy the appetite of working consumers seeking a quick, substantial meal that doesn't require preparation. The typical Hungry Man consumer includes families with children where the mother works part-time or full-time, with Pinnacle Foods estimating that 20% of all American households consume Hungry Man meals annually . This demographic represents a substantial market opportunity for frozen meals that are convenient, satisfying, and of high quality, a market that Tazewell County's food processing sector can serve with locally sourced ingredients.

The evolution of the Hungry Man brand reflects the broader trend in the frozen food industry toward larger portion sizes and heartier meals. Conagra's Hungry-Man brand continues to be a freezer case fixture with a comfort food positioning, even as other brands have moved toward more healthful fare . The Double Meat Bowls line includes Boneless Pork with BBQ Sauce, Chipotle Chicken with rice, and Smothered Salisbury Steak with mashed potatoes and onion and mushroom gravy . The availability of multiple SKUs demonstrates the variety within the large-portion frozen meal category and the opportunity for Tazewell County producers to develop their own product lines in this category.

The meat processing facility in Tazewell County will have two processing lines for beef, pork, lamb, and poultry, providing the capacity to produce portion-controlled cuts for frozen meal manufacturing . The facility's flexible design will allow for the production of multiple product lines, including the portion-controlled meats that form the center of frozen meals. The development of branded frozen meal products that combine locally sourced meats with the region's agricultural products would create a product line that leverages the region's strengths in livestock production and agricultural diversity. The frozen meal category offers a market that can absorb significant production volumes while generating premium prices.

### From Farm to Freezer: The Frozen Food Processing Value Chain

The development of a frozen food processing sector in Tazewell County requires the creation of a comprehensive value chain that moves agricultural products from the farm through processing, packaging, and distribution to the consumer. The value chain begins with the livestock production that has been central to the region's agricultural economy for centuries, with cattle, hogs, and poultry providing the raw materials for frozen meal production. The meat processing facility at the Bluestone Industrial Park provides the processing capacity to transform whole animals into portion-controlled cuts that are appropriate for frozen meal production. The facility's two processing lines  provide the flexibility to handle multiple species, enabling the production of a diverse range of meal products.

The frozen food packaging industry is a significant component of the food processing sector, with packaging playing a pivotal role in preserving the quality, safety, and marketability of frozen foods . The frozen food packaging market continues to evolve with advances in modified atmosphere packaging (MAP), high-pressure processing (HPP), active and intelligent packaging systems, and advanced insulation materials . These technologies are designed to improve product stability, extend shelf life, reduce energy consumption, and enhance supply-chain efficiency . The adoption of appropriate packaging technologies is essential to the success of frozen food manufacturing, as the packaging must protect the product during freezing, storage, and distribution.

The advances in frozen food packaging technology create opportunities for Tazewell County's food processing sector to differentiate its products through packaging innovation. Edible sensors that can detect defrosting events through color changes are among the emerging technologies that can guarantee that frozen food is handled properly throughout the supply chain . The adoption of smart packaging technologies can enhance the region's reputation for quality and innovation, creating a competitive advantage in the frozen food market. The packaging sector also offers opportunities for local businesses to supply packaging materials to the region's food processors, creating jobs and economic activity that complement the food processing sector.

The cold chain logistics that support frozen food processing represent another opportunity for economic development in Tazewell County. The storage and distribution of frozen foods require freezer storage facilities, refrigerated transportation, and cold chain management systems that ensure the product remains frozen from the point of production to the point of sale. The development of cold chain logistics infrastructure in the region would support the growth of the frozen food sector while also serving other industries, including the traditional food processing sector and the agricultural sector. The proximity of the US 19 corridor and Interstate 81 provides access to markets throughout the eastern United States, making Tazewell County an attractive location for cold chain logistics operations.

### The Mobile Meat Processing Model for Southern Virginia

The mobile meat processing model offers a complementary approach to the fixed facility being developed in Tazewell County, providing the flexibility to serve farmers who are located far from centralized processing facilities. The mobile harvest unit concept, developed by companies like Friesla, provides a solution to the processing bottleneck that has constrained the growth of the livestock industry in the region . The mobile units can be transported to farms, enabling on-farm slaughter that reduces stress on the animals and eliminates the need for long-distance transportation of live animals. The mobile units are designed to be fully self-contained, with all the equipment needed for slaughter and initial processing.

The combination of fixed and mobile processing capacity provides a comprehensive solution to the region's processing needs, with the fixed facility providing the capacity for high-volume processing and value-added manufacturing, while the mobile units serve farmers who are located far from the fixed facility. The mobile units can also be used to supplement the capacity of the fixed facility during peak demand periods, providing flexibility that a fixed facility alone cannot match. The availability of multiple processing options supports the growth of the livestock industry, as producers have confidence that there is a processing option available regardless of their location.

The business case for mobile meat processing is supported by the demand from livestock farmers for local processing services. A business plan for a value-added processing facility in the region found that the vast majority of livestock farmers are making specialized marketing claims about their practices, such as grass-fed or antibiotic-free, that could be leveraged to create higher price points and offset processing costs . The plan also found that a majority of farmers would be interested in finishing more animals on the farm if market opportunities existed . The mobile processing units provide a solution to the processing bottleneck that constrains the growth of the livestock industry.

The financial requirements for mobile meat processing are substantial but manageable, with the development of a processing facility requiring $300,000 to $500,000 in startup capital for equipment, building, and operating expenses . The equipment for a processing facility includes a processing line with a rail system, walk-in coolers for aging and storage, a walk-in freezer, and specialized processing machines for value-added products such as smoking and deli meat processing . The equipment can be purchased used in many cases, reducing the initial capital requirements. The operating costs of a mobile unit are lower than those of a fixed facility, as the mobile unit does not require a permanent building or the associated utilities.

### Large-Format Value-Added Processing Equipment Needs

The expansion of Tazewell County's food processing sector into frozen meal manufacturing requires a range of equipment that goes beyond the basic meat processing capabilities of the Blue Ridge Butchery facility. A full end-to-end plant may include harvest (slaughter), processing (cut and wrap), value-added or ready-to-eat goods production, and cold storage . The equipment required for value-added processing includes grinders, mixers, stuffers, smokehouses, cookers, and packaging equipment, as well as the freezers needed to freeze and store the finished products. The scale of the equipment investment depends on the volume of production and the range of products to be manufactured.

The planning of a meat processing facility requires careful attention to the product flow, with the facility layout designed to minimize congestion and ensure that the product moves efficiently from receiving through processing to packaging and storage. The Connecticut Department of Consumer Protection's regulations for frozen food plants require that the plant be arranged so that there is a proper flow of product, without undue congestion or backtracking, from the time raw materials are received until the frozen, packaged article is shipped from the plant . The regulations also require separate rooms for raw material storage, preparation operations, processing and packaging, and storage of inedible materials . The Virginia Department of Agriculture and Consumer Services imposes similar requirements, with provisions for water supply, employee facilities, steam supply, and waste disposal .

The equipment selection for a frozen food processing plant must consider both the current production needs and the potential for future expansion. The modular approach to facility development, pioneered by companies like Friesla, allows operators to start with the equipment needed for initial operations and add capacity as the business grows . This approach reduces the upfront capital investment and reduces the financial risk of overbuilding before the business has demonstrated its viability. The modular approach also provides the flexibility to add new product lines and expand operations as market opportunities emerge. The right-sized approach to equipment investment is more sustainable in the long run, as most meat processing businesses don't fail because they lack demand but because they overbuild before they're well down the road of returning on their investment .

The site infrastructure is a critical consideration for a frozen food processing plant, with requirements for water supply, wastewater treatment, and power that exceed those of a typical commercial building. The site must have access to potable water in sufficient volume for all plant operations, with both hot and cold water available . The wastewater system must be adequate to handle the processing waste, with options including septic systems, sewer connections, and evaporation lagoons . The power supply must be sufficient for the processing equipment and the refrigeration systems, with three-phase power often required for commercial-grade equipment . The site must also comply with local zoning and land use regulations, which may impose setback requirements and other restrictions.

### Development of Branded Hungry Man Style Meals

The development of branded frozen meals that appeal to the "hungry man" segment of the market represents a significant opportunity for Tazewell County's food processing sector, with the region's agricultural heritage and livestock production providing the foundation for premium quality products. The market for large-portion frozen meals is substantial, with legacy brands like Hungry-Man, Banquet, and Marie Callender's remaining freezer case fixtures . The category is characterized by comfort food positioning, with meal components that are familiar and satisfying rather than trendy or health-oriented. The success of these brands demonstrates the market demand for frozen meals that are convenient, satisfying, and of reasonable quality.

The differentiation of Tazewell County branded frozen meals can be achieved through the use of locally sourced, premium quality ingredients and the development of flavor profiles that reflect the region's culinary traditions. The meat processing facility at the Bluestone Industrial Park will produce portion-controlled cuts that can be the foundation of frozen meals . The agricultural production of the region can provide the vegetables, potatoes, and other components that complete the meal. The development of the "Back of the Dragon" brand, which has been successful in attracting tourists to the region, provides a template for the development of a food brand that leverages the region's unique identity and cultural heritage.

The production of branded frozen meals requires a range of value-added processing capabilities, including portion control, cooking, assembly, and packaging. The portion-control step ensures that each meal contains the correct amount of each component, with the size and shape of the cuts determined by the meal formulation. The cooking step may involve pre-cooking of the meat components, the vegetables, or the sauce, depending on the meal formulation. The assembly step involves combining the components in the correct proportions and arranging them in the packaging. The packaging step involves sealing the meal in a tray or pouch that is appropriate for freezing and heating.

The quality standards for frozen meals are established by the USDA, which requires inspections and certification for products that contain meat or poultry. The Blue Ridge Butchery facility will be federally inspected, enabling the production of USDA-certified products that can be sold across state lines . The federal inspection status is essential for the development of branded products, as the USDA inspection provides assurance to consumers that the products meet federal standards for safety and quality. The inspection also provides assurance to retailers that the products are produced in a facility that meets federal standards, reducing the risk of liability.

### Business Planning and Financial Analysis

The development of a frozen food manufacturing enterprise in Tazewell County requires a comprehensive business plan that addresses the financial, operational, and marketing aspects of the venture. A business plan for a value-added processing facility should include an analysis of the market opportunity, the competitive environment, the operational requirements, and the financial projections. The plan should also address the regulatory requirements, including USDA inspection and state and local regulations that apply to food processing facilities. The business plan for the Blue Ridge Butchery facility has been developed with support from the Virginia Tech Office of Economic Development, which identified the need for a federally inspected meat processing facility in the region .

The financial analysis of a frozen food manufacturing enterprise should consider the startup costs, the operating costs, the revenue projections, and the break-even analysis. The startup costs for a value-added processing facility can range from $300,000 to $500,000 for a basic processing line, with additional costs for specialized equipment, packaging, and regulatory compliance . The operating costs include labor, utilities, maintenance, and raw materials, with the cost of raw materials being the largest expense. The revenue projections should be based on realistic assumptions about production volume, product pricing, and market penetration.

The financial sustainability of a frozen food manufacturing enterprise depends on the ability to generate sufficient revenue to cover the operating costs and to provide a return on the investment. A basic assumption for a value-added processing facility is $1 million in sales, which would require substantial production volume and market penetration . The break-even analysis should identify the volume of production needed to cover the fixed costs of the operation, providing a target for the marketing and sales efforts. The financial projections should be updated regularly as the business develops, with adjustments made to the assumptions based on actual performance.

The funding for a frozen food manufacturing enterprise can come from a range of sources, including the Virginia Tobacco Commission, the Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority, USDA grants, and commercial lenders. The Tobacco Commission has provided $350,000 in grants for the Tazewell County project, with additional funding from other sources . The VCEDA has provided an $800,000 loan, with a portion of the loan forgivable if at least five full-time jobs are averaged over the ten-year loan term . The funding from these sources reduces the risk of the project and makes it more attractive to commercial lenders.

### Strategic Sales Plan for Frozen Food Products

The success of a frozen food manufacturing enterprise depends on the development of a comprehensive sales plan that identifies the target market, the distribution channels, and the marketing strategies for the products. The sales plan for the Tazewell County facility should target regional livestock farmers as the primary customers for the processing services, with the frozen food products sold through the farmers' sales channels or through wholesale and retail distribution . The sales plan should also consider the potential for developing a house brand that is sold directly to consumers or through regional retailers.

The marketing channels for frozen food products include farmers' markets, CSA programs, online stores, wholesale operations, and retail outlets. The direct-to-consumer channels, such as farmers' markets and CSA programs, provide the highest price point and the largest budget to cover processing costs . The wholesale channels, including distribution to regional retailers and food service establishments, provide access to a larger market but at lower margins. The online sales channel provides access to a national market but requires investment in e-commerce infrastructure and shipping logistics.

The sales strategy should leverage the branding of the "Back of the Dragon" and other regional attractions to create a connection between the food products and the region's unique identity. The farm-to-table restaurants that have developed in the region, including those in Abingdon and Tazewell, provide a channel for the sale of local food products and a demonstration of the quality of the region's agricultural production. The development of a regional food brand that is associated with quality, authenticity, and sustainability can differentiate Tazewell County products in the marketplace and command premium prices.

The strategic sales plan should also consider the development of value-added products that go beyond basic frozen meals, including specialty items such as smoked meats, sausages, and ready-to-eat products. The Blue Ridge Butchery facility has experience in producing high-quality meat products, and the development of specialty items can create additional revenue streams. The product diversification reduces the risk of the enterprise and provides opportunities for growth as the business expands.

### Quality Standards and Food Safety

The production of frozen food products requires adherence to stringent quality standards and food safety protocols, with the USDA and state regulatory agencies providing oversight and inspection. The Connecticut Department of Consumer Protection's regulations for frozen food plants require that the plant be designed to promote cleaning and sanitary maintenance, with separate rooms for raw material storage, preparation operations, processing and packaging, and storage of inedible materials . The regulations also require that the plant be located in areas reasonably free from objectionable odors, smoke, flying ash, and dust or other contamination . The Virginia Department of Agriculture and Consumer Services imposes similar requirements, with provisions for water supply, employee facilities, steam supply, and waste disposal .

The quality standards for frozen food products are established by the USDA and the FDA, with specific requirements for labeling, ingredient listing, and nutritional information. The products must be produced in a facility that meets federal inspection standards, with the inspection process ensuring that the products meet federal standards for safety and quality. The federal inspection process includes regular inspections of the facility, the equipment, and the products, with enforcement actions taken when violations are found. The inspection process is essential for ensuring the safety and quality of the frozen food products.

The food safety protocols for a frozen food processing plant should include a HACCP (Hazard Analysis and Critical Control Points) plan that identifies the potential hazards in the production process and the controls that are in place to mitigate those hazards. The HACCP plan should be developed with the support of a consultant who is familiar with the regulations and the best practices for frozen food processing. The HACCP plan must be implemented consistently, with regular monitoring and documentation to ensure that the controls are effective. The HACCP plan is the foundation of the food safety system for the facility.

The quality assurance program for the frozen food products should include testing and monitoring of the raw materials, the processing environment, and the finished products. The testing should include microbiological analysis to ensure that the products are free from pathogens, and sensory analysis to ensure that the products meet the quality standards for taste, texture, and appearance. The monitoring should include regular checks of the refrigeration systems, the packaging integrity, and the cleanliness of the facility. The quality assurance program is essential for maintaining the reputation of the brand and ensuring consumer satisfaction.

### Integration of Value-Added Processing with Farm Production

The integration of value-added processing with farm production creates a seamless supply chain that connects the farm to the consumer, enabling farmers to capture more of the value of their animals and to develop branded products that differentiate their operations. The Blue Ridge Butchery facility provides the processing capacity for value-added products, and the farmers in the region provide the raw materials. The integration of the two sectors creates a supply chain that is more resilient and more profitable than the traditional commodity marketing system.

The vertical integration of farm production with processing and marketing is essential for the development of branded products. A farmer who processes and markets his own beef can differentiate the product through claims about the production practices, the breed, and the region of origin. The differentiation enables the farmer to command premium prices, increasing the profitability of the operation. The vertical integration also provides the farmer with more control over the marketing of his products, reducing the vulnerability to commodity price fluctuations.

The integration of processing with farm production requires coordination and cooperation between the farmers and the processor. The processor needs to know the schedule for the farmers' livestock production to plan the processing runs, and the farmers need to be confident that the processor will be available when their animals are ready. The coordination can be facilitated through contracts and agreements that provide the framework for the relationship, ensuring that both parties meet their obligations.

The partnerships between farmers and processors can be supported by the economic development organizations in the region, including VCEDA and the Tobacco Commission. These organizations can provide funding and technical assistance for the development of processing facilities and the marketing of local products. The partnerships can also be supported by the extension services that provide training and education for farmers and processors.

### Workforce Development for Food Processing

The development of a frozen food manufacturing sector in Tazewell County requires a skilled workforce that can operate the processing equipment, manage the food safety program, and ensure the quality of the products. The workforce development needs include training in meat processing, food safety, quality assurance, and cold chain logistics. The training programs should be designed to meet the specific needs of the industry, with the involvement of the processors in the design and delivery of the training.

The workforce development programs can be provided by the community colleges, the extension services, and the industry associations in the region. The Virginia Tech Office of Economic Development, which completed the agricultural strategic plan that identified the need for a federally inspected meat processing facility in the region , can provide training and technical assistance for the development of the frozen food sector. The region's community colleges can provide vocational training in meat processing and food safety, while the extension services can provide training in business management and marketing.

The workforce challenges for the frozen food sector are significant, as the sector requires skilled labor that is often in short supply. The business plan for a value-added processing facility identified the need for access to skilled and reliable operators, and the development of cooperative models and leasing arrangements can provide access to the workforce that is needed . The workforce development programs should address the shortage of skilled labor by providing training and creating career pathways that attract workers to the sector.

The expansion of the frozen food sector provides employment opportunities for workers in the region, particularly those who have been displaced from the declining coal industry. The food processing sector offers stable employment at wages that are competitive with other industries in the region. The development of the sector can create a pipeline of workers who can move from entry-level positions to supervisory and management roles, creating career opportunities that contribute to the economic well-being of the community.

### Investment and Growth Strategy

The investment strategy for the expansion of Tazewell County's frozen food sector should focus on the development of the processing infrastructure, the cold chain logistics, and the marketing capabilities needed to build a competitive industry. The investment in processing infrastructure should include the Blue Ridge Butchery facility, the mobile processing units, and the value-added processing capabilities. The investment in cold chain logistics should include freezer storage facilities, refrigerated transportation, and the technology needed to manage the cold chain. The investment in marketing should include the development of a regional brand that identifies products from the Tazewell County region.

The growth strategy for the sector should include the expansion of the processing capacity, the development of new product lines, and the penetration of new markets. The expansion of processing capacity should be based on the demand for the products and the availability of raw materials. The development of new product lines should be based on market research and the identification of consumer trends. The penetration of new markets should be based on the development of distribution channels and the marketing of the products.

The funding for the investment and growth strategy can come from a combination of public and private sources, including the Virginia Tobacco Commission, VCEDA, USDA grants, and commercial lenders. The Tobacco Commission has provided $350,000 in grants for the Tazewell County project, with additional funding from other sources . The VCEDA has provided an $800,000 loan, with a portion of the loan forgivable if at least five full-time jobs are averaged over the ten-year loan term . The funding from these sources reduces the risk of the project and makes it more attractive to commercial lenders.

The economic impact of the frozen food sector is substantial, with the development of a $1 million processing facility generating significant economic activity and creating jobs. The business plan for a value-added processing facility estimated that $1 million in sales would create significant economic benefits for the region, including the creation of jobs, the generation of tax revenue, and the support of the agricultural sector . The development of a cluster of frozen food producers in Tazewell County would amplify these economic benefits, creating a sector that is a significant contributor to the regional economy.

### Conclusion: The Frozen Food Future

The expansion of Tazewell County's food production into frozen food manufacturing and packaging represents a strategic opportunity to capture more value from the region's agricultural production, create jobs, and build a more resilient and diversified economy. The $2 million investment in the Blue Ridge Butchery facility provides the processing capacity for value-added products, and the agricultural production of the region provides the raw materials. The integration of the two sectors creates a supply chain that is more profitable and more resilient than the traditional commodity marketing system.

The frozen food market is substantial and growing, with the average American consuming a frozen meal approximately six times per month . The market offers opportunities for the development of branded products that appeal to the "hungry man" segment, with larger portions and comfort food positioning that are consistent with the region's agricultural heritage. The development of branded products that leverage the "Back of the Dragon" brand and other regional identifiers can differentiate Tazewell County products in the marketplace and command premium prices.

The workforce development, infrastructure investment, and business planning required for the expansion of the frozen food sector are significant but achievable with the support of the region's economic development organizations. The Virginia Tobacco Commission, VCEDA, and other agencies have demonstrated a commitment to the development of the sector, providing funding and technical assistance that reduce the risk of the projects. The partnership between the public and private sectors is essential to the success of the frozen food sector, as the public sector provides the infrastructure and the private sector provides the entrepreneurship and the market knowledge.

The frozen food sector will be a pillar of Tazewell County's diversified economy, providing jobs, economic activity, and resilience for the agricultural sector.
