For the GRID-FLEXER project, Dominion Energy Virginia partnered with NREL to leverage the ARIES research platform for validating strategies for controlling large numbers of interconnected devices . Similar partnerships could be established for PSH projects in the region.

5. **Prepare for Award Negotiations**: If the application is selected for award negotiations, the implementor must be responsive during the negotiation process, providing requested documentation and meeting negotiation deadlines. The award negotiation process may take several months, and the implementor must designate a primary and a backup point-of-contact with whom DOE will communicate . Receipt of a notification letter selecting an application for award negotiations does not authorize the applicant to commence performance of the project; the applicant does not receive an award until award negotiations are complete and the Grants and Agreements Officer executes the funding agreement .

6. **Comply with Post-Award Requirements**: If an award is granted, the implementor must comply with all post-award requirements, including National and Administrative Policy Requirements, Build America Buy America requirements, Davis-Bacon Act requirements, and Environmental Review in accordance with the National Environmental Policy Act . The implementor must also comply with reporting requirements, which are identified on the Federal Assistance Reporting Checklist attached to the award agreement . Failure to comply with these requirements could result in the termination of the award.

### Securing Federal Grants: The Appalachian Regional Commission POWER Initiative

The ARC POWER Initiative is the most direct and accessible source of federal funding for the US 19 corridor's near-term economic development projects, providing grants that support workforce development, business attraction, and community revitalization. The POWER Initiative directs federal resources to economic diversification and revitalization projects in Appalachian communities affected by the downturn of the coal industry . The 2026 award package of $68.2 million represents the largest POWER investment since the initiative was launched in 2015.

The POWER Initiative supports a wide range of project types, including entrepreneurship development, workforce training, infrastructure investment, and business attraction. The initiative's flexibility makes it well-suited to the diverse needs of the US 19 corridor, where different communities may have different priorities and challenges. The projects funded by the POWER Initiative are expected to create jobs, train workers, and attract private investment, contributing to the economic diversification of coal-impacted communities.

ARC prioritizes POWER funding for projects that will produce economic opportunities tied to fostering entrepreneurial activities, developing industry clusters in communities, building a competitive workforce, and increasing access to broadband . Funding available through the competitive ARC POWER program is up to $100,000 for Planning awards, $400,000 to $2 million for Implementation awards, and up to $2.5 million for Broadband Implementation awards . The key dates for the fiscal year 2026 POWER application process include a May 22, 2026 deadline for required letters of intent (LOI), a July 8, 2026 deadline for grant applications, and awards announced in Winter 2027 .

**Steps for a Plan Implementor to Secure ARC POWER Grants**

1. **Register for Pathways**: All applicants must register for an account in Pathways, ARC's grant management system, before submitting an application . The implementor should complete this registration by May 15, 2026 to ensure access to the application portal . Registration is a required step in the application process, and applicants who do not register will not be able to submit their applications .

2. **Submit a Letter of Intent (LOI)**: A required LOI must be submitted by May 22, 2026 at 5:00 p.m. ET . The LOI must include a project summary that describes the project's goals, the target area, the expected outcomes, and the alignment with the POWER Initiative's priorities. The implementor should work with the applicable State Program Manager to ensure that the LOI is complete and competitive .

3. **Develop a Competitive Application**: The application must demonstrate how the project will create high-quality, in-demand jobs, attract new sources of private investment, and provide comprehensive workforce services and skills training . The implementor should include a detailed budget, a timeline, a description of the project team, and evidence of community support. The application should also include a plan for measuring and reporting on the project's outcomes.

4. **Engage with Regional Partners**: The POWER Initiative emphasizes collaboration and partnership. The implementor should engage with regional partners, including VCEDA, local governments, community colleges, and workforce development boards, to build a coalition that can support the application and implement the project . Letters of support from these partners should be included in the application.

5. **Prepare for Implementation**: If the application is awarded, the implementor must be prepared to begin implementation promptly. This includes establishing a project management structure, hiring staff, and beginning the activities outlined in the application. The implementor should also prepare for the reporting requirements associated with the grant, including regular progress reports and financial reports.

6. **Leverage Previous Success**: The region has already demonstrated success in securing ARC POWER funding, with Radford University receiving $97,472 for an AI workforce planning initiative and Southwest Virginia Community College receiving a $1.8 million grant to expand career and technical education programs. The implementor should leverage these successes to demonstrate the region's capacity to manage POWER grants effectively.

### Securing Federal Grants: Broadband and Digital Infrastructure

The expansion of broadband and cellular connectivity along the US 19 corridor is essential to the region's economic development, enabling businesses to participate in the digital economy, workers to access training and employment opportunities, and communities to attract investment. The federal government has made significant funding available for broadband expansion, with the Broadband Equity, Access, and Deployment (BEAD) program providing $42.45 billion nationally to expand high-speed internet access. Virginia has been allocated over $545 million in BEAD funding to connect the remaining 133,000 unserved locations across the Commonwealth .

The BEAD program is the primary federal funding mechanism for broadband expansion, providing grants to states for planning, infrastructure deployment, and adoption programs. Virginia's BEAD proposal was approved by the National Telecommunications and Information Administration (NTIA) in November 2025, paving the way for the release of over $545 million in federal funding . The funding will mobilize nearly $430 million in additional private investment, extending broadband infrastructure to over 133,000 homes, businesses, and community anchor institutions.

The Virginia Department of Housing and Community Development (DHCD) administers the BEAD program in Virginia, working with localities, broadband providers, and community organizations to ensure that funding is deployed effectively. The program includes a range of initiatives, including the Local Government Broadband Affordability and Adoption Planning Grants, which provide $25,000 to cities, counties, and tribal governments to conduct local needs assessments and develop broadband affordability and adoption plans. These planning grants help communities prepare for infrastructure deployment by identifying gaps and prioritizing investments.

The Appalachian Regional Commission also provides funding for broadband deployment in the region, with the ARC's grant programs supporting the extension of broadband service to areas currently unserved by a provider. The combination of BEAD and ARC funding provides significant resources for broadband expansion along the US 19 corridor.

**Steps for a Plan Implementor to Expand Broadband and Cellular Connectivity**

1. **Conduct a Broadband Needs Assessment**: The implementor should work with local governments, broadband providers, and community organizations to conduct a comprehensive needs assessment that identifies the locations that lack access to high-speed internet. The assessment should include both residential and business locations, as well as community anchor institutions like schools, libraries, and hospitals. The needs assessment should be completed in coordination with the local planning district commission (PDC) and should align with the state's broadband planning efforts.

2. **Secure Funding Through VATI and BEAD**: The Virginia Telecommunication Initiative (VATI) provides grants to localities partnered with internet service providers to finance the deployment of high-speed broadband to unserved locations . The implementor should work with local governments to apply for VATI and BEAD funding, partnering with internet service providers to develop competitive proposals. The implementor should also work with the Virginia Department of Housing and Community Development to ensure that applications are complete and competitive.

3. **Prepare for Broadband Deployment Challenges**: Broadband deployment projects face a range of challenges, including make-ready work (preparing existing electric poles for aerial network construction), supply chain issues, labor market constraints, railroad crossings, permitting for laying fiber in state-owned rights of way, and permitting to cross federally owned lands . The implementor should anticipate these challenges and develop mitigation strategies, including engaging with VDOT on permitting, working with railroad companies on crossings, and identifying alternative routes where needed.

4. **Leverage Private Investment**: The BEAD program requires significant matching funds from private and local sources, with $1.198 billion in private and local match funding leveraged for FY22, FY23, and FY24 VATI projects . The implementor should work with internet service providers to secure the necessary matching funds and to identify opportunities for private investment in broadband infrastructure.

5. **Develop a Broadband Adoption Strategy**: Expanding broadband infrastructure is only the first step; the region must also develop strategies to encourage broadband adoption among residents and businesses. The implementor should work with community organizations, libraries, and schools to develop digital literacy programs and to promote the benefits of broadband access.

6. **Monitor Progress and Report Outcomes**: The implementor should establish monitoring and reporting mechanisms to track the progress of broadband deployment projects and to report outcomes to funders and stakeholders. This includes tracking the number of locations connected, the speeds achieved, and the economic impact of the broadband access.

### Developing Incubator Space for Entrepreneurs

The development of incubator space for entrepreneurs is essential to fostering the innovation and small business growth that will diversify the US 19 corridor's economy. VCEDA has demonstrated its commitment to entrepreneurship through its Seed Capital Matching Fund program, which provides dollar-for-dollar matching grants up to $10,000 for start-ups and small businesses. The program has been highly successful, with a 2021 Virginia Tech study finding a very high return on investment ratio of 34:1 . Since the program was established in 2017, hundreds of new business startups have been assisted in the region.

The Seed Capital Matching Fund program is administered in partnership with the Small Business Development Centers at Mountain Empire and Southwest Virginia community colleges, which provide technical assistance to entrepreneurs . The program supports a wide range of businesses, including those in the food manufacturing, technology, and services sectors. The businesses assisted by the program have created hundreds of jobs and generated millions in private investment, with 230 businesses supported, 1,700 jobs projected, and $14 million in private investment .

The development of physical incubator space would complement the Seed Capital program, providing entrepreneurs with the shared facilities and services they need to start and grow their businesses. The "Well on Main" project in Clintwood, Virginia, which received VCEDA seed capital grant funding, provides a model for how incubator space can be developed in rural communities. The project demonstrates the potential for small-scale incubators to support entrepreneurship and job creation in communities of all sizes.

**Steps for a Plan Implementor to Develop Incubator Space**

1. **Identify Suitable Locations**: The implementor should identify existing buildings in the region's downtowns or industrial parks that could be repurposed for incubator space. The former Alcoa building in Lebanon, which has been repurposed for Tate's manufacturing operations, demonstrates the potential for repurposing existing facilities for new uses. The implementor should also consider the needs of different types of entrepreneurs, including those in food manufacturing, technology, and services, when selecting locations.

2. **Secure Funding for Incubator Development**: The implementor should identify potential funding sources for incubator development, including the VCEDA Seed Capital Matching Fund, the ARC POWER Initiative, and state and federal economic development programs. The implementor should also work with local governments and private foundations to secure the matching funds needed for these grant programs.

3. **Establish Partnerships with SBDCs**: The implementor should establish partnerships with the Small Business Development Centers at Mountain Empire and Southwest Virginia community colleges to provide technical assistance to incubator tenants . The SBDCs can provide business planning, market research, and financial analysis to entrepreneurs, increasing their chances of success.

4. **Develop Shared Facilities and Services**: The incubator should provide shared facilities and services that reduce the costs and barriers for entrepreneurs. This includes shared office space, meeting rooms, equipment, and administrative services. The incubator should also provide access to high-speed internet and other digital infrastructure that is essential for modern businesses.

5. **Create a Pipeline of Entrepreneurs**: The implementor should work with the SBDCs and other partners to create a pipeline of entrepreneurs who can utilize the incubator space. This includes outreach to potential entrepreneurs, training programs, and mentorship opportunities. The implementor should also connect entrepreneurs with potential customers, investors, and partners.

6. **Measure and Report Outcomes**: The implementor should establish metrics to measure the success of the incubator, including the number of businesses supported, the jobs created, and the private investment generated. This data should be reported to funders and stakeholders to demonstrate the impact of the incubator and to secure continued support.

### Workforce Training Programs

The success of the near-term actions depends on the availability of a skilled workforce, and the region has made significant investments in workforce development and training. The ARC POWER Initiative has provided substantial funding for workforce development in the region, with Southwest Virginia Community College receiving a $1.8 million grant to expand career and technical education programs with a focus on advanced manufacturing and trade-based skilled technician training. The programs are expected to serve 390 students and will be developed in collaboration with regional partners, including VCEDA and the Southwest Virginia Workforce Development Board.

The VCEDA workforce training grants have also supported workforce development in the region, with a $200,000 grant to the Mountain Empire Community College (MECC) Foundation for workforce development and training. The funds support future training and certification needs for residents in the VCEDA service territory for the next two years, meeting the ongoing demand for skilled, trained workers in high-need manufacturing, technology, and health care sectors. MECC anticipates training 500 or more students in 2025-2026, with existing industries expanding their employment offerings.

**Steps for a Plan Implementor to Develop Workforce Training Programs**

1. **Conduct a Workforce Needs Assessment**: The implementor should work with employers, community colleges, and workforce development boards to conduct a comprehensive assessment of the region's workforce needs. The assessment should identify the skills that are in demand, the gaps in the current workforce, and the training programs that are needed to fill those gaps.

2. **Secure Funding for Workforce Training**: The implementor should identify potential funding sources for workforce training, including the ARC POWER Initiative, the VCEDA workforce training grants, and the Virginia Talent Accelerator Program. The implementor should work with employers and training providers to develop competitive proposals for these funding sources.

3. **Develop Training Partnerships**: The implementor should establish partnerships with community colleges, four-year universities, and other training providers to develop and deliver training programs. The partnerships should include employers who can provide input on the skills that are needed and who can offer internships and job placements for graduates.

4. **Focus on In-Demand Industries**: The training programs should focus on the industries that are growing in the region, including advanced manufacturing, healthcare, information technology, and renewable energy. The implementor should work with employers to ensure that the training programs are aligned with their needs.

5. **Support Entrepreneurship**: The training programs should also support entrepreneurship, including training in business planning, marketing, and financial management. The implementor should work with the SBDCs to deliver this training, building on the success of the Seed Capital Matching Fund program.

6. **Measure and Report Outcomes**: The implementor should establish metrics to measure the success of the workforce training programs, including the number of workers trained, the certifications earned, and the jobs obtained. This data should be reported to funders and stakeholders to demonstrate the impact of the programs and to secure continued support.

### Conclusion: A Foundation for Transformation

The near-term actions outlined in this chapter represent the foundation for the US 19 corridor's economic transformation, creating the conditions for the larger investments and transformations that will follow in the medium and long term. The completion of feasibility studies for PSH sites will provide the technical foundation for large-scale energy storage development, while the securing of federal grants will provide the capital for implementation. The development of incubator space for entrepreneurs will foster innovation and job creation, while the expansion of broadband and cellular connectivity will enable participation in the digital economy.

The successful implementation of these near-term actions will require collaboration among a diverse set of stakeholders, including federal and state agencies, regional economic development organizations, local governments, businesses, educational institutions, and community organizations. VCEDA will play a central role in coordinating these efforts, leveraging its relationships with state and federal agencies and its deep understanding of the region's needs and opportunities. The ARC's POWER Initiative will provide critical funding and technical assistance, with its 2026 award package representing the largest investment in coal-impacted communities since the initiative's launch .

For a plan implementor, the key to success is to establish a centralized project management office that can coordinate the various workstreams, track progress against milestones, and serve as the primary point of contact for federal and state agencies. The implementor should also build strong partnerships with employers, training providers, and community organizations, and should develop a clear plan for measuring and reporting on the outcomes of the various initiatives. The near-term actions thus represent the essential first step in the region's journey toward a diversified, sustainable economic future, and the implementor's role is to ensure that this journey is well-planned, well-executed, and well-communicated.

# Chapter Eleven: Implementation Roadmap

## Section 11.2: Medium-Term Actions (3-5 Years) — Building the Infrastructure for Growth

### The Next Horizon: From Planning to Execution

Following the successful completion of the foundational near-term actions, the US 19 corridor enters a critical phase of implementation and construction that will transform the region's economic landscape. The medium-term actions, spanning years three through five of the implementation roadmap, represent the transition from planning to execution, with major capital projects breaking ground, manufacturing capacity expanding, and new industries taking root. These actions are designed to build on the feasibility studies, grant awards, and infrastructure investments that were initiated in the first two years, translating planning documents and funding commitments into tangible economic assets.

The medium-term actions are organized around four interconnected priorities: beginning construction of pumped storage hydropower facilities at selected sites, expanding food manufacturing capacity through the Blue Ridge Butchery Phase 2 project, establishing productive timber plantations on reclaimed mine lands, and developing industrial parks along the US 19 corridor. Each of these priorities addresses a critical gap in the region's economic infrastructure while creating jobs, generating economic activity, and building the foundation for long-term prosperity. The actions are designed to be sequential and cumulative, with the PSH construction providing energy infrastructure that supports manufacturing, the food processing expansion providing a market for agricultural production, the timber plantations creating a new industry on underutilized lands, and the industrial parks providing sites for new businesses.

The successful implementation of the medium-term actions will require continued collaboration among federal and state agencies, regional economic development organizations, local governments, businesses, and community organizations. The Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority (VCEDA) will play a central role in coordinating these efforts, building on the relationships and momentum established during the near-term phase. The Appalachian Regional Commission's POWER Initiative will continue to provide critical funding, with its 2026 award package of $68.2 million demonstrating the federal government's commitment to the region . The medium-term actions represent the most significant period of investment and construction in the region since the coal boom of the early twentieth century, and their success will determine the trajectory of the region's economic development for generations to come.

For a plan implementor, the medium-term phase requires a shift from planning and project management to construction and operations management. The implementor must establish construction management capabilities, coordinate with contractors and suppliers, and ensure that projects are completed on schedule and within budget. The implementor must also maintain the stakeholder engagement and communication channels that were established during the near-term phase, ensuring that communities are informed of progress and that any issues are identified and addressed promptly. The implementor's role is thus both strategic and operational, requiring the ability to manage complex construction projects while maintaining the vision and momentum that drive the region's transformation.

---

### Begin PSH Construction at Selected Sites: The Flagship Energy Project

The commencement of construction at the Tazewell County pumped storage hydropower (PSH) site represents the single most significant economic development project in the region's history, with an estimated cost of approximately $2 billion and a construction timeline of up to 10 years . The project, which has been in development since Dominion Energy filed a preliminary permit application with FERC in 2017, would create a facility capable of generating approximately 800 megawatts of power, functioning as a "giant battery" for the region's energy grid . The PSH facility would store energy by pumping water from a lower to an upper reservoir during periods of low demand, then releasing it through turbines to generate electricity during peak demand periods, providing grid stability and supporting the integration of renewable energy sources .

The Tazewell County PSH site offers ideal topographic conditions for pumped storage operations, with an elevation drop of approximately 1,000 feet between the upper and lower reservoirs . The site encompasses approximately 2,600 acres owned by Dominion Energy, which has completed extensive environmental, geological, archeological, and technical assessments . In 2018, the company conducted extensive core drilling at eight different sites and constructed a road on the top of the mountain to access the upper reservoir site . The company also hosted informational open house events in Tazewell and Bland Counties in July 2019 as part of the public consultation process required for FERC licensing .

The economic benefits of the PSH project are substantial, with a study by Chmura Economics & Analytics finding that the 10-year construction window could bring more than 2,000 jobs to Southwest Virginia . The study also estimated millions in tax revenue to the counties involved, with Tazewell County projected to receive approximately $2.5 million annually . The project would also create approximately 45 permanent jobs once operational, providing stable, high-quality employment for the region . The PSH facility would contribute to Virginia's clean energy goals, providing large-scale energy storage that can support the integration of renewable energy sources.

The PSH project faces significant challenges, including its $2 billion price tag and the decade-long construction timeline . Critics have raised concerns about the project's cost and its dependence on coal-based power sources, with the Natural Resources Defense Council characterizing the project as a "greenwashed pumped-storage pipedream" that would be "powered by an uneconomic, climate-changing coal plant" . However, the 2017 legislation that enabled the project requires that at least part of the energy used to operate the facility be generated by renewable resources in the area . The project's developers are also exploring the integration of renewable energy sources, including solar and wind, to power the facility's pumping operations.

The PSH construction will require significant coordination among multiple stakeholders, including Dominion Energy, FERC, state and local governments, and community organizations. The construction will also require the development of supporting infrastructure, including roads, transmission lines, and water supply systems. The implementor will need to work with Dominion Energy to ensure that the construction proceeds on schedule and that the community benefits that have been promised are delivered. The implementor will also need to manage the community impacts of the construction, including traffic, noise, and dust, ensuring that the community's concerns are addressed and that the project maintains public support.

**Steps for a Plan Implementor to Begin PSH Construction**

1. **Finalize FERC Licensing and Permitting**: The implementor should work with Dominion Energy to finalize the FERC licensing process, ensuring that all required environmental studies and public consultations are completed. The implementor should also coordinate with state and local agencies to secure the necessary permits for construction, including building permits, water permits, and environmental permits. The implementor should develop a comprehensive permitting timeline and ensure that all permits are secured before construction begins.

2. **Establish Construction Management Capabilities**: The implementor should establish a construction management team with expertise in large-scale infrastructure projects. The team should include project managers, engineers, safety officers, and community liaison personnel. The implementor should also establish relationships with contractors and suppliers, ensuring that the necessary resources are available for construction.

3. **Secure Financing and Manage Costs**: The implementor should work with Dominion Energy and other stakeholders to secure the necessary financing for the project, leveraging the federal grants that were secured during the near-term phase. The implementor should also establish cost management systems to track project costs and ensure that the project stays within budget. The implementor should develop contingency plans for cost overruns and schedule delays.

4. **Coordinate Workforce Development**: The implementor should work with training providers to develop programs that prepare workers for the jobs created by the PSH construction. The implementor should also establish hiring preferences for local workers, ensuring that the project's economic benefits are realized by the region's residents. The implementor should work with the Mountain Empire Community College (MECC) Foundation and other training providers to develop the workforce training programs needed for the project.

5. **Manage Community Impacts**: The implementor should develop a community impact management plan that addresses the potential impacts of the construction on the community, including traffic, noise, dust, and disruption to local businesses. The implementor should establish regular communication with the community, providing updates on construction progress and addressing concerns as they arise.

6. **Prepare for Operational Phase**: The implementor should begin planning for the operational phase of the PSH facility, including the recruitment and training of permanent staff and the development of operational procedures. The implementor should also begin planning for the integration of renewable energy sources, ensuring that the facility meets the legislative requirement that at least part of its power be derived from renewable resources in the region.

---

### Expand Food Manufacturing Capacity: Blue Ridge Butchery Phase 2

The expansion of food manufacturing capacity through the Blue Ridge Butchery Phase 2 project represents a critical component of the region's agricultural diversification strategy, building on the success of the initial processing facility at the Bluestone Industrial Park in Springville. The Phase 2 expansion would increase the facility's processing capacity, enabling it to serve a larger number of livestock producers and to develop value-added products that capture more of the consumer dollar . The expansion is expected to create additional jobs and to support the growth of the region's cattle industry, which is the leading agricultural sector in Tazewell County .

The Blue Ridge Butchery Phase 2 expansion would build on the initial $2 million investment in the facility, which was funded by grants from the Virginia Tobacco Commission and a loan from the Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority . The 7,500-square-foot plant currently has two processing lines for beef, pork, lamb, and poultry, and is expected to bring approximately 15 new jobs to the area . The Phase 2 expansion would increase the facility's capacity, enabling it to serve a larger number of producers and to develop new product lines, including branded beef products that can be sold in regional and national markets .

The expansion of food manufacturing capacity is essential to the growth of the region's livestock industry, which has been constrained by the lack of local processing capacity . The County has approximately 27,500 cattle that must currently travel far from the county to be processed, incurring significant transportation costs and stress on the animals that reduce the quality of the beef . The Blue Ridge Butchery facility provides a local processing option, reducing transportation costs and enabling producers to capture more of the value chain . The Phase 2 expansion would increase the facility's capacity, enabling it to serve a larger number of producers and to support the growth of the region's cattle industry .

The Blue Ridge Butchery Phase 2 expansion would also support the development of value-added products, including branded beef products that can be sold in regional and national markets . The facility's federal inspection status, which allows it to sell meat across state lines, provides access to national markets . The development of a local meat brand that leverages the region's reputation for quality livestock production would create a premium product that generates higher returns for producers . The Phase 2 expansion would provide the processing capacity needed for the development of such branded products .

The Blue Ridge Butchery Phase 2 expansion would also create jobs and economic activity, building on the initial 15 jobs created by the facility . The expansion would create additional jobs in processing, quality control, logistics, and management, providing employment opportunities for workers in the region . The expansion would also generate economic activity through the increased processing of local livestock, supporting the region's agricultural economy and creating jobs throughout the supply chain . The expansion would thus provide both direct and indirect economic benefits to the region.

The Blue Ridge Butchery Phase 2 expansion would be supported by the Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority, which has provided loans and grants for the initial facility . The expansion would also be eligible for support from the Virginia Tobacco Commission and other state and federal programs that support agricultural development . The implementor should work with these agencies to secure the necessary funding for the expansion, building on the relationships established during the near-term phase.

**Steps for a Plan Implementor to Expand Food Manufacturing Capacity**

1. **Conduct a Feasibility Study for Phase 2 Expansion**: The implementor should work with Blue Ridge Butchery and local stakeholders to conduct a feasibility study for the Phase 2 expansion. The study should assess the demand for additional processing capacity, the potential for value-added products, and the economic benefits of the expansion. The study should also identify the costs and financing needs for the expansion.

2. **Secure Funding for the Expansion**: The implementor should work with the Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority, the Virginia Tobacco Commission, and other funding sources to secure the necessary financing for the expansion. The implementor should also explore federal funding opportunities, including USDA grants and the Appalachian Regional Commission's POWER Initiative.

3. **Develop Value-Added Product Lines**: The implementor should work with Blue Ridge Butchery to develop value-added product lines, including branded beef products that can be sold in regional and national markets. The implementor should also explore opportunities for developing other value-added products, including smoked meats, sausages, and ready-to-eat products.

4. **Expand Workforce Training Programs**: The implementor should work with the Mountain Empire Community College Foundation and other training providers to develop training programs for the additional workers needed for the Phase 2 expansion. The implementor should also work with the Virginia Talent Accelerator Program to secure recruitment and training services for the expansion.

5. **Market the Facility to Livestock Producers**: The implementor should work with the Virginia Cattlemen's Association and other agricultural organizations to market the Blue Ridge Butchery facility to livestock producers in the region. The implementor should also work to build relationships with producers, ensuring that the facility has a consistent supply of livestock for processing.

6. **Monitor and Report Outcomes**: The implementor should establish metrics to measure the success of the Phase 2 expansion, including the number of jobs created, the volume of livestock processed, and the economic impact of the expansion. The implementor should report this data to funders and stakeholders to demonstrate the impact of the expansion and to secure continued support.

---

### Establish Timber Plantations on Reclaimed Mine Lands: A New Industry on Underutilized Lands

The establishment of productive timber plantations on reclaimed mine lands represents a significant opportunity to create a new industry in the region, transforming degraded lands into productive forests that provide both economic and environmental benefits. The Appalachian region contains hundreds of thousands of acres of reclaimed mine lands that are currently underutilized, offering potential for the development of sustainable timber plantations . The Appalachian Regional Reforestation Initiative (ARRI), a coalition of groups including citizens, the coal industry, and government, is dedicated to restoring forests on coal mined lands in the Eastern United States . ARRI advocates using the Forestry Reclamation Approach (FRA) to plant trees on coal mined lands, which can create highly productive forests under existing laws and regulations .

The establishment of timber plantations on reclaimed mine lands offers significant economic potential, with hybrid poplar emerging as a particularly promising species for the region. Research has demonstrated that hybrid poplar can achieve exceptional growth on reclaimed mine lands, with one study finding that hybrid poplar grew 127 cm in the first year in the most intensive treatment at a site in Virginia . The survival and growth of hybrid poplar on these sites suggest that this species may be well suited for reforesting reclaimed mined land in the Appalachians . The tree's rapid growth, with some hybrids capable of growing 5 to 8 feet per year, enables relatively short rotations of 10 to 15 years, providing returns that are substantially faster than those of native hardwoods.

The Forestry Reclamation Approach (FRA) provides the scientific foundation for establishing timber plantations on reclaimed mine lands. The FRA consists of five core principles: creating a non-compacted growth medium, using the best available soil materials for root growth, planting a variety of native tree species, using proper planting techniques, and applying a tree-compatible ground cover to control competition . The approach emphasizes the creation of a favorable rooting environment, which is essential for tree survival and growth on the compacted, nutrient-poor soils that characterize many reclaimed mine sites . The use of tree-compatible ground covers, as opposed to the competitive grasses and legumes that are often used for erosion control, is also critical to the success of reforestation .

The silvicultural requirements for establishing timber plantations on reclaimed mine lands are specific and must be carefully managed to achieve optimal growth. Competing vegetation is a significant challenge, as the grasses and legumes that are often used for erosion control can outcompete tree seedlings for light, water, and nutrients . Weed control is therefore essential, with studies showing that the weed control+tillage+fertilization treatment improves the survival and growth of hybrid poplar . Soil compaction is another challenge, as the heavy equipment used during reclamation can create compacted soils that inhibit root growth . Tillage treatments can ameliorate the detrimental effects of compaction and improve seedling growth . Fertilization is often necessary because plant available nitrogen and phosphorus tend to be low on mine soils .

The establishment of timber plantations on reclaimed mine lands offers additional economic opportunities beyond the sale of timber. The plantations can be used for biomass energy production, providing a renewable energy source that supports the region's energy independence. The plantations can also be used for carbon sequestration, generating carbon credits that can be sold on carbon markets. The plantations can also support the region's tourism sector, with the restored forests providing opportunities for hiking, wildlife viewing, and outdoor recreation. The use of reclaimed mine lands for timber production thus provides both economic and environmental benefits, transforming liabilities into assets that support the region's diversification.

The economic viability of timber plantations on reclaimed mine lands depends on the species planted, the silvicultural treatments applied, and the markets for the timber produced. Hybrid poplar, with its rapid growth and high biomass yield, offers attractive returns for landowners willing to invest in intensive silviculture. The wood can be used for pulp and paper, oriented strand board (OSB), and other wood products, with demand driven by the building and paper industries. The carbon sequestration benefits of hybrid poplar plantations can also be monetized through carbon credit markets, providing an additional revenue stream for plantation owners.

The success of timber plantations on reclaimed mine lands will require continued research and technical assistance, with Virginia Tech's Powell River Project providing resources for landowners and land managers . The project has published numerous resources on reclamation, including guides on how to restore forests on surface-mined land, growing Christmas trees on reclaimed surface-mined land, and reclaiming mined lands as industrial sites . These resources provide the technical foundation for the establishment and management of timber plantations on reclaimed mine lands.

**Steps for a Plan Implementor to Establish Timber Plantations on Reclaimed Mine Lands**

1. **Identify Suitable Reclaimed Mine Lands**: The implementor should work with the Virginia Department of Energy, the Office of Surface Mining Reclamation and Enforcement (OSMRE), and local landowners to identify reclaimed mine lands that are suitable for timber production. The implementor should assess the soil conditions, topography, and vegetation cover of potential sites, ensuring that they meet the requirements for successful reforestation.

2. **Develop a Reforestation Plan**: The implementor should work with Virginia Tech's Powell River Project and other technical assistance providers to develop a reforestation plan for each site. The plan should include site preparation, species selection, planting techniques, and management practices. The plan should follow the Forestry Reclamation Approach (FRA), ensuring that the site is prepared correctly and that the trees are planted using proper techniques .

3. **Secure Funding for Reforestation**: The implementor should identify potential funding sources for reforestation, including the Appalachian Regional Reforestation Initiative (ARRI), the Virginia Department of Energy, and federal programs. The implementor should also explore opportunities for carbon credit markets, which can provide a revenue stream for reforestation projects.

4. **Establish Partnerships with Foresters and Contractors**: The implementor should establish partnerships with professional foresters and contractors who can provide the technical expertise needed for successful reforestation. The implementor should also work with nurseries to secure high-quality seedlings that are adapted to the local conditions.

5. **Implement the Reforestation Plan**: The implementor should oversee the implementation of the reforestation plan, ensuring that site preparation, planting, and management are carried out correctly. The implementor should monitor the survival and growth of the trees, applying additional treatments as needed to ensure their success.

6. **Develop Markets for the Timber Produced**: The implementor should work with industry partners to develop markets for the timber produced from the plantations. This includes identifying potential buyers for pulpwood, sawlogs, and other wood products, and developing relationships with wood processing companies.

---

### Develop Industrial Parks Along US 19 Corridor: Creating Shovel-Ready Sites for New Businesses

The development of industrial parks along the US 19 corridor is essential to attracting new businesses and supporting the growth of existing ones, providing the sites and infrastructure that companies need to locate and expand in the region. The Virginia Business Ready Sites Program provides a framework for the development of industrial parks, classifying sites based on their readiness for development . A tier-one site is one that has just been identified for development and has no sales price, while tier-five sites are fully prepared for development . Southside Virginia, which includes the US 19 corridor, has the most shovel-ready industrial sites in the state, demonstrating the effectiveness of the program in attracting investment .

The development of industrial parks along the US 19 corridor should be guided by the lessons learned from successful industrial park development in the region. The Southern Virginia Regional Alliance's service region has worked with engineering firms to complete right-of-way and engineering for reliable, redundant and abundant sites across the region . The alliance's success in preparing sites for development demonstrates the importance of collaboration between economic development organizations, engineering firms, and local governments. The alliance's strategy of keeping an inventory of ready sites that serve diverse industries leads to sites that are ready to market for prospects who need to ensure that the risks of building on the sites have been assessed and minimized .

The site selection criteria for industrial parks should consider the needs of different industries, including the requirements for land, utilities, transportation, and workforce. The location of industrial parks along the US 19 corridor provides access to transportation infrastructure, including the highway itself and the rail networks of CSX and Norfolk Southern. The parks should also have access to utilities, including electricity, water, and sewer, as well as broadband and cellular connectivity that is essential for modern businesses. The availability of a skilled workforce is also a critical factor, with the parks located near communities that can provide the workers needed for manufacturing and other industrial operations.

The development of industrial parks along the US 19 corridor should be supported by the Virginia Business Ready Sites Program, which provides the technical assistance and certification needed to attract businesses . The program's five-tiered system of classifying sites provides a framework for site development, with tier-five sites being fully prepared for development . The implementor should work with the Virginia Business Ready Sites Program to ensure that the parks meet the standards for certification, which can attract businesses seeking to reduce the risks associated with development .

The economic impact of industrial parks is significant, with research showing that industrial park development can create jobs, generate tax revenue, and stimulate economic activity . The absorption rate of industrial parks, or the percentage of land occupied by businesses, is influenced by the age of the park and its location . Older parks tend to have higher absorption rates, as do parks in less rural areas . The implementor should consider these factors when developing industrial parks, ensuring that the parks are marketed effectively and that the businesses attracted to the parks are a good fit for the region.

The development of industrial parks along the US 19 corridor should be integrated with the other economic development initiatives in the region, including the PSH construction, the food manufacturing expansion, and the timber plantations. The parks can provide sites for businesses that support these initiatives, including manufacturers of components for the PSH facility, processors of agricultural products, and wood products manufacturers. The integration of the parks with the other initiatives creates a synergistic effect, with each initiative supporting the others and contributing to the region's economic transformation.

The successful development of industrial parks along the US 19 corridor will require collaboration among a diverse set of stakeholders, including local governments, economic development organizations, and private developers. The implementor should work with these stakeholders to identify suitable sites, to secure funding for development, and to market the parks to potential businesses. The implementor should also work with the Virginia Business Ready Sites Program to ensure that the parks meet the standards for certification, which can attract businesses seeking to reduce the risks associated with development .

**Steps for a Plan Implementor to Develop Industrial Parks**

1. **Identify Suitable Sites Along US 19**: The implementor should work with local governments and economic development organizations to identify sites along the US 19 corridor that are suitable for industrial park development. The sites should have access to transportation infrastructure, utilities, and a skilled workforce. The implementor should also consider the needs of different industries, including the requirements for land, buildings, and services.

2. **Secure Funding for Site Development**: The implementor should identify potential funding sources for site development, including the Virginia Business Ready Sites Program, the Appalachian Regional Commission's POWER Initiative, and federal economic development programs. The implementor should also work with private developers to secure the necessary investment for site development.

3. **Prepare Sites for Development**: The implementor should work with engineering firms and contractors to prepare the sites for development, including grading, utilities installation, and road construction. The implementor should also ensure that the sites meet the standards for certification under the Virginia Business Ready Sites Program.

4. **Market the Parks to Businesses**: The implementor should work with economic development organizations to market the industrial parks to businesses seeking to locate or expand in the region. The marketing efforts should highlight the advantages of the region, including its transportation infrastructure, skilled workforce, and business-friendly environment.

5. **Coordinate with Other Economic Development Initiatives**: The implementor should coordinate the development of industrial parks with the other economic development initiatives in the region, including the PSH construction, the food manufacturing expansion, and the timber plantations. The parks should provide sites for businesses that support these initiatives, creating synergies that enhance their economic impact.

6. **Monitor and Report Outcomes**: The implementor should establish metrics to measure the success of the industrial parks, including the number of jobs created, the investment attracted, and the tax revenue generated. The implementor should report this data to funders and stakeholders to demonstrate the impact of the parks and to secure continued support.

---

### Conclusion: A New Economic Landscape Emerges

The medium-term actions outlined in this chapter represent the most significant period of investment and construction in the US 19 corridor since the coal boom of the early twentieth century. The commencement of PSH construction at the Tazewell County site will create a $2 billion energy storage facility that provides the foundation for the region's clean energy future. The expansion of food manufacturing capacity through the Blue Ridge Butchery Phase 2 project will support the growth of the region's agricultural sector, creating value-added products that capture more of the consumer dollar. The establishment of timber plantations on reclaimed mine lands will transform degraded lands into productive forests, creating a new industry that provides both economic and environmental benefits. The development of industrial parks along the US 19 corridor will provide shovel-ready sites for new businesses, attracting investment and creating jobs .

The successful implementation of these medium-term actions will require continued collaboration among federal and state agencies, regional economic development organizations, local governments, businesses, and community organizations. VCEDA will play a central role in coordinating these efforts, building on the relationships and momentum established during the near-term phase. The ARC's POWER Initiative will continue to provide critical funding, with its 2026 award package of $68.2 million representing the largest investment in coal-impacted communities since the initiative's launch . The medium-term actions thus represent the period when the region's economic transformation becomes visible, with new facilities, new industries, and new jobs emerging on the landscape.

For a plan implementor, the medium-term phase requires a shift from planning and project management to construction and operations management. The implementor must establish construction management capabilities, coordinate with contractors and suppliers, and ensure that projects are completed on schedule and within budget. The implementor must also maintain the stakeholder engagement and communication channels that were established during the near-term phase, ensuring that communities are informed of progress and that any issues are identified and addressed promptly. The implementor's role is thus both strategic and operational, requiring the ability to manage complex construction projects while maintaining the vision and momentum that drive the region's transformation.

The medium-term actions set the stage for the long-term success of the region's economic transformation, creating the infrastructure, industries, and jobs that will define the region's future. The PSH facility will provide clean, reliable energy storage that supports the region's energy independence and contributes to Virginia's clean energy goals. The food manufacturing expansion will support the growth of the region's agricultural sector, creating value-added products that capture more of the consumer dollar. The timber plantations will transform degraded lands into productive forests, creating a new industry that provides both economic and environmental benefits. The industrial parks will provide the sites and infrastructure that businesses need to locate and grow in the region. Together, these actions will create a new economic landscape that honors the region's industrial heritage while embracing the opportunities of the clean energy economy.

**Section 11.3: Long-Term Vision (5-15 Years)**
- Full operational PSH facilities providing grid-scale storage
- Green food manufacturing cluster with dairy integration
- Regional energy independence from renewable sources
- Sustainable forestry and bioenergy feedstock production

# Chapter Twelve: Resource Guide - Banks, Lenders, Coordinators, Tools, and Government Programs

## Introduction: The Support Infrastructure for Economic Transformation

The successful implementation of the US 19 Corridor's economic transformation plan depends on access to a comprehensive network of financial resources, government programs, and technical assistance providers. This chapter serves as a practical resource guide for plan implementors, entrepreneurs, business owners, and community leaders seeking to navigate the complex landscape of funding opportunities and support services available in the region. The guide is organized into sections covering financial lenders and banks, government programs and agencies, project coordinators and economic development organizations, workforce training and education resources, and key legislative and policy tools.

The resources identified in this chapter are interconnected, with many programs requiring collaboration between multiple agencies and organizations. The Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority (VCEDA) serves as the central coordinating entity for many of these resources, leveraging its relationships with state and federal agencies and its deep understanding of the region's needs and opportunities . The Appalachian Regional Commission (ARC) provides critical funding through its POWER Initiative, which has invested $484.7 million in 564 projects impacting coal-impacted counties since 2015 . The USDA Rural Development programs provide support for agricultural and rural business development, while the Virginia Department of Housing and Community Development (DHCD) administers broadband and community development programs.

For a plan implementor, understanding the full range of available resources is essential to securing the funding and technical assistance needed for project implementation. The guide provides contact information, program descriptions, and application guidance for each resource, enabling implementors to identify the most appropriate funding sources for their specific projects. The guide also highlights the interconnections between programs, enabling implementors to develop comprehensive funding strategies that leverage multiple sources.

---

## Section 12.1: Financial Lenders and Banks

### Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority (VCEDA)

The Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority (VCEDA) is the primary economic development organization for Southwest Virginia's coalfield region, serving as the central coordinating entity for the region's economic transformation . VCEDA was formed by the Virginia General Assembly in 1988 to enhance and diversify the economy of the seven-county, one-city coalfield region, which includes Buchanan, Dickenson, Lee, Russell, Scott, Tazewell, and Wise counties and the City of Norton . Since its formation, the region has seen the creation of tens of thousands of jobs and billions in private investment, with the economic diversity index improving by more than 250 percent and the region's unemployment rate being cut by at least half .

VCEDA's mission, as outlined in the Code of Virginia, is "to provide financial support for the purchase of real estate, construction of buildings for sale or lease, installation of utilities, direct loans and grants to private for-profit basic employers; may apply for matching funds from the state or federal government, or the private sector; and any other support improvements it deems necessary" . The Authority is funded by a portion of the severance taxes paid by the coal and natural gas industries in the region, ensuring that the industries that created the region's economic dependency contribute to its diversification .

VCEDA provides a range of financing programs, including loans and grants for economic development projects, infrastructure development, workforce development and training, tourism development, and entrepreneurship. In 2024 alone, the VCEDA Board approved 37 loans and grants totaling $13,342,233 in new VCEDA funding for projects in all seven counties in the region, projected to help create 192 full-time and 80 part-time jobs and $7,107,526 in private investment . The Authority's financing is based in part upon the number of new jobs created, wage rates, and amount of private investment .

The Seed Capital Matching Fund is VCEDA's flagship program for supporting entrepreneurship and small business development. The program provides dollar-for-dollar matching grants up to $10,000 for new, startup, and emerging small businesses located within the region . According to a 2021 Virginia Tech study, the Seed Capital Matching Fund program had a very high return on investment ratio of 34:1 . Since the program was established in 2017, more than 200 new business startups have been assisted in the region, creating hundreds of jobs and generating millions in private investment . The program is administered in partnership with the Small Business Development Centers at Mountain Empire and Southwest Virginia community colleges .

VCEDA also provides financing for workforce development and training, infrastructure development, and site development. The Authority has funded workforce training programs at Mountain Empire Community College and Southwest Virginia Community College, supporting the development of the skilled workforce needed for the region's new industries . VCEDA has also funded broadband expansion projects, including a $125,000 grant to the Buchanan County IDA for Starlink Internet service for unserved and underserved citizens with telehealth and telemedicine needs .

The VCEDA Board includes 16 members, all Virginia residents, representing the three largest coal producers in the region, the largest oil and gas producer, the Virginia Coal and Energy Alliance, five gubernatorial appointees, representatives from the boards of supervisors of the three largest coal producing counties, the executive directors of the LENOWISCO and Cumberland Plateau planning district commissions, and a representative from the Virginia Economic Development Partnership . The Board annually elects a chairman, vice chairman, secretary, and treasurer from its membership .

### How to Work with VCEDA

For a plan implementor seeking to work with VCEDA, the first step is to contact the main office or the appropriate staff member based on the type of project. For entrepreneurship and small business projects, Diane Thomas, Business Development Specialist, is the primary contact. For workforce development and training projects, Angie Farmer, Economic Development Specialist, provides assistance. For marketing and communications, Susan Copeland, Marketing Coordinator, is the appropriate contact.

The application process for VCEDA funding varies by program. For the Seed Capital Matching Fund, applicants begin by contacting and working through the applicable Small Business Development Center (SBDC) for their area (Mountain Empire Community College or Southwest Virginia Community College) . The SBDC then coordinates the request with VCEDA. Applicants are also required to obtain a letter or resolution of support for their application from the county industrial development authority of the county in which their project is located (or from the Norton Industrial Development Authority for businesses in the City of Norton) .

For larger loan and grant programs, applicants should contact the VCEDA main office to discuss their project and determine the appropriate financing program. Applicants should be prepared to provide information about the project's scope, job creation potential, private investment, and alignment with VCEDA's mission of enhancing and diversifying the region's economy. The VCEDA Board meets regularly to consider applications, and applicants should allow sufficient time for the review process.

## Section 12.2: Government Programs and Agencies

### Appalachian Regional Commission (ARC)

The Appalachian Regional Commission is a federal-state partnership that works with the people of Appalachia to create opportunities for self-sustaining economic development and improved quality of life. The Commission was created in 1965 as a U.S. federal-state partnership focused on the economic and social development of the Appalachian region, which includes 423 counties across 13 states, including Virginia. The ARC's mission is to support economic and community development in Appalachia, with a focus on job creation, infrastructure improvement, and workforce development.

The POWER (Partnerships for Opportunity and Workforce and Economic Revitalization) Initiative is ARC's flagship program for coal-impacted communities, directing federal resources to economic diversification and revitalization projects in Appalachian communities affected by the downturn of the coal industry. Since 2015, ARC has invested $484.7 million in 564 projects impacting 365 coal-impacted counties, projected to support nearly 54,000 jobs and prepare nearly 170,000 workers and students for new opportunities . The 2024 POWER award package of $68.2 million represents the largest POWER investment since the initiative was launched in 2015.

The POWER Initiative supports a wide range of project types, including entrepreneurship development, workforce training, infrastructure investment, and business attraction. Funding available through the competitive ARC POWER program is up to $100,000 for Planning awards, $400,000 to $2 million for Implementation awards, and up to $2.5 million for Broadband Implementation awards. The program emphasizes collaboration and partnership, with projects required to demonstrate community support and engagement.

ARC also provides funding for broadband deployment in the region, with the Commission's grant programs supporting the extension of broadband service to areas currently unserved by a provider. The broadband grants can be used for infrastructure deployment, planning, and adoption programs. The combination of ARC funding and BEAD funding provides significant resources for broadband expansion along the US 19 corridor.

### How to Apply for ARC POWER Grants

For a plan implementor seeking to apply for ARC POWER grants, the application process requires several steps. First, all applicants must register for an account in Pathways, ARC's grant management system, before submitting an application. A required Letter of Intent (LOI) must be submitted by the annual deadline, typically in May, including a project summary that describes the project's goals, the target area, the expected outcomes, and alignment with the POWER Initiative's priorities.

The full application must demonstrate how the project will create high-quality, in-demand jobs, attract new sources of private investment, and provide comprehensive workforce services and skills training. The application should include a detailed budget, a timeline, a description of the project team, and evidence of community support. The application should also include a plan for measuring and reporting on the project's outcomes. ARC prioritizes POWER funding for projects that will produce economic opportunities tied to fostering entrepreneurial activities, developing industry clusters in communities, building a competitive workforce, and increasing access to broadband.

The implementor should engage with regional partners, including VCEDA, local governments, community colleges, and workforce development boards, to build a coalition that can support the application and implement the project. Letters of support from these partners should be included in the application. If the application is awarded, the implementor must be prepared to begin implementation promptly, establishing a project management structure, hiring staff, and beginning the activities outlined in the application.

### USDA Rural Development

The U.S. Department of Agriculture's Rural Development programs provide a range of support for agricultural and rural business development in the Tazewell County region, including farm subsidies, disaster assistance, rural business development programs, and the Conservation Reserve Program. The 2024 farm subsidies for Tazewell County included $230,362 in Non-insured Disaster Assistance, $13,721 through the Conservation Reserve Program, and $5,047 in Dairy Programs. These programs provide a vital safety net for farmers facing the uncertainties of weather, markets, and production conditions.

The Non-insured Disaster Assistance Program (NAP) provides financial assistance to producers of crops for which federal crop insurance is not available. The NAP program provides coverage for losses caused by natural disasters, including drought, excessive moisture, hail, wind, frost, and other weather-related events that are common in the Appalachian region. The Conservation Reserve Program (CRP) encourages farmers to convert environmentally sensitive cropland to conservation uses, providing annual rental payments and cost-share assistance. The Dairy Programs include the Dairy Margin Coverage program and the Dairy Indemnity Payment Program, providing a safety net for dairy producers.

The USDA Rural Development programs also support rural business development through loans, grants, and technical assistance. The Rural Business Development Grants provide funding for business development projects in rural communities, while the Intermediary Relending Program provides loans to intermediaries that lend to businesses in rural areas. The Value-Added Producer Grants provide funding for producers seeking to add value to their products through processing, marketing, and branding.

### Virginia Department of Housing and Community Development (DHCD)

The Virginia Department of Housing and Community Development (DHCD) administers a range of programs that support community development, broadband expansion, and housing in the Tazewell County region. The Community Business Launch grant program provides funding for incubator projects and small business development, supporting the growth of entrepreneurship in rural communities. The program provides matching funds for incubator projects, reducing the cost of developing shared facilities and services for entrepreneurs.

DHCD also administers the Broadband Equity, Access, and Deployment (BEAD) program in Virginia, which provides over $545 million in federal funding to connect the remaining 133,000 unserved locations across the Commonwealth. The funding will mobilize nearly $430 million in additional private investment, extending broadband infrastructure to homes, businesses, and community anchor institutions. The Local Government Broadband Affordability and Adoption Planning Grants provide $25,000 to cities, counties, and tribal governments to conduct local needs assessments and develop broadband affordability and adoption plans.

DHCD also administers the Virginia Telecommunication Initiative (VATI), which provides grants to localities partnered with internet service providers to finance the deployment of high-speed broadband to unserved locations. The program has leveraged $1.198 billion in private and local match funding for FY22, FY23, and FY24 VATI projects, demonstrating the effectiveness of public-private partnerships in broadband deployment.

### Virginia Tobacco Commission

The Virginia Tobacco Region Revitalization Commission, commonly known as the Tobacco Commission, provides grants for economic diversification projects in the coalfield region, including the Blue Ridge Butchery facility in Tazewell County. The Commission funds projects that enhance economic growth across 40 localities in Southern and Southwest Virginia, including the seven counties and one city served by VCEDA. The Commission has provided significant funding for agricultural diversification, manufacturing development, and infrastructure projects in the region.

The Tobacco Commission's funding is derived from the Master Settlement Agreement, which settled lawsuits against tobacco companies for health-related costs. The Commission's mission is to use these funds to stimulate economic growth and revitalization in tobacco-dependent communities, supporting projects that create jobs, attract investment, and diversify local economies. The Commission has funded a wide range of projects, including the Blue Ridge Butchery facility, which received a $350,000 grant from the Tobacco Commission for construction and equipment.

The Tobacco Commission's funding is administered through a competitive application process, with projects evaluated based on their economic impact, job creation potential, and alignment with the Commission's strategic priorities. The Commission works in partnership with VCEDA and other economic development organizations to identify and support high-impact projects in the region.

### Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC)

The Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) plays a central role in the development of pumped storage hydropower projects, as it has exclusive authority to license non-federal hydropower projects under the Federal Power Act. The FERC application process is rigorous and comprehensive, requiring extensive environmental, geological, archeological, and technical studies to demonstrate the project's feasibility and its compliance with applicable regulations. The Tazewell County PSH project would require a FERC license, and Dominion Energy has filed a preliminary permit application with FERC to initiate the process.

The FERC licensing process for PSH projects involves multiple stages, including the preparation of a pre-application document (PAD), the conduct of environmental and technical studies, and the filing of a license application. The entire process typically takes five to ten years from preliminary permit to construction authorization. The process also includes extensive public consultation, with opportunities for interested parties to comment on proposed projects and to participate in the regulatory process.

FERC also regulates the wholesale electricity market, including the rates for electricity sales and transmission services. The PSH facility would participate in the wholesale electricity market, generating revenue through the sale of electricity during peak demand periods. FERC's oversight of the wholesale market ensures that the facility operates in a competitive and non-discriminatory manner.

## Section 12.3: Project Coordinators and Economic Development Organizations

### LENOWISCO Planning District Commission

The LENOWISCO Planning District Commission serves as a regional planning and economic development organization for Lee, Scott, Wise, and Norton, providing staff functions for VCEDA and coordinating economic development efforts in the region. The Commission works with local governments, businesses, and community organizations to support economic development, transportation planning, and community development. The Commission's staff provides technical assistance to VCEDA and to local governments, helping to identify and secure funding for economic development projects.

The LENOWISCO Planning District Commission also administers a range of programs, including the Virginia Community Development Block Grant program, the Virginia Housing Trust Fund, and the Virginia Department of Transportation's transportation planning programs. The Commission works with VCEDA to coordinate economic development efforts across the region, ensuring that projects are aligned with regional priorities and that resources are deployed effectively.

## Section 12.4: Workforce Training and Education

### Mountain Empire Community College (MECC)

Mountain Empire Community College serves as a primary provider of workforce training and education for the Tazewell County region, offering programs in advanced manufacturing, healthcare, information technology, and other fields. The MECC Foundation has received significant support from VCEDA for workforce development and training, including a $200,000 grant for future training and certification needs for residents in the VCEDA service territory. The college anticipates training 500 or more students in 2025-2026, with existing industries expanding their employment offerings.

MECC offers a range of programs relevant to the region's economic development strategy, including programs in welding, machining, industrial maintenance, and other manufacturing skills. The college also offers programs in healthcare, information technology, and business, providing the workforce needed for the region's growing service and healthcare sectors. MECC works with employers to develop customized training programs that meet their specific needs, ensuring that workers have the skills required for the jobs created by new and expanding businesses.

### Virginia Talent Accelerator Program

The Virginia Talent Accelerator Program provides recruitment and training services at no cost to eligible companies expanding or locating assets in Virginia. The program offers customized recruitment and training services, helping companies find and train the workers they need for their operations. The program is designed to support the growth of Virginia's economy by ensuring that companies have access to a skilled workforce.

The Virginia Talent Accelerator Program has supported a number of manufacturing projects in the region, including VFP, Inc., which received support for its expansion in Scott County. The program's services include recruitment, pre-employment testing, training program design, and delivery of training. The program works with Virginia's community colleges, four-year universities, and other training providers to deliver the training needed for each company.

## Section 12.5: Tools and Infrastructure

### VDOT Corridor Studies

The Virginia Department of Transportation (VDOT) conducts corridor studies along US 19 to address safety and congestion concerns, ensuring that the highway continues to serve its vital function as a Corridor of Statewide Significance. The studies evaluate traffic conditions, crash data, and potential improvements, developing performance-based solutions that can be effectively implemented. The US 19 / ALT US 58 intersection study in Russell County is a prime example of the kind of planning that supports the corridor's development.

The VDOT studies are part of the state's broader transportation planning process, which includes the identification of critical transportation needs, the development of performance-based solutions, and the application for funding through programs like SMART SCALE. The studies address both safety and congestion issues, including crash analysis, traffic data collection, and consideration of environmental impacts. The studies include public engagement, ensuring that the concerns of the community are addressed in the study's recommendations.
