### Coalfields Expressway

The Coalfields Expressway is a major transportation infrastructure project that will provide improved access to the region, supporting the development of the US 19 corridor. The project is being developed by the Virginia Coalfields Expressway Authority, which is working to secure funding and advance the project. The project has received significant support from VCEDA and other regional partners, with the Authority securing federal funding needed to complete the Coalfields Expressway as a four-lane highway from Grundy, Virginia to Buchanan County's Southern Gap development.

The Coalfields Expressway will provide improved transportation access to the region, reducing travel times and improving safety. The project is expected to support economic development by providing better access to industrial sites and commercial areas. The project is also expected to support the region's energy and manufacturing sectors, enabling the movement of goods and resources essential to economic development.

## Section 12.6: Key Legislative and Policy Tools

### Virginia Code § 15.2-6003: VCEDA Enabling Legislation

The Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority was created by the Virginia General Assembly in 1988 through the passage of Chapter 60 of Title 15.2 of the Code of Virginia . The legislation defines the Authority's powers and responsibilities, including its authority to provide financial support for economic development projects, to apply for and accept grants and loans from federal agencies, and to engage in economic development marketing and business attraction activities .

### Virginia Code § 56-585.1: Electric Utility Regulation

The 2017 legislation that enabled the Tazewell County PSH project (SB 1418 and HB 1760) amended Virginia's electric utility regulations to authorize cost recovery for pumped storage projects in the coalfield region. The legislation declared that the construction of pumped storage facilities in the coalfield region "is in the public interest," establishing a presumption that PSH projects serve important public policy objectives. The legislation also requires that at least part of the energy used to operate the facility be generated by renewable resources in the area.

### Virginia Code § 15.2-6007: Acceptance of Funds, Property, and Grants or Loans

The Virginia Code authorizes VCEDA to accept funds and property from the federal government, the Commonwealth, persons, and localities, and to use the same for any of the purposes for which the Authority is created . Localities are also authorized to lend or donate money or other property to the Authority for any of its purposes . This provision provides the legal authority for the public-private partnerships that are essential to the region's economic development strategy.

---

## Conclusion: Building a Network of Support

The resources identified in this chapter provide a comprehensive network of support for the US 19 corridor's economic transformation, enabling the region to access the funding, technical assistance, and expertise needed for successful project implementation. The Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority serves as the central coordinating entity for many of these resources, leveraging its relationships with state and federal agencies and its deep understanding of the region's needs and opportunities . The Appalachian Regional Commission's POWER Initiative provides critical funding for coal-impacted communities, while the USDA Rural Development programs support agricultural and rural business development.

For a plan implementor, the key to success is to understand the full range of available resources and to develop comprehensive funding strategies that leverage multiple sources. The implementor should build strong relationships with the key contacts identified in this chapter, ensuring that they have the support needed to navigate the complex application processes and to secure the funding required for their projects. The implementor should also work to coordinate the various programs and agencies, ensuring that the region's economic development efforts are aligned and that resources are deployed effectively.

The resource guide provides a practical tool for implementors, entrepreneurs, and community leaders seeking to access the support they need for economic development projects. The guide's contact information, program descriptions, and application guidance enable users to identify the most appropriate resources for their specific needs and to navigate the application processes effectively. The guide's comprehensive coverage of financial lenders, government programs, project coordinators, and workforce training resources ensures that users have access to the full range of support available in the region.


# Chapter Twelve: Resource Guide - Banks, Lenders, Coordinators, Tools, and Government Programs

## Section 12.3: Project Coordinators and Economic Development Organizations

### Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority (VCEDA)

The Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority serves as the central coordinating entity for economic development in Southwest Virginia's coalfield region, providing the leadership, financing, and technical assistance essential to the region's economic transformation . Formed by the Virginia General Assembly in 1988, VCEDA was established to enhance and diversify the economy of the seven-county, one-city region, which includes Buchanan, Dickenson, Lee, Russell, Scott, Tazewell, and Wise counties, and the City of Norton . Since its formation, the region has seen the creation of tens of thousands of jobs and billions in private investment, with the Authority playing a pivotal role in attracting new industries and supporting the growth of existing businesses .

The Authority is funded by a portion of the severance taxes paid by the coal and natural gas industries in the region, ensuring that the industries that created the region's economic dependency contribute to its diversification . This self-sustaining funding model enables VCEDA to provide a comprehensive set of services and programs to help qualifying businesses locate or expand in Southwest Virginia's e-Region, including loans for real estate, building construction and expansion, equipment acquisition, and seed capital grants to assist new start-ups . The Authority's financing is based in part upon the number of new jobs created, wage rates, and the amount of private investment, ensuring that the resources are deployed where they will have the greatest economic impact.

VCEDA's leadership is provided by Executive Director/General Counsel Jonathan Belcher, who has been at the helm since 2006 and was also named executive director of the Virginia Coalfields Expressway Authority in 2020 . Belcher has been recognized multiple times as a Virginia Business Power 500 honoree, reflecting his leadership in economic development and his role in advancing what he describes as "one of the most critical road projects in the state" . His work has been instrumental in securing major job announcements, including a $1.3 million loan with Lawrence Brothers, a Tazewell County manufacturing company, for an expansion expected to create several dozen new jobs, and a $3.2 million loan to the Norton Industrial Development Authority to assist with construction of a new facility for Wrap Technologies anticipated to create 126 jobs .

The VCEDA Board provides strategic oversight and governance for the Authority, with 16 members representing the key stakeholders in the region's economy . Membership includes the three largest coal producers in the region, the largest oil and gas producer, the Virginia Coal and Energy Alliance, five gubernatorial appointees, representatives from the boards of supervisors of the three largest coal producing counties, and the executive directors of the LENOWISCO and Cumberland Plateau planning district commissions, as well as a representative from the Virginia Economic Development Partnership . The Board annually elects a chairman, vice chairman, secretary, and treasurer from its membership .

The 2026 VCEDA Board officers include Greg Edwards as chairman, Andy Hrovatic as vice chairman, Troy Nichols as secretary, and Brian Hess as treasurer, with outgoing chairman J.P. Richardson continuing to serve on the board and as chairman of the Executive Committee . The Board meets regularly to consider loan and grant applications, with 59 loans and grants totaling $18,759,753 in new funding for projects in the region approved by the VCEDA board during the 2025-2026 period . This substantial investment reflects the Authority's commitment to supporting economic growth and diversification across the entire seven-county, one-city region.

### LENOWISCO Planning District Commission

The LENOWISCO Planning District Commission serves as a vital regional planning and economic development organization for Lee, Scott, and Wise counties and the City of Norton, providing staff functions for VCEDA and coordinating economic development efforts in the region . The Commission works with local governments, businesses, and community organizations to support economic development, transportation planning, and community development, with a focus on attracting emerging industries to Southwest Virginia, creating jobs, and cleaning up abandoned sites in the region .

The Commission's leadership is provided by Executive Director Duane A. Miller, a Scott County native who has played an integral role in advancing the region's economic development interests . Miller has testified to the U.S. House of Representatives' Subcommittee on Environment regarding the benefits of the Environmental Protection Agency's Brownfields Program in rural, coal communities, demonstrating the region's commitment to brownfield redevelopment and the repurposing of former industrial sites for new economic uses . The Commission's work on brownfield redevelopment has been instrumental in attracting new industries and creating jobs in the region.

The LENOWISCO Planning District Commission's service area encompasses the counties of Lee, Wise, and Scott, and the City of Norton, which are among the most economically distressed counties in Virginia . The Commission works closely with VCEDA to coordinate economic development efforts, ensuring that projects are aligned with regional priorities and that resources are deployed effectively. The Commission also administers a range of programs, including the Virginia Community Development Block Grant program, the Virginia Housing Trust Fund, and the Virginia Department of Transportation's transportation planning programs.

### Cumberland Plateau Planning District Commission

The Cumberland Plateau Planning District Commission serves as the regional planning and economic development organization for Buchanan, Dickenson, Russell, and Tazewell counties, providing staff functions for VCEDA and coordinating economic development efforts in the region. The Commission works with local governments, businesses, and community organizations to support economic development, transportation planning, and community development, with a focus on attracting new industries and supporting the growth of existing businesses in the region.

The Commission's leadership is provided by Executive Director Scotty Wampler, who also serves on the VCEDA Board and represents the interests of the Cumberland Plateau region . The Commission's service area encompasses the counties of Buchanan, Dickenson, Russell, and Tazewell, which are among the most economically distressed counties in Virginia and have been significantly impacted by the decline of the coal industry. The Commission works closely with VCEDA to coordinate economic development efforts, ensuring that projects are aligned with regional priorities and that resources are deployed effectively.

### Industrial Development Authorities

The Industrial Development Authorities (IDAs) in each county and city serve as the primary local economic development organizations, working with VCEDA and other partners to attract and support businesses in their communities. The IDAs provide a range of services, including site development, building acquisition and renovation, financing, and workforce development. The IDAs work with VCEDA to coordinate economic development efforts across the region, ensuring that projects are aligned with regional priorities and that resources are deployed effectively.

The Virginia Coalfields Expressway Authority, led by Jonathan Belcher, works to advance what is described as "one of the most critical road projects in the state," providing improved transportation access that supports economic development across the region . The Authority's work is essential to the region's economic diversification, as improved transportation infrastructure enables the movement of goods and resources essential to economic development.

### Why This Organization is Useful

For a plan implementor, the project coordinators and economic development organizations identified in this section provide the essential leadership, coordination, and support needed to navigate the complex landscape of economic development in the US 19 corridor. VCEDA serves as the central coordinating entity, providing the financing, technical assistance, and strategic guidance that enable businesses to locate and expand in the region . The Authority's Board includes representatives from the region's key economic sectors, ensuring that the Authority's activities are aligned with the needs of the region's businesses and communities .

The Planning District Commissions provide the regional planning capacity that enables the effective coordination of economic development efforts across multiple jurisdictions. The LENOWISCO Planning District Commission's work on brownfield redevelopment has been instrumental in attracting new industries and creating jobs in the region, while the Cumberland Plateau Planning District Commission provides similar services for the counties in its service area . The Industrial Development Authorities provide the local capacity for site development, building acquisition, and financing, enabling businesses to locate and expand in specific communities.

The leadership provided by Jonathan Belcher, Duane Miller, and Scotty Wampler ensures that the region's economic development efforts are well-coordinated and that the region's interests are effectively represented at the state and federal levels . The recognition of Belcher in the Virginia Business Power 500 listing reflects the effectiveness of the region's economic development leadership and the importance of the region's economic development efforts to the Commonwealth of Virginia . The organizations identified in this section provide the essential foundation for the region's economic transformation, enabling the implementation of the projects and strategies outlined in this plan.

# Chapter Twelve: Resource Guide - Banks, Lenders, Coordinators, Tools, and Government Programs

## Section 12.3: Project Coordinators and Economic Development Organizations (Continued)

### Mount Rogers Planning District Commission

The Mount Rogers Planning District Commission (MRPDC) serves as a comprehensive regional planning and economic development organization for southwestern Virginia, providing essential technical assistance, grant administration, and coordination services to its member localities . Officially established in July 1969 under the Virginia Regional Cooperation Act, the MRPDC operates as one of 21 planning district commissions in the Commonwealth, recognized by the Appalachian Regional Commission as an ARC Local Development District and by the Economic Development Administration as an Economic Development District .

The MRPDC's service area encompasses the counties of Bland, Carroll, Grayson, Smyth, Washington, and Wythe, as well as the independent cities of Bristol and Galax . While this region is adjacent to the VCEDA coalfield region rather than directly within it, the MRPDC provides important coordination and support for economic development projects that have regional impacts. The Commission's work is centered chiefly on providing technical assistance to area jurisdictions in economic and community development, public management and administration, and cartography .

The MRPDC's economic development activities include assisting jurisdictions in preparing grant applications for economic development projects, publishing a quarterly report of economic indicators, and maintaining a comprehensive regional economic development strategy . The Commission has for several years provided circuit-riding town management assistance to member jurisdictions who cannot afford full-time managers, demonstrating its commitment to supporting rural communities with limited resources . The GIS Director provides mapping assistance to localities, including updating property maps, zoning maps, and house numbering maps.

The MRPDC also provides rural transportation planning services through funding under the Rural Transportation Program offered by the Virginia Department of Transportation (VDOT) . These services include the development of the rural Regional Long-Range Transportation Plan (RLRTP), provision of transportation elements for local Comprehensive Plans, and technical assistance to localities. Planning issues addressed include road capacity and safety monitoring and expansion of multimodal options (transit, rail, bicycle and pedestrian) as they relate to land use, economic development and environmental conservation .

### Cumberland Plateau Planning District Commission

The Cumberland Plateau Planning District Commission (CPPDC) is the primary regional planning and economic development organization for the coalfield counties of Buchanan, Dickenson, Russell, and Tazewell, serving as a critical partner to VCEDA and local governments in advancing the region's economic transformation . Founded in 1968, the CPPDC is one of 21 planning districts in the Commonwealth of Virginia established by the Virginia Area Development Act to promote regional cooperation . The CPPDC is designated as Planning District 2 .

The Commission's history reflects the region's commitment to regional cooperation in addressing economic development challenges. In 1968, the four coalfield counties voluntarily came together to form the Cumberland Plateau Economic Development Commission as an Economic Development District to work with the U.S. Economic Development Administration (EDA) . At about the same time, the Cumberland Plateau was designated as a Local Development District under the Appalachian Regional Commission (ARC) . Shortly thereafter, the Virginia State Legislature passed the Virginia Area Development Act which set forth the framework for the establishment of regional planning district commissions throughout the State, and the name was changed to the Cumberland Plateau Planning District Commission .

The purpose of the Commission as stated in the original By-laws was to assist the District's localities in addressing local problems on a regional basis as well as promoting economic development in the District in cooperation with Federal, State and local governments and organizations . That mission remains the same today, and the four counties have greatly benefitted from this cooperative effort. The spirit of regional cooperation has served the localities well as hundreds of millions of federal, state and regional grant and low-interest loan dollars have been obtained for much-needed water, sewer, industrial sites, telecommunications and other infrastructure projects in the four counties .

The return on investment from the CPPDC's work is substantial. In 1969, the District had received $111,469.01 in grants and services for the 4 counties from $12,622.50 in dues . In FY 2014, District localities received federal, state and regional grant/loan funds of nearly $48 million, amounting to $340 returned to the District for every one dollar in local dues . Over the Commission's history, the total grant and loan dollars received has reached nearly $500 million .

### Dickenson County Industrial Development Authority

The Dickenson County Industrial Development Authority (DCIDA) serves as the primary organization responsible for economic and industrial development in Dickenson County, working to promote a vibrant, diversified, and sustainable economy to enhance the quality of life for the citizens of Dickenson County . The IDA was created by the Dickenson County Board of Supervisors pursuant to the Industrial Development and Revenue Bond Act, Title 15.1, Chapter 33 of the Code of Virginia 1950 .

The DCIDA's mission is to promote industry and development of trade by inducing manufacturing, industrial, governmental and commercial enterprises to locate in or remain in Dickenson County, and furthering the use of the County's agricultural products and natural resources . The IDA furthers its objectives through the development of publicly-owned industrial parks and financing assistance for the attraction of new or expansion of existing industry within the County . The IDA is composed of a seven member Board of Directors appointed by the Dickenson County Board of Supervisors for four year terms .

The DCIDA has been actively engaged in supporting small business development and entrepreneurship in the county. In December 2025, VCEDA approved a $42,500 grant to the Dickenson County IDA to assist in the development of a small business incubator space at the Dickenson Center for Education and Research (DCER) in Clintwood . The funds will be used to make improvements to 3,200 square feet of unused space at the DCER and for workforce and small business development and training as the Dickenson County Community Business Launch program is started .

The new Community Business Launch program plans to foster local entrepreneurship by constructing small business incubator space and offering comprehensive support services to assist small businesses going through the incubator program, with capacity for approximately five businesses . The IDA plans to use Opportunity SWVA to run the programmatic piece of the initiative and has recently hired a new full-time community marketing specialist to oversee the day-to-day operations and serve as the first point of contact for business incubator participants . The program, which is expected to launch in spring 2026, will include a business pitch competition component with monetary support and free incubator space for one year for the winners .

### Russell County Industrial Development Authority

The Russell County Industrial Development Authority (RCIDA) serves as the primary organization responsible for economic and industrial development in Russell County, developing publicly owned local and regional industrial parks and collaborating with other economic development organizations to facilitate the location or expansion of industry within the county . The IDA is composed of a nine-member board of directors appointed by the Russell County Board of Supervisors .

The RCIDA's mission is to promote industry and develop economic trade by inducing manufacturing, industrial, governmental, nonprofit and commercial enterprises, and institutions of higher education to locate in or remain in Russell County, Virginia, to advance job opportunities, general prosperity and the economic welfare of citizens . The RCIDA's location has become recognized as a prime spot for companies requiring high inland security and access to a world-class telecommunications network .

The RCIDA Board draws its members from key areas of the county's business and industrial community, with executives representing finance, public utilities, construction, telecommunications, agriculture, development, education, and business . The RCIDA assists businesses with workforce hiring and training opportunities, locating available state and local incentives, channeling building permits, providing statistical information, securing quality service accounts, and delivering a whole range of other services .

The RCIDA has been successful in securing federal funding to support its economic development efforts. In December 2024, U.S. Representative Morgan Griffith announced that USDA Rural Development had awarded the Russell County IDA a $96,727 grant to support an existing loan fund to create new jobs . The funding was made available through the USDA Rural Development Rural Business Development Grants Program, which directs funds to promote economic development and job creation projects .

### Wise County Industrial Development Authority

The Wise County Industrial Development Authority (WCIDA) serves as the primary organization responsible for economic and industrial development in Wise County, working to support existing industry and new industrial development efforts . The IDA is supported by the Wise County Office of Economic & Industrial Development, with Ms. Mullins serving as secretary to the Wise County Industrial Development Authority .

The WCIDA has been instrumental in the development of the Lonesome Pine Regional Business and Technology Park, one of the premiere economic development locations in the region, home to several businesses and hundreds of workers . The IDA, in partnership with the Wise County Office of Economic & Industrial Development, has undertaken a branding and marketing plan that includes a new branding logo, new marketing materials, a new dedicated economic development website, and new signage at the technology park, with VCEDA providing a $50,000 grant for the signage project .

The WCIDA has been actively engaged in site development to attract new businesses to the region. In March 2024, VCEDA approved up to a $700,000 grant and a $300,000 loan to assist with site development of the WCIDA's Elam Farm property, comprised of some 203 acres adjacent to the Lonesome Pine Regional Business and Technology Park . The multi-phase development of the Elam Farm site is a critical piece of the future growth and investment in Wise County, and once complete will add a highly attractive and marketable 65-acre site to the technology park, allowing Wise County to be much more competitive for projects in target sectors .

The development of the Elam Farm site has been supported by a range of federal and state funding sources. The IDA obtained a $1.854 million award from the Virginia Department of Energy AMLER program for access road improvements and utility infrastructure extensions, a federal HUD Community Development Grant in the amount of $1 million, and a $750,000 grant from the Virginia Economic Development Partnership Business Ready Sites Program . VCEDA's support for the project has been long-standing, including an $834,250 loan to purchase the property in 2013 .

### How These Organizations Support the Region's Economic Development

For a plan implementor, the project coordinators and economic development organizations identified in this section provide the essential leadership, coordination, and support needed to navigate the complex landscape of economic development in the US 19 corridor. The Mount Rogers Planning District Commission provides regional planning services that support economic development in the counties adjacent to the VCEDA region, while the Cumberland Plateau Planning District Commission provides direct support to the coalfield counties at the heart of the region's economic transformation .

The Industrial Development Authorities serve as the primary local economic development organizations, providing site development, building acquisition and renovation, financing, and workforce development services . The Dickenson County IDA's work on the small business incubator project at the Dickenson Center for Education and Research demonstrates the importance of supporting entrepreneurship and small business growth in the region . The Russell County IDA's success in securing federal funding and its comprehensive approach to business support demonstrates the effectiveness of local economic development efforts . The Wise County IDA's work on the Elam Farm site development and the Lonesome Pine Regional Business and Technology Park demonstrates the importance of site development in attracting new businesses .

The collaboration between these organizations and VCEDA is essential to the success of the region's economic development efforts. VCEDA provides the financing and technical assistance that enables IDAs and PDCs to undertake projects that support economic growth . The partnership between the public and private sectors is essential to the success of the region's economic transformation, with the public sector providing the financing and infrastructure that enable private companies to invest and grow.

# Chapter Twelve: Resource Guide - Banks, Lenders, Coordinators, Tools, and Government Programs

## Section 12.4: Workforce Training and Education

### The Foundation of Economic Transformation: A Skilled Workforce

The successful transformation of the US 19 corridor's economy depends fundamentally on the availability of a skilled workforce capable of meeting the demands of the region's emerging industries. The investments in manufacturing, energy production, agriculture, and technology that characterize the region's economic development strategy require workers with specialized skills in welding, machining, industrial maintenance, healthcare, information technology, and renewable energy. The workforce training and education organizations identified in this section provide the essential infrastructure for developing this skilled workforce, offering training programs, certifications, and educational opportunities that prepare workers for the jobs of the future.

The workforce training ecosystem in the US 19 corridor is characterized by strong partnerships between educational institutions, economic development organizations, and employers, ensuring that training programs are aligned with the needs of the region's businesses. The Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority (VCEDA) has been a critical partner in this effort, providing substantial funding for workforce development through its Coalfield Workforce Development and Training Fund. Since the initial $500,000 grant approved in 2018, VCEDA has provided more than $1.2 million in grants to the Mountain Empire Community College (MECC) Foundation, supporting the training of approximately 1,313 students who have completed 1,426 certifications and training courses to meet workforce needs in the region .

The workforce training programs supported by these organizations have benefited a wide range of employers in the region, including Scott County Telephone Cooperative, VFP, Komatsu, Ballad Health, Pike Electric, and many more . These employers have created hundreds of jobs in the Southwest Virginia region, building long-term, sustainable employment and a renewed economy for the coalfield region. The training programs have been particularly valuable for workers seeking to transition from declining industries to growing sectors, providing the skills needed for new careers in manufacturing, healthcare, and technology.

For a plan implementor, the workforce training and education organizations identified in this section provide the essential resources for developing the skilled workforce needed for the region's economic transformation. The organizations offer a range of programs, from basic skills training to advanced certifications, enabling workers to build the skills needed for the jobs created by the region's new and expanding industries. The partnerships between these organizations and employers ensure that the training provided is relevant to the needs of the region's businesses, and the funding provided by VCEDA and other sources ensures that training is accessible to workers who might otherwise face financial barriers.

### Mountain Empire Community College (MECC) Foundation

The Mountain Empire Community College (MECC) Foundation serves as the primary fundraising and grant administration arm for Mountain Empire Community College, which is a two-year college with a main campus located at Big Stone Gap in Wise County, Virginia . The Foundation has been a critical partner in the region's workforce development efforts, receiving substantial funding from VCEDA and other sources to support training programs that prepare workers for the region's emerging industries.

The MECC Foundation has been the recipient of more than $1.2 million in grants from VCEDA since an initial $500,000 grant approved in 2018 . These grants have supported workforce development and training in high-need manufacturing, technology, and healthcare sectors, with the funds used for VCEDA region resident scholarships, tuition, training instructional costs, retraining costs, and customized workforce training for area businesses . The most recent grant, an up to $200,000 grant approved in June 2025, will support training and certification needs for residents in the VCEDA service territory for the next two years .

The impact of the VCEDA workforce training grants has been substantial. Since the initial grant in 2018, approximately 1,313 students have completed 1,426 certifications and training courses to meet workforce needs in the region . The training programs have been particularly valuable for workers seeking to enter high-demand fields, with many students obtaining nationally recognized credentials and professional licensures in their fields. The MECC Foundation has noted that the costs of these certifications are often a financial barrier for students, and the grant funding removes that barrier, enabling students to complete their training and enter the workforce .

The MECC Foundation has also received funding from the Appalachian Regional Commission (ARC) through its INSPIRE (Investments Supporting Partnerships in Recovery Ecosystems) Initiative. In September 2025, ARC awarded the MECC Foundation a $491,548 grant to support Project Southwest Virginia Opioid Abuse Recovery (SOAR), which helps those impacted by substance use disorder and trains prospective candidates for careers in skilled trades . Participants in the Project SOAR program receive hands-on training in fields such as carpentry, welding, electrical and plumbing, with 180 participants impacted by substance use disorder prepared for careers in skilled trades and other in-demand occupations across Dickenson, Lee, Scott, and Wise counties .

The MECC Foundation has also received ARC funding through the INSPIRE initiative for the Amelioration Expansion (AM-EX) Project, with a $493,669 grant awarded in September 2023 . Through this funding, about 40 workers/trainees and 60 students will obtain employment, work-based training, and/or internships in Dickenson, Lee, and Wise counties, while also receiving wrap-around services for their substance use disorder . These grants demonstrate the Foundation's capacity to secure federal funding for workforce development and its commitment to serving the region's most vulnerable populations.

MECC anticipates training 500 or more students in 2025-2026, with existing industries expanding their employment offerings . The college offers a range of programs relevant to the region's economic development strategy, including programs in welding, machining, industrial maintenance, healthcare, and information technology. The college works with employers to develop customized training programs that meet their specific needs, ensuring that workers have the skills required for the jobs created by new and expanding businesses.

### Dickenson Center for Education and Research (DCER)

The Dickenson Center for Education and Research (DCER) in Clintwood, Virginia, serves as a multi-purpose educational and research facility that is central to Dickenson County's economic development strategy. The DCER provides a range of services, including workforce training, educational programs, and small business incubator space, supporting the county's efforts to diversify its economy and create new opportunities for residents.

The DCER has been the recipient of significant VCEDA funding to support its workforce development and small business incubation activities. In December 2025, VCEDA approved a $42,500 grant to the Dickenson County Industrial Development Authority to assist in the development of a small business incubator space at the DCER . The funds will be used to make improvements to 3,200 square feet of unused space at the DCER and for workforce and small business development and training as the Dickenson County Community Business Launch program is started .

The Community Business Launch program is designed to foster local entrepreneurship by constructing small business incubator space and offering comprehensive support services to assist small businesses going through the incubator program, with capacity for approximately five businesses . The program, which is expected to launch in spring 2026, will include a business pitch competition component with monetary support and free incubator space for one year for the winners of the pitch competition . The IDA plans to use Opportunity SWVA to run the programmatic piece of the initiative and has recently hired a new full-time community marketing specialist to oversee the day-to-day operations and serve as the first point of contact for business incubator participants .

The Dickenson County Community Business Launch program is open to Dickenson County residents who have a business idea, have started a business and need guidance or access to capital, or are interested in learning more about entrepreneurship . Participants receive free, hands-on business training, coaching and technical assistance, an opportunity to compete for up to $60,000 in cash and in-kind awards, access to small business resources and mentorship, and potential access to rent-free incubator space at the DCER . The program consists of six weeks of weekly classes, culminating in a live pitch competition where up to five awards are available, with the top three winners receiving access to incubator space at DCER for one year .

The DCER has also received VCEDA funding for workforce training programs. In June 2025, VCEDA approved a $20,000 grant to support the addition of welding classes to the offerings at the DCER . The welding classes provide hands-on training in a high-demand skill that is essential for the region's manufacturing and construction industries, preparing workers for jobs that offer competitive wages and benefits.

### Appalachian Council for Innovation (ACI)

The Appalachian Council for Innovation (ACI) is a nonprofit regional economic development organization founded in 1999 as the Southwestern Virginia Technology Council . ACI promotes Southwest Virginia through the lens of regionalism, cooperation, and education, working with businesses, institutions, and individuals from the Wytheville region to the far western corner of Virginia . The organization includes businesses and organizations in 16 Southwest Virginia cities and counties, including Bristol and the counties of Wythe, Russell, Wise, and Buchanan .

The organization's mission is to lead Southwestern Virginians in addressing technology issues for business, education, and government with the purpose of enhancing the vitality and social well-being of Southwestern Virginia . The Council's membership includes businesses, institutions, and individuals from across the region, reflecting its broad-based approach to economic development. The organization's activities include workforce development initiatives, entrepreneurship support, and regional collaboration efforts.

ACI has been the recipient of significant VCEDA funding for workforce development initiatives. In May 2025, VCEDA approved an up to $129,080 grant to the Appalachian Council for Innovation to implement a Simulated Workforce pilot program in the Southwest Virginia VCEDA region . The Simulated Workforce pilot program is designed to provide workers with hands-on training in a simulated work environment, preparing them for the demands of the region's emerging industries. The program provides a bridge between classroom training and actual employment, enabling workers to develop the skills needed for manufacturing, technology, and healthcare jobs.

In addition to the Simulated Workforce program, ACI has been active in promoting entrepreneurship and small business development in the region. The organization has been involved in the Entrepreneur Challenge program, a competitive process for potential and emerging entrepreneurs that provides business training, mentorship, networking opportunities, and an improved level of coordinated resources and community support . Since 2012, the Southwest Virginia region has held 36 competitions with over 290 program graduates, demonstrating the region's commitment to supporting entrepreneurship .

ACI is led by Executive Director John Bebber, a U.S. Navy veteran with degrees from King University and Appalachian State University . Bebber was hired in January 2022 and has emphasized the importance of promoting regionalism, cooperation, and growth among businesses, localities, schools, and communities . His leadership has been focused on showing the region's youth that they can grow up and build a life in Southwest Virginia without having to sacrifice prosperity or success .

### Opportunity SWVA

Opportunity SWVA is a regional economic development initiative that provides resources and support for entrepreneurs, businesses, and communities in Southwest Virginia. The organization focuses on building entrepreneurial ecosystems, supporting small business development, and promoting economic diversification in the region. Opportunity SWVA works with a range of partners, including VCEDA, the Appalachian Regional Commission, and local economic development organizations, to provide the resources and support needed for business success.

One of Opportunity SWVA's key programs is the Entrepreneur Challenge, a competitive process for potential and emerging entrepreneurs that provides business training, mentorship, networking opportunities, and an improved level of coordinated resources and community support . The competitions raise awareness of Southwest Virginia entrepreneurial opportunities and typically offer investment grants to top competitors following a pitch event and submission of a business plan. Since 2012, the Southwest Virginia region has held 36 competitions with over 290 program graduates .

Opportunity SWVA also administers the Industrial Revitalization Fund (IRF), which leverages local and private resources to achieve market-driven redevelopment of vacant and deteriorated industrial and commercial properties . The program is targeted toward vacant industrial structures whose poor condition creates physical and economic blight to the surrounding area. Eligible properties include those formerly used for manufacturing, warehousing, mining, transportation, and power production, as well as large-scale white elephant structures such as department stores, theaters, hotels, and shopping centers . The maximum level of funding is $500,000 .

The organization provides an Entrepreneur Challenge Toolkit to help communities host their own business plan competitions, making it easier for localities to support entrepreneurial development . The toolkit provides guidance on the process of organizing a competition, including training, mentorship, and resource coordination. Communities across Southwest Virginia are joining the trend of supporting entrepreneurial development by hosting business plan competitions, demonstrating the region's commitment to entrepreneurship as a driver of economic growth .

Opportunity SWVA is playing a key role in the Dickenson County Community Business Launch program, running the programmatic piece of the initiative at the Dickenson Center for Education and Research . The organization's expertise in entrepreneurship and small business development is being leveraged to provide comprehensive support to the businesses housed in the DCER incubator space, including training, workshops, and meetings, as well as covering participant curriculum fees, workbooks, facilitators, and staff support .

### How These Organizations Support the Region's Economic Development

For a plan implementor, the workforce training and education organizations identified in this section provide the essential infrastructure for developing the skilled workforce needed for the region's economic transformation. The Mountain Empire Community College Foundation and Southwest Virginia Community College provide the training programs and certifications that prepare workers for the region's emerging industries, with substantial funding from VCEDA and the Appalachian Regional Commission . The Dickenson Center for Education and Research provides the physical space for workforce training and small business incubation, supporting entrepreneurship and job creation in Dickenson County .

The Appalachian Council for Innovation provides the regional coordination and innovation leadership that supports workforce development and economic diversification across 16 Southwest Virginia cities and counties . The Council's Simulated Workforce pilot program provides a bridge between classroom training and actual employment, preparing workers for the demands of the region's emerging industries . Opportunity SWVA provides the entrepreneurial ecosystem support that enables small businesses to start and grow, with a track record of 36 competitions and over 290 program graduates since 2012 .

The collaboration between these organizations and VCEDA is essential to the success of the region's workforce development efforts. VCEDA provides the funding that enables these organizations to offer training and support programs, while the organizations provide the expertise and infrastructure needed to deliver effective training . The partnership between the public and private sectors is essential to the success of the region's workforce development strategy, with employers providing input on the skills needed and training providers developing programs that meet those needs.

The workforce training programs supported by these organizations have a proven track record of success, with approximately 1,313 students completing 1,426 certifications and training courses since 2018 . The employers who have benefited from this supported training include Scott County Telephone Cooperative, VFP, Komatsu, Ballad Health, and Pike Electric, among others, demonstrating the relevance of the training to the region's business needs . The workforce training and education organizations thus provide the essential foundation for the region's economic transformation, ensuring that the workers needed for the region's new and expanding industries are available and prepared.

# Chapter Twelve: Resource Guide - Banks, Lenders, Coordinators, Tools, and Government Programs

## Section 12.5: Tools and Infrastructure — A Resource Guide

### Introduction: The Infrastructure Foundation for Economic Growth

The successful transformation of the US 19 corridor depends on modern, efficient, and well-maintained infrastructure. This section provides a practical guide to the key planning tools, infrastructure projects, and connectivity initiatives that enable economic development in the region. These resources facilitate safer travel, more efficient transportation of goods and people, and the digital connectivity essential for modern business operations.

---

### US 19 Corridor Study (VDOT)

The U.S. 19 corridor study represents a critical planning tool for improving safety, traffic flow, and multimodal access along this vital transportation artery. The study is a direct response to documented safety risks that affect motorists, pedestrians, and cyclists alike. It is being funded by a substantial **$1.275 million federal RAISE Grant**, reflecting the corridor's importance to regional and federal transportation priorities.

The study is designed to be comprehensive, evaluating the corridor's current safety and operational performance to develop a package of improvements. Key areas of focus include the installation and repair of sidewalks, the creation of designated bike lanes, and the implementation of enhanced traffic management strategies. The ultimate goal is to produce a unified plan that ensures all future work along the corridor is coordinated to achieve the best possible outcome, rather than being completed in isolated, piecemeal segments. Projects resulting from the study are expected to be at least two years away.

---

### Transportation Modeling: Synchro/HCS

Transportation modeling tools such as Synchro and HCS (Highway Capacity Software) are essential for analyzing and optimizing traffic flow along complex corridors like US 19. These "HCM-based" (mesoscopic) software packages provide transportation engineers with the analytical capability to model existing conditions, identify bottlenecks, and test potential improvements before they are built.

*   **Synchro:** This software is the recommended tool for analyzing arterial roadways, where the Level of Service (LOS) is largely determined by delay at intersections. It is widely used for signal coordination projects to develop optimal signal timing plans that reduce delays, stops, and congestion. Synchro can evaluate a variety of intersection types, including signalized, unsignalized, and roundabouts, and integrates with SimTraffic for more detailed microsimulation.
*   **HCS:** This is another standard HCM-based tool that is recommended for segment analysis (like freeways and multi-lane highways) and can evaluate specific interchange types including diamond, partial cloverleaf, and DDI. Both tools are critical for ensuring that infrastructure improvements are based on rigorous, data-driven analysis.

---

### Broadband Expansion: Six New Fiber-Supported Cell Towers

While information on specific cell tower sites is limited, broadband expansion is a recognized priority for economic development and quality of life in rural parts of Virginia. Such projects are often complex and lengthy, involving work by multiple entities on utility poles and rights-of-way.

Local governments and private partners often work together on these initiatives to bridge the digital divide, as seen in other Virginia counties where similar projects use federal and state funds to deploy fiber infrastructure. These efforts aim to attract businesses and residents, with county officials drawing comparisons to the electrification of rural areas in the 1930s.

---

### Beckley Z-Way Project: A $110 Million Infrastructure Investment

The Beckley Z-Way is a major infrastructure project designed to alleviate severe congestion in one of West Virginia's most densely populated cities. The project involves constructing a **5.5-mile stretch of new highway** that will run from Shady Spring, through Beaver, and into Beckley. With a price tag of **approximately $111 million**, this is a transformative investment for the region. The funding is provided through the governor's Roads to Prosperity program.

The project is divided into two main sections for construction:
1.  **Section One (Shady Spring to Beaver):** This 3.6-mile section involves widening and converting a roadway to improve traffic flow. The work is being managed by Mountaineer Contractors, Inc..
2.  **Section Two (Beaver to South Eisenhower):** This 1.93-mile section includes the construction of nearly two miles of new roadway, a new overpass bridge, and connector roads. The complex work, which includes stream realignment, is being performed by Kanawha Stone Company.

The project is intended to be a major connector for the area, reducing congestion for the thousands of daily commuters and providing improved access for the region's growing tourism sector. Construction is expected to be completed by the spring of 2028.

---

### Coalfields Expressway: Additional 2.74 Miles Opened

The Coalfields Expressway (CFX) is a planned major transportation corridor that will dramatically improve connectivity across Southwest Virginia, West Virginia, Tennessee, and Kentucky. A significant milestone was achieved in October 2025 with the opening of a **2.74-mile portion of the expressway in Buchanan County**.

This new segment, known as Poplar Creek Phase A, is a limited-access, four-lane highway that is a part of the larger Corridor Q project. Its completion enhances transportation access and is expected to spur economic development in the area.

The project involved immense earth-moving work, with over 12 million cubic yards of material moved, and features the longest and deepest triple precast box culvert in Virginia. This section opened just 0.07 miles short of its initially projected 2.81-mile length, with the final 2.07-mile segment of Corridor Q (Phase B) in Virginia scheduled to open in late 2027. The completed corridor will provide a modern highway linking the Kentucky state line to Christiansburg.

---

### Summary Table: Key Tools and Infrastructure Projects

| :--- | :--- | :--- |

| **Broadband Expansion** | Deployment of fiber-supported cell towers to improve rural connectivity and support economic growth. | Ongoing, multi-year initiative. |

# Chapter Twelve: Resource Guide - Banks, Lenders, Coordinators, Tools, and Government Programs

## Section 12.6: Key Legislative and Policy Tools

### Introduction: The Legal Framework for Economic Transformation

The transformation of the US 19 corridor from a coal-dependent economy to a diversified, sustainable economic ecosystem is fundamentally enabled by a robust legal and policy framework that provides the authority, funding mechanisms, and regulatory pathways for economic development projects. The Virginia Code contains a series of statutes that establish the Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority (VCEDA), define its purpose and powers, and provide the mechanisms for financing economic development projects in the region. These statutes also establish the regulatory framework for energy projects, including pumped storage hydropower, and provide the cost recovery mechanisms that make large-scale energy investments financially viable.

For a plan implementor, understanding these legislative and policy tools is essential to navigating the regulatory landscape and securing the approvals and financing needed for economic development projects. The statutes identified in this section provide the legal foundation for VCEDA's work, establish the boundaries of the coalfield region, and authorize the cost recovery that enables utilities to invest in pumped storage hydropower. These tools are not merely legal technicalities; they are the mechanisms that make the region's economic transformation possible.

---

### Virginia Code § 56-585.1: Electric Utility Regulation; Pumped Hydro Cost Recovery

Virginia Code § 56-585.1, as amended by the 2017 legislation (HB 1760 and SB 1418), provides the critical regulatory mechanism that enables investor-owned electric utilities to recover the costs of developing pumped hydroelectricity generation and storage facilities in the coalfield region . The statute authorizes an investor-owned electric utility to petition the State Corporation Commission (SCC) for approval of a rate adjustment clause for recovery of the costs of one or more pumped hydroelectricity generation and storage facilities .

The statute includes several key provisions that are essential to understanding the regulatory framework for PSH development. First, the facilities must "utilize associated on-site or off-site renewable energy resources as all or a portion of their power source" . This requirement ensures that PSH facilities contribute to Virginia's clean energy goals and are not simply an extension of the fossil fuel economy. The renewable energy resources must be located in the coalfield region of the Commonwealth, creating a direct link between PSH development and renewable energy investment in the region.

Second, the measure provides that the requirement that a utility demonstrate that it has considered and weighed alternative options, including third-party market alternatives, in its selection process applies only to a generation facility that is described in clauses (i) and (ii) of subdivision A 6 of § 56-585.1 . This provision streamlines the regulatory process for PSH facilities, recognizing that the unique characteristics of pumped storage make it difficult to compare directly with other generation options.

The cost recovery mechanism provided by § 56-585.1 is essential to making PSH projects financially viable for utilities. The construction of a PSH facility requires substantial upfront capital investment—the proposed Tazewell County project is estimated at approximately $2 billion—and the return on this investment would be realized over the 50-year operational life of the facility. Without a mechanism to recover these costs through utility rates, the financial risk of PSH development would be prohibitive for most utilities.

The significance of this statute for the US 19 corridor's economic transformation cannot be overstated. By providing a clear regulatory pathway for cost recovery, § 56-585.1 removes a major barrier to PSH development and enables the utility investment that will fund the construction of the Tazewell County facility. The statute is a critical policy tool that supports the region's energy transition and the development of the energy storage infrastructure needed for regional energy independence.

---

### Virginia Code § 15.2-6002: Purpose of Authority; Performs Governmental Function

Virginia Code § 15.2-6002 establishes the primary purpose of the Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority and declares that the Authority performs a governmental function . The statute states that the Authority was created by the Virginia General Assembly to enhance the economic base for the seven county and one city coalfield region of Virginia, which includes Lee, Wise, Scott, Buchanan, Russell, Tazewell, and Dickenson counties and the City of Norton .

The statute further provides that "the exercise of the powers granted by this chapter shall be in all respects for the benefit of the inhabitants of the Commonwealth, particularly the aforesaid seven county and one city areas, for the increase of their commerce, and for the promotion of their safety, health, welfare, convenience and prosperity" . This provision establishes the public purpose of VCEDA's activities and provides the legal justification for the Authority's use of public funds for economic development purposes.

The declaration that VCEDA performs a governmental function is essential to the Authority's operations, as it provides the legal basis for the Authority's powers and immunities. The governmental function designation enables VCEDA to exercise its powers in the public interest, to enter into contracts, to acquire and dispose of property, and to apply for and accept grants and loans from federal agencies . The declaration also provides the legal foundation for VCEDA's financing activities, including its loans and grants to businesses and industrial development authorities.

The definition of the coalfield region in § 15.2-6002 is also significant, as it establishes the geographic boundaries of VCEDA's jurisdiction. The seven-county, one-city region includes the counties that have been most affected by the decline of the coal industry and that are the focus of the region's economic transformation strategy. The geographic boundaries ensure that VCEDA's resources are targeted to the communities that need them most and that the benefits of economic development are distributed across the region.

---

### Virginia Code § 56.2-6002: Coalfield Region Definition

Virginia Code § 56.2-6002 defines the coalfield region for the purposes of the Virginia Electric Utility Regulation Act and other statutes that reference the region. While the search results do not provide the full text of this specific section, the Virginia Code contains a definition of the coalfield region that is consistent with the definition in § 15.2-6002. The coalfield region includes the seven counties of Lee, Wise, Scott, Buchanan, Russell, Tazewell, and Dickenson, and the City of Norton .

The definition of the coalfield region is significant for several reasons. First, it establishes the geographic boundaries for the application of statutes that provide special provisions for the region, including the cost recovery provisions for pumped hydroelectricity generation and storage facilities . Second, it ensures that the benefits of economic development programs are targeted to the communities that have been most affected by the decline of the coal industry. Third, it provides clarity for businesses and investors seeking to locate in the region, ensuring that they understand the geographic scope of the incentives and programs available.

The definition of the coalfield region is also significant for the Appalachian Regional Commission's POWER Initiative, which directs federal resources to coal-impacted communities. The POWER Initiative's focus on coal-impacted counties aligns with the Virginia coalfield region, ensuring that the region is eligible for the federal funding that supports the region's economic transformation. The alignment of state and federal definitions ensures that the region can access a comprehensive range of resources for economic development.

---

### Additional Statutory Context

The statutory framework for economic development in the coalfield region extends beyond the statutes explicitly identified in this section. Virginia Code § 15.2-6003 establishes the composition of VCEDA's Board and the terms of its members, providing the governance structure for the Authority . Virginia Code § 15.2-6006 grants VCEDA a range of powers, including the power to enter into contractual agreements, to acquire and dispose of property, to apply for and accept grants and loans from federal agencies, and to engage in economic development marketing and business attraction activities .

The statutes also provide for the Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Fund, which receives one-fourth of the revenues from the local gas road improvement and Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority tax . This funding mechanism ensures that the Authority has a dedicated revenue stream for its economic development activities, reducing its dependence on annual appropriations from the General Assembly.

The legislative framework is also dynamic, with the General Assembly regularly updating the statutes to address emerging needs and opportunities. The extension of the local gas road improvement and Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority tax from January 1, 2026, to January 1, 2028, through HB 1579 (2025), demonstrates the legislature's commitment to supporting the region's economic development . The extension ensures that the Authority continues to have access to the funding it needs to support economic development projects in the region.

---

### How to Use These Legislative Tools

For a plan implementor seeking to use the legislative and policy tools identified in this section, the first step is to understand the specific statutes that apply to their project. For energy projects, including pumped storage hydropower, § 56-585.1 provides the regulatory pathway for cost recovery, and the implementor should work with the utility and the State Corporation Commission to ensure compliance with the statute's requirements . The implementor should also ensure that the project is located in the coalfield region, as defined in § 56.2-6002 and § 15.2-6002, to qualify for the provisions of the statute.

For economic development projects, the implementor should work with VCEDA to understand the Authority's powers and the funding mechanisms available under Chapter 60 of Title 15.2. The implementor should also be familiar with § 15.2-6006, which grants VCEDA the power to enter into contractual agreements, to acquire and dispose of property, and to apply for and accept grants and loans from federal agencies . The implementor should also be aware of the funding mechanisms provided by the Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Fund, which receives one-fourth of the revenues from the local gas road improvement and Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority tax .

The implementor should also be aware of the broader legislative context, including the Virginia Electric Utility Regulation Act (Chapter 23 of Title 56) and the Electric Utility Integrated Resource Planning provisions (Chapter 24 of Title 56) . These statutes establish the regulatory framework for electric utilities and provide the context for the cost recovery provisions of § 56-585.1. The implementor should also monitor legislative developments, as the General Assembly regularly updates the statutes to address emerging needs and opportunities.

| Statute | Key Provision | Relevance to Plan Implementor |
|---|---|---|
| **§ 56-585.1** | Authorizes cost recovery for pumped hydroelectricity generation and storage facilities in the coalfield region | Provides the regulatory pathway for PSH cost recovery; enables utility investment in PSH projects |
| **§ 15.2-6002** | Establishes the purpose of VCEDA and declares that the Authority performs a governmental function | Provides the legal foundation for VCEDA's economic development activities; establishes the coalfield region's boundaries |
| **§ 56.2-6002** | Defines the coalfield region | Establishes the geographic boundaries for statutes that provide special provisions for the region |
| **§ 15.2-6003** | Establishes the composition of VCEDA's Board | Provides the governance structure for VCEDA; ensures representation of key stakeholders |
| **§ 15.2-6006** | Grants VCEDA the powers to enter contracts, acquire property, and accept grants | Provides the legal authority for VCEDA's economic development activities |
| **§ 58.1-3713** | Establishes the local gas road improvement and VCEDA tax | Provides funding for VCEDA's economic development activities |

---

### Conclusion: A Legal Foundation for Economic Transformation

The legislative and policy tools identified in this section provide the legal foundation for the US 19 corridor's economic transformation, enabling the development of pumped storage hydropower, the operation of the Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority, and the targeting of resources to the coalfield region. These tools are not merely legal technicalities; they are the mechanisms that make the region's economic transformation possible. The cost recovery provisions of § 56-585.1 enable the utility investment that will fund the Tazewell County PSH project, while the enabling legislation of Chapter 60 of Title 15.2 provides VCEDA with the authority and funding it needs to support economic development .

For a plan implementor, understanding these legislative tools is essential to navigating the regulatory landscape and securing the approvals and financing needed for economic development projects. The implementor should work with VCEDA and other stakeholders to ensure compliance with the statutes and to leverage the opportunities they provide. The implementor should also monitor legislative developments, as the General Assembly regularly updates the statutes to address emerging needs and opportunities . By understanding and leveraging these legislative tools, the implementor can help to build the legal and regulatory infrastructure for the region's economic transformation.

---
# Chapter Twelve: Resource Guide - Technology Integration with LOWL

## Introduction: The LOWL Advantage for Regional Organizations

The transformation of the US 19 corridor's economy depends on modernizing the operations of the very organizations tasked with building that future. The Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority (VCEDA), planning district commissions, industrial development authorities, and workforce training institutions all manage physical infrastructure, complex projects, and sensitive data. Integrating LOWL-based systems into their operations can enhance security, improve efficiency, and enable new capabilities through low-level device control, encryption, and mesh networking.

LOWL (Low-Level Language) provides a unique foundation for these enhancements. Originally designed as a portable systems programming language that compiles to low-level targets with direct hardware access, LOWL enables developers to write code that interacts directly with sensors, actuators, and communication hardware without the overhead of higher-level operating systems . The language's ability to map unambiguously to operations on real machines makes it ideal for embedded systems and critical infrastructure where reliability and performance are paramount .

Modern security threats require robust defenses. End-to-end encrypted mesh network protocols like IronMesh demonstrate how LOWL-based systems can provide zero-configuration, offline-capable secure communication between devices using XSalsa20-Poly1305 encryption and X25519 ECDH key exchange . These capabilities are essential for protecting critical infrastructure and sensitive data across the US 19 corridor's distributed networks.

The following sections detail how each organization from Chapter 12 can leverage LOWL technology to enhance their operations.

---

## Virginia Coalfield Economic Development Authority (VCEDA)

### Project Management and Infrastructure Monitoring

VCEDA manages loans, grants, and infrastructure projects across seven counties and one city. LOWL-based systems can provide real-time monitoring of infrastructure projects, tracking progress, equipment utilization, and safety conditions.

- **Construction Site Monitoring**: Deploy LOWL-powered sensor nodes on construction equipment and at project sites to monitor vibration, temperature, and structural integrity. These systems use LOWL's hardware access features for direct sensor I/O over SPI and I2C, with data transmitted through encrypted mesh networks . The mesh ensures data reaches monitoring stations even when cellular coverage is unavailable.

- **Energy System Monitoring**: For the PSH facility and other energy infrastructure, LOWL can implement low-latency control systems that respond to grid conditions in real-time. The decentralized robust optimal tracking control algorithms developed for robotic manipulators can be adapted for PSH turbine control, with LOWL providing the deterministic execution needed for safety-critical systems .

- **Safety and Security**: Implement low-power intrusion detection systems using LOWL's `#[interrupt]` attributes for immediate response to security events. Systems can be deployed on GSM-connected devices that transmit alerts to VCEDA's security operations center through encrypted channels .

### Implementation Guidance

VCEDA should develop a technical roadmap for IoT deployment, identifying priority infrastructure for monitoring, selecting mesh network protocols that provide end-to-end encryption, and establishing data governance policies. The Authority can work with embedded systems developers to create LOWL firmware for sensor nodes and implement verification layers that ensure code correctness before production deployment .

The $11 million AMLER program funds can support technology integration, with a portion of future grants dedicated to smart infrastructure investments . Leveraging partners like Appalachian Council for Innovation can provide expertise in embedded systems programming and mesh network deployment.

---

## LENOWISCO Planning District Commission

### Regional Infrastructure Coordination

LENOWISCO coordinates transportation, broadband, and community development across Lee, Scott, Wise, and Norton. LOWL-based systems can enhance regional planning through real-time data collection and analysis.

- **Broadband and Connectivity Monitoring**: Deploy LOWL-powered network monitoring nodes that assess broadband performance across the region. These devices run LOWL firmware with the `lowLevelDevice` capability for direct network interface control, measuring latency, packet loss, and throughput . Data is transmitted via encrypted mesh networks between monitoring nodes and regional planning offices.

- **Brownfield and Infrastructure Tracking**: Implement LOWL-based sensors on brownfield sites to monitor environmental conditions, groundwater quality, and structural stability. The systems use low-power, offline-capable mesh networks to ensure data collection continues during power outages and connectivity disruptions .

- **Transportation Data Collection**: LOWL can power traffic counting and road condition monitoring devices that integrate with VDOT's Synchro and HCS modeling tools. Real-time data from these devices can improve the accuracy of transportation models and enable dynamic traffic management .

### Implementation Guidance

LENOWISCO should prioritize pilot deployments in areas with limited connectivity, using mesh networks to bridge coverage gaps. The Commission can work with Virginia's broadband office to ensure interoperability with existing infrastructure. The $545 million BEAD program can support these investments, with LOWL devices providing local connectivity where fiber is not yet deployed .

---

## Cumberland Plateau Planning District Commission

### Industrial Park and Infrastructure Management

The CPPDC manages industrial parks and infrastructure projects across Buchanan, Dickenson, Russell, and Tazewell. LOWL technology can enhance site management and security.

- **Industrial Park Security**: Deploy LOWL-powered security systems using low-power sensors, motion detectors, and license plate recognition cameras. These systems operate on self-healing mesh networks with XSalsa20-Poly1305 encryption, providing end-to-end security without cloud dependency .

- **Utility Infrastructure Monitoring**: LOWL systems can monitor water, sewer, and electrical infrastructure across the district. Sensors measure flow rates, pressure, power quality, and detect leaks or failures. Devices operate on encrypted mesh networks ensuring data integrity and security .

- **Asset Tracking and Management**: Implement LOWL-based asset tracking for equipment, vehicles, and materials. Systems use low-power GPS and LoRa communication to provide real-time location data through encrypted mesh networks, operating without internet or cellular connectivity .

### Implementation Guidance

The CPPDC should leverage its established relationships with VCEDA and federal programs to secure funding for technology integration. The $500 million in grants and loans received by the district over its history demonstrates capacity to manage large infrastructure projects, including technology investments .

---

## Mount Rogers Planning District Commission

### Regional Development Technology

MRPDC coordinates development across Bland, Carroll, Grayson, Smyth, Washington, and Wythe. LOWL-based systems can enhance regional planning through data collection and environmental monitoring.

- **Environmental Monitoring**: Deploy LOWL-powered environmental sensors that measure air quality, water quality, and weather conditions. Systems use self-contained, solar-powered nodes communicating via encrypted mesh networks, providing data for regional planning without requiring cloud infrastructure .

- **Rural Infrastructure Management**: Implement LOWL-based systems for monitoring rural roads, bridges, and culverts. Sensors detect structural issues, frost heave, and flooding, with data transmitted through mesh networks to regional planning offices. The offline capability ensures data collection continues during emergencies .

- **Community Development Data**: LOWL can power community development data collection, tracking housing availability, business growth, and demographic changes. Systems use secure, encrypted communication to protect sensitive data while enabling informed planning decisions .

### Implementation Guidance

The MRPDC should leverage its circuit-riding town management assistance to deploy LOWL-based monitoring systems across member jurisdictions. The Commission's GIS capabilities can integrate with sensor data to provide comprehensive regional mapping. ARC POWER Initiative funding can support pilot deployments .

## Practical Guidance for Technology Integration

### Encryption and Mesh Networking

The IronMesh protocol demonstrates how LOWL-based systems can implement zero-configuration, end-to-end encrypted communication without cloud dependency . Key features include:

- **X25519 ECDH Key Exchange**: Provides secure session establishment with forward secrecy
- **XSalsa20-Poly1305 Encryption**: Provides authenticated encryption for all data transmission
- **Ed25519 Signatures**: Ensure message authenticity and integrity
- **mDNS Discovery**: Enables zero-configuration device discovery on local networks
- **Offline Operation**: All communication occurs without internet connectivity

Organizations should deploy IronMesh-compatible devices that run LOWL firmware, enabling secure, encrypted communication across their networks. This ensures data integrity and prevents unauthorized access even in challenging conditions.

### Implementation Steps

1. **Assessment**: Identify infrastructure and operations that would benefit from real-time monitoring, automation, or security enhancement
2. **Pilot**: Deploy small-scale LOWL-based systems with encrypted mesh networking to validate technology and build expertise
3. **Deployment**: Scale to additional sites and applications, integrating with existing systems
4. **Training**: Develop workforce skills in LOWL programming, embedded systems, encryption, and mesh networking
5. **Integration**: Connect LOWL-based systems with regional economic development initiatives, supporting the region's transformation
